The domineering person (*jabbār*) is ruined by the misuse of strength — arrogance, pride, the will to dominate. The wretched person (*shaqiyy*) is ruined by the misuse of weakness — despair, misery, a life lived under the sign of perdition. Jesus was neither. He was gentle without being weak, and he was blessed without being proud. His strength expressed itself as dutifulness and service rather than domination, and his difficult circumstances did not sink him into wretchedness. He walked between the two ruinous extremes, the balanced servant of God. The tradition records that he lived humbly and simply, and the verse teaches that a man can endure the most unusual and difficult of circumstances and remain neither arrogant nor wretched — provided his life is rooted, as Jesus' was, in servanthood, prayer, charity, and devotion to his mother.

**Jesus the Model: The Balance of the Blessed Life**

Taken together, verses 30–32 present a complete and balanced portrait of the Qur'anic Jesus. He is a servant of God who received the Book and prophethood (30). He is blessed wherever he goes, commanded to pray and give alms for his whole life (31). And he is dutiful toward his mother, neither domineering nor wretched (32). The portrait is at once lofty and human — a man of extraordinary spiritual gifts whose practical life was one of worship, charity, and filial devotion. It is also, not incidentally, a pointed corrective. Against the Christians who would later elevate Jesus to divinity, the sūrah has him begin by declaring his servanthood; against any who would reduce him, it affirms his prophethood and his blessed mission; and for the whole of humanity it holds him up as a model of the balanced, blessed life — the life that combines worship of God, care for others, and devotion to one's mother, free alike from the arrogance of the strong and the misery of the broken.

---
## Sūrah Maryam 19:33

> **Peace be upon me the day I was born, the day I die, and the day I am raised alive!"**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Triple Peace Spoken in the First Person**

Jesus' speech from the cradle concludes with a benediction that echoes the one pronounced upon John in verse 15, but with a crucial difference in voice: *wa-al-salāmu ʿalayya yawma wulidtu wa-yawma amūtu wa-yawma ubʿathu ḥayyan* — "And peace be upon me the day I was born, the day I die, and the day I am raised alive!" In verse 15, the blessing upon John was spoken in the third person by the divine voice or the narrator: "Peace be upon him." Here Jesus pronounces the same triple peace upon *himself*, in the first person: "Peace be upon *me*." The blessing spans the three great thresholds of his existence — birth, death, and resurrection — and the commentators explain each in turn. The peace upon the day of his birth refers to his protection from the touch of Satan, from which he was spared because of his purity (Ṭ). The peace upon the day of his death — which Muslims believe will occur after his return to earth near the end of time (43:61) — refers to his freedom from fear and punishment in the grave. And the peace upon the day he is raised alive refers to his tranquility at the Resurrection, when he will rise without the terror and dread that overcome others (Ṭ).

The shift to the first person is significant. Jesus is not merely the object of a blessing pronounced by another; he is the one who declares his own peace. Speaking from the cradle, with all the authority of his miraculous speech, he announces the security that God has ordained for him across the whole span of his existence. The declaration is a form of testimony: Jesus bears witness to his own protected destiny. It is also, in the context of the scene, a completion of his vindication of Mary. The infant who has already declared his servanthood, his blessedness, his prayer and charity and devotion to his mother, now seals his speech with a benediction of peace across the thresholds of life — a benediction that confirms the divine favor resting upon him and, through him, upon the mother who bore him.

**The Echo of John: Two Servants, One Peace**

The deliberate parallelism between verse 15 (John) and verse 33 (Jesus) is one of the sūrah's most elegant structural features. Both miraculous births are sealed with the identical triple blessing of peace across birth, death, and resurrection. The repetition binds the two prophets together and emphasizes that they are, in the Qur'anic vision, twin signs of the same divine power — the power that gave a son to the aged and barren Elizabeth and a son to the young virgin Mary. And the echo draws the reader's eye to what is common between them: both John and Jesus, however extraordinary their births, are servants of God protected by His peace from the moment of birth through the resurrection. Neither is divine; both are blessed. The parallelism is the Qur'an's way of insisting that the miraculous birth of Jesus does not set him apart from the company of the prophets in kind, but rather places him within that company as a supreme instance of God's creative mercy.

**The Peace That Spans Every Threshold**

For the believer, the triple peace of Jesus (and of John before him) is a promise and a pattern. The three thresholds of birth, death, and resurrection are the points at which every human being is most helpless — entering the world as a dependent infant, leaving it at the mercy of what follows, and rising from the grave into an unknown judgment. Jesus and John are shown to be at peace at all three. The source of that peace is not any immunity conferred by miraculous birth but the orientation of a life lived in servanthood to God — the very orientation that verses 30–32 have just described. The believer who lives as Jesus is described to have lived — as a servant of God, constant in prayer and charity, dutiful toward those to whom duty is owed, neither arrogant nor despairing — is promised the same peace that spans every threshold. The one who lives in peace with God is at peace in the cradle of his birth, at peace at the hour of his death, and at peace when he is raised alive. The benediction that Jesus pronounces upon himself is, in its principle, the benediction offered to every servant of God.

---
## Sūrah Maryam 19:34

> **That is Jesus son of Mary—a statement of the truth, which they doubt.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**"That Is Jesus Son of Mary": The Qur'an's Identification**

With the infant's speech concluded, the divine voice or the narrator turns to the reader and delivers the sūrah's assessment: *dhālika ʿĪsā ibnu Maryam* — "That is Jesus, son of Mary." The demonstrative "that" (*dhālika*) points back to the figure just described — the servant who spoke from the cradle, the prophet, the dutiful son, the one blessed across the thresholds of life. And the identification is given in the most precise terms: *ʿĪsā ibnu Maryam*, "Jesus son of Mary." The Qur'an's consistent identification of Jesus by his mother rather than by a father is itself significant; it reflects the Qur'anic conviction that Jesus had no human father and is therefore properly known as "son of Mary." The phrase *ibn Maryam* recurs throughout the Qur'an as Jesus' title, preserving always the memory of his virgin birth and his human, maternal lineage. He is a man born of a woman — fully human, however miraculous his origin — and the sūrah identifies him as such before proceeding to the theological assessment.

The verse then delivers that assessment with lapidary economy: *qawla al-ḥaqq* — "a statement of the truth" — *alladhī fīhi yamtarūn* — "concerning which they doubt." The commentators debate the precise referent of "statement of the truth" (*qawl al-ḥaqq*). Most understand it as a description of the account of Jesus just given — that this Qur'anic narrative of Jesus is the true statement, the accurate account of his life and person, in contrast to the accounts of the Jews, who rejected his prophethood, and of the Christians, who elevated him to divinity (Q, Ṭ). On this reading, the Qur'an presents its own story of Jesus as *the* true account, correcting the distortions that have grown up on either side. Some commentators instead understand "a statement of the truth" as describing the words Jesus himself uttered in verses 30–33 — that the infant's declaration of servanthood, prophethood, and peace was itself a true statement (Q). Others connect the phrase to Jesus' description elsewhere as "a Word from God" (3:45), noting that since *al-Ḥaqq* ("the Truth") is one of God's Names, the phrase may carry an echo of that title (Th) — though the sūrah's emphasis remains on the truth of the account rather than on any metaphysical claim.

**"Which They Doubt": The Two Communities in Error**

The verse closes by noting the doubt that surrounds Jesus: *alladhī fīhi yamtarūn* — "concerning which they doubt." The pronoun "they" is understood by the commentators to refer primarily to the Jews and the Christians (Ṭ, Th), the two communities who, for opposite reasons, have failed to accept the true account of Jesus. The Jews doubted or denied his prophethood, refusing to accept that he was a messenger of God. The Christians, in the Qur'anic view, erred in the opposite direction, elevating him to a divinity he himself had disavowed in the cradle. Both, in their different ways, are "in doubt" concerning the true nature of Jesus — the one by denying his mission, the other by exaggerating his person. The Qur'an stands between them with its "statement of the truth": Jesus is a servant of God and a prophet, neither a false claimant (as the Jews held) nor a divine being (as the Christians came to hold), but the miraculous son of Mary, God's servant and messenger.

**The Truth That Stands Between Two Errors**

The verse's framing of Jesus — as the truth about whom two communities are in doubt — is a recurring pattern in the Qur'an's treatment of religious controversy. The Qur'an repeatedly presents itself as the arbiter that restores balance where human communities have fallen into exaggeration or denial. Concerning Jesus, the two errors are symmetrical: the Jews denied his rank, the Christians exceeded it. The Qur'anic Jesus is presented as the correction of both — a man of the highest spiritual rank who is nevertheless, by his own first words, a servant of God. The sūrah's portrait is designed to be unassailable from either direction: the one who would deny Jesus' greatness is answered by the miracles of his virgin birth and his speech in the cradle; the one who would deify him is answered by his own confession of servanthood. For the believer, the verse is a reminder that the truth about sacred figures is often a precise balance that human communities are prone to disturb — and that the Qur'an's function is to restore that balance, presenting each prophet in his true rank, neither diminished nor deified. Jesus son of Mary, the servant and prophet, is the truth about which they doubt; and the sūrah has just spoken that truth from the cradle.

---
## Sūrah Maryam 19:35

> **It is not for God to take a child. Glory be to Him! When He decrees a thing, He only says to it, "Be!" and it is.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Rejection of Divine Parenthood**

Having corrected the errors about Jesus, the sūrah now moves to its most fundamental theological assertion: *mā kāna lillāhi an yattakhidha min waladin* — "It is not for God to take a child." The commentators explain that the phrase "it is not for God" (*mā kāna lillāh*) expresses what is impossible and inconceivable for God — what is incompatible with His nature (R). To attribute a child to God is not merely a mistaken belief but an impossibility, an offense against the very concept of divinity. The Qur'an rejects the notion that God could have a child in numerous places (2:116; 4:171; 6:100–102; 10:68; 16:57; 21:26; 39:4), always with the same urgency. The rejection is directed in the Qur'anic context against the pagan Arabs, who claimed the angels were God's daughters, and, by extension, engages the Christian claim that Jesus is the son of God. Both attributions, the sūrah insists, are impossible — not because God is unwilling but because the very idea is incoherent with His nature as the One, the Self-Sufficient, the incomparable.

The verse immediately follows the declaration with the exclamation of transcendence: *subḥānahu* — "Glory be to Him!" The *tasbīḥ* — the declaration that God is far above every deficiency — is the Qur'an's standard accompaniment to its refutations of divine parenthood (see commentary on 112:3: "He begets not, nor was He begotten"). To say "Glory be to Him" after denying that God has a child is to affirm that God is *munazzah*, absolutely transcending every attribute unworthy of Him — including, supremely, the attributes of need, dependence, and generation that fatherhood implies. God does not need a child, for He needs nothing; God is not subject to the processes of reproduction, for He is the Creator of all such processes. "Glory be to Him" clears the divine nature of every creaturely limitation, and in particular of the limitation of parenthood as human beings know it.

**"Be!" and It Is: The Creative Fiat That Makes Parenthood Unnecessary**

The verse then gives the positive ground of the rejection, and it is the sūrah's central theological argument in a nutshell: *idhā qaḍā amran fa-innamā yaqūlu lahu kun fa-yakūn* — "When He decrees a thing, He only says to it, 'Be!' and it is." The reasoning is unanswerable. God does not need a son to continue His existence, to inherit His nature, to extend His being, or to accomplish His will — because He creates by the simple word of command. When God wills a thing, He says "Be!" (*kun*) and it comes to be (cf. 2:117; 3:47, 59; 6:73; 16:40; 36:82; 40:68). No intermediary, no offspring, no partner is needed. The very existence of Jesus, which the Christians cite as evidence of a special divine sonship, is in fact evidence of the opposite: Jesus came into being by the divine fiat, exactly as did Adam and John and everything else. He is not God's son; he is one of the countless things to which God said "Be!" — a created servant, brought into existence by the same effortless command that creates all things.

**The Logic That Demolishes Divine Sonship**

The theological logic of this verse is profound and worth making explicit. Human beings beget children because they are mortal and wish to continue, or because they are incomplete and wish to extend themselves, or because generation is simply the natural process of their kind. Every reason a human being has a child is a reason rooted in need, limitation, or process — none of which applies to God. God is eternal, so He has no need to continue His being in descendants. God is complete, so He has no need to extend Himself. God is the Creator of all processes, so He is not subject to the process of generation. The very concept of God "taking a child" therefore collapses under its own weight: it imports into the divine nature the neediness and processuality that are the marks of creatures. The sūrah's alternative is clean and liberating: God does not need to beget, because God creates. And the creation of Jesus — the very miracle that prompted the attribution of sonship — is itself the demonstration that God needs no son, for He made Jesus with a word, as He makes all things. "Be!" and it is: this is the whole theology of creation, and it is the definitive answer to every attempt to introduce a child into the divine nature.

---
## Sūrah Maryam 19:36

> **"Truly God is my Lord and your Lord; so worship Him. This is a straight path."**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Continuation of Jesus' Speech**

The verse returns to the words of Jesus, continuing the speech that began in verse 30: *wa-inna Allāha rabbī wa-rabbukum fa-ʿbudūhu* — "And truly God is my Lord and your Lord; so worship Him." The commentators note that this may be read either as a continuation of Jesus' words from the cradle (Ṭ, Ṭs) or as the divine voice adding the conclusion to the account. Read as Jesus' speech, the verse completes his self-revelation. He has already declared that he is a servant of God (30), blessed and commanded to pray and give alms (31), dutiful toward his mother (32), and at peace across the thresholds of life (33). Now he adds the explicit summons: God is *my* Lord and *your* Lord — the Lord of Jesus no less than the Lord of those he addresses. There is no higher Lord for Jesus than the God who created him; and since God is the Lord of all alike, the worship that is due to Him is due from all alike. Jesus' own relationship to God — that of creature to Creator, servant to Lord — is exactly the relationship he calls others to share.

The first sentence is nearly identical to words the Qur'an elsewhere attributes to Jesus: in 5:117 Jesus is recorded as having said to God, "I said not to them except what Thou didst command me: worship God, my Lord and your Lord." The repetition across sūrahs underscores the constancy of Jesus' message: from the cradle to the end of his earthly mission, Jesus called people to the worship of the one God, whom he himself worshipped as his Lord. This is the Qur'an's portrait of a consistent Jesus — a prophet whose whole message was the summons to worship God alone, a message that stands in direct continuity with the message of every prophet and, ultimately, with the message of Islam itself.

**"So Worship Him": The Imperative That Answers Every Exaggeration**

The command *fa-ʿbudūhu* — "so worship Him" — is the practical conclusion of Jesus' self-revelation. If Jesus is a servant of God and God is his Lord, then the worship that belongs to God alone cannot be directed to Jesus. Those who would worship Jesus are redirected to the God whom Jesus himself worships. The verse is thus a quiet but decisive answer to the later Christian devotion to Jesus: Jesus himself, in the Qur'anic account, commanded that he not be worshipped, and commanded instead that God be worshipped. The servant who calls others to worship his own Lord cannot himself be the object of that worship. The logic is simple and unbreakable: Jesus says "God is my Lord"; therefore Jesus is not God; therefore the worship due to God is not due to Jesus; therefore "worship Him [God]." The sūrah places this self-disqualifying declaration in the mouth of Jesus himself, so that any who would worship him must contradict his own teaching.

**"This Is a Straight Path"**

The verse concludes with Jesus' seal upon his message: *hādhā ṣirāṭun mustaqīm* — "this is a straight path." The "straight path" (*al-ṣirāṭ al-mustaqīm*) is the phrase made famous by the Fātiḥah (1:6), where the believer prays "Guide us upon the straight path." Here Jesus identifies his own message — the worship of the one God whom he serves — as that straight path. The path is "straight" because it does not deviate to either side: it neither denies God's messengers (as the Jews did regarding Jesus) nor elevates them to divinity (as some Christians did), but keeps to the middle way of worshipping God alone through His servants and messengers. Jesus' declaration from the cradle — that God is his Lord and theirs, and that He alone is to be worshipped — is presented as the very definition of the straight path that every believer prays to be guided upon. The infant's words thus reach across the centuries to answer the prayer of the Fātiḥah: here is the straight path, the path of worshipping the one God, the path that Jesus himself walked and taught and sealed with his benediction of peace.

---
## Sūrah Maryam 19:37

> **Yet the parties differed among themselves, and woe unto those who disbelieve for the witnessing of a tremendous day!**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Parties That Differed Over Jesus**

After presenting the true account of Jesus and his call to worship God alone, the sūrah observes the historical reality with which that message was met: *fa-ikhtalafa al-aḥzābu min baynihim* — "Yet the parties (al-aḥzāb) differed among themselves." The commentators offer two readings of who "the parties" were. Some say they were the Jews and the Christians, who differed with each other over the status of Jesus (Q, Z) — the Jews rejecting his prophethood, the Christians asserting his divinity, each community divided from the other and, indeed, divided within itself. Others identify the parties more specifically as the different Christian sects — such as the Jacobites and the Nestorians (Q, Ṭ, Ṭs, Z) — the rival Christian groups of the Near East that disagreed over the nature of Christ, one emphasizing his divinity, another his humanity, in ways that the Qur'an regarded as so many wanderings from the straight path. Whichever reading one adopts, the point is the same: the message that Jesus delivered from the cradle — worship God alone as Lord — became, among those who came after him, the occasion of division, as communities argued over his nature instead of obeying his call.

The verse's observation is quietly damning. Jesus had declared the straight path with perfect clarity: "God is my Lord and your Lord; so worship Him." Yet those who claimed to follow him "differed among themselves," splitting into parties over precisely the questions the straight path had answered. The divisions were not over how to worship God but over the status of the very messenger who had called them to that worship — each party, in effect, losing the simplicity of Jesus' message in the complexity of its own speculations. The Qur'an repeatedly laments the division of religious communities over matters on which they were given clear guidance (cf. 2:213; 3:19; 42:14), and the differing parties over Jesus are presented as a prime instance: the clarity of the straight path was shattered by the discord of those who claimed to walk it.

**"Woe Unto Those Who Disbelieve"**

The verse then turns from the historical division to its eschatological consequence: *fa-waylun lilladhīna kafarū min mashhadi yawmin ʿaẓīm* — "and woe (wayl) unto those who disbelieve for the witnessing (mashhad) of a tremendous day." The word *wayl* is an exclamation of woe and destruction, and the commentators explain that it is directed against those whose disbelief — whether in Jesus' prophethood (the Jews) or in the pure worship of God alone (those Christians who elevated Jesus to a status he himself disavowed, and the pagans generally) — has placed them on the wrong side of the divine truth. The "witnessing of a tremendous day" refers to the Day of Resurrection, and the commentators gloss *mashhad* as the time and place of the reckoning and judgment (Z). "Witnessing" may refer to the great fear that will overwhelm those present at that scene, or to the testimony that will be borne against the disbelievers on that Day — by the angels, the prophets, the leaders of their communities, and even their own limbs (cf. 24:24; 36:65; 41:20). The tremendous Day will be one of witnessing in the fullest and most terrifying sense: every deed witnessed, every truth testified, every error exposed.

**The Warning Beneath the Woe**

The verse's warning is not directed at the past alone; it is a warning to the present and the future. The "parties" who divided over Jesus are a pattern that has repeated throughout religious history — and the Qur'an, by recording the division and its consequence, warns its own hearers against repeating it. The lesson is double-edged. First, the straight path that Jesus taught — the worship of God alone — must not be abandoned for divisive speculation about the status of messengers; the clarity of worshipping God alone is the safeguard against the fragmentation that followed Jesus. Second, the "tremendous day" is real, and those who disbelieve will witness it to their loss. The sūrah, which has spent its opening verses demonstrating God's mercy and power through the accounts of the prophets, now sounds the note of warning that runs through its later portions: the mercy is real, but so is the Day, and the choice of how to respond to the truth of God — in worship, in belief, in the avoidance of divisive error — is a choice with eternal stakes. The woe is pronounced so that hearers may be warned in time, and turn to the straight path while there is yet opportunity.

---
## Sūrah Maryam 19:38

> **How well they will hear and how well they will see on the Day they come unto Us. But the wrongdoers, today, are in manifest error.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**Keen Hearing and Sight on the Day of Return**

The verse contrasts the spiritual dullness of the wrongdoers in this life with the terrifying acuity of their perception on the Last Day: *asmiʿ bihim wa-abṣir yawma yaʾtūnanā* — "How well they will hear and how well they will see on the Day they come unto Us." The Arabic construction (*asmiʿ bihim*, *abṣir*) is an exclamation of wonder or astonishment: "How extraordinary is their hearing! How extraordinary their sight!" The commentators explain that on the Day of Resurrection, the disbelievers who were spiritually "blind" and "deaf" in this life — who refused to hear the truth and see the signs of God — will hear and see with piercing clarity (Ṭs). The Qur'an often describes those who reject guidance as having ears that do not hear and eyes that do not see the truth (2:18, 171; 5:71; 17:97). On the Last Day, that dullness will be stripped away, and they will see and hear the realities they denied — the truth of resurrection, the reality of judgment, the folly of their rejection. The verse holds up this reversal with a kind of awful wonder: the faculties that were sealed in this life will be opened on the Day of Return, but opened too late to be of any benefit.

The parallel is drawn explicitly elsewhere: "You were indeed heedless of this. Now We have removed from you your cover, so today your sight is piercing" (50:22). On that Day the veil of heedlessness is lifted, and the disbeliever sees with a clarity that only magnifies his regret, for he sees the truth he rejected while powerless to change his fate. Even if their hearing and sight become keen on that Day, it will avail them nothing — just as believing at the sight of punishment avails no one who did not believe in this life (cf. 40:84–85; Ṭ, Ṭs). The opening of the faculties on the Day of Judgment is not a second chance but a final confrontation with the reality that was always before them.

**"But the Wrongdoers, Today, Are in Manifest Error"**

The verse then draws the contrast back to the present: *wa-lākin al-ẓālimūna al-yawma fī ḍalālin mubīn* — "But the wrongdoers, today, are in manifest error." The word "today" (*al-yawm*) refers to the present life, the day of this world, in contrast to the Day they will come unto God. The wrongdoers (*al-ẓālimūn*) — those who wronged themselves by rejecting the truth and wronged others by leading them astray — are, in this present life, in "manifest error" (*ḍalāl mubīn*), error that is clear and evident. The point is that their condition is not hidden: their error is manifest to anyone with eyes to see, even if they themselves cannot perceive it. The irony is complete: in this life, they think they see clearly (the idolaters of Makkah were confident in their wealth and their ways), yet they are in manifest error; on the Last Day, when their eyes are truly opened, they will see the reality of their error, but it will be too late.

**The Tragedy of Delayed Sight**

The verse's teaching is a profound meditation on the nature of spiritual blindness and its cure. The Qur'an insists, throughout, that human beings possess the faculties to perceive the truth — eyes and ears and hearts — but that these faculties can be dulled by heedlessness, pride, and the love of the world. The tragedy of the wrongdoer is not that he lacks the capacity to see but that he refuses to use it, so that his sight is deferred to a Day when it can only condemn him. The verse is thus a summons to use one's faculties now, while they can still guide: to hear the truth while hearing can lead to belief, to see the signs while sight can lead to guidance. The believer is invited to cultivate, in this life, the very keenness of perception that the wrongdoers will only achieve on the Day of Return — to hear God's signs and see His handiwork with the eye of the heart, so that when the veil is finally lifted, it will reveal not a truth rejected but a truth embraced.

---
## Sūrah Maryam 19:39

> **And warn them of the Day of Regret, when the matter will have been decreed, while they are in a state of heedlessness and believe not.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Day of Regret: A Name Found Only Here**

The verse instructs the Prophet ﷺ to warn his people of a Day with a name unique to this verse in the entire Qur'an: *wa-andhirhum yawma al-ḥasrah* — "And warn them of the Day of Regret (al-ḥasrah)." The word *ḥasrah* denotes intense regret, remorse, and grief — the anguish of one who realizes, too late, that he has lost what he might have gained. Although the name "Day of Regret" appears only here, the theme of regret in the Hereafter runs throughout the Qur'an (cf. 2:167; 8:36). The commentators explain the nature of this regret: on the Day of Judgment, those whose disbelief and evil deeds have earned them Hellfire will be shown the dwellings in the Garden that had been prepared for them had they believed, and the sight of what they lost will be a source of the most piercing regret (Ṭ). The regret is the realization of lost opportunity — the knowledge that the good was attainable, that the path was clear, and that one turned away from it.

The verse situates this Day of Regret at a precise moment: *idh quḍiya al-amru* — "when the matter will have been decreed." The commentators explain that similar phrases are used to indicate the coming of the Last Day and Judgment (2:210; 6:8; 14:22). The "matter is decreed" at the point when final judgment is made — when the inhabitants of the Garden and the Fire are assigned their eternal abodes and the outcome is settled forever. The phrase signals finality: after the Last Day, there is no further opportunity for repentance, no change of course, no appeal. The regret of the Day of Regret is all the more acute because it comes when the matter has been finally decided — when the doors of mercy and amendment are closed and the verdict is sealed.

**"In a State of Heedlessness and Believe Not"**

The verse explains why the warning is necessary: the people are *fī ghaflatin hum wa-lā yuʾminūn* — "in a state of heedlessness, and they believe not." The *ghaflah* (heedlessness, negligence, being unaware) is the spiritual condition of those who go through life without attending to the realities of God, death, and judgment. They are preoccupied with the present, with their wealth and their affairs, and they do not turn their minds to the Day that is coming. Compounding the heedlessness is their refusal to believe: *wa-lā yuʾminūn*, "and they do not believe." The combination is what makes the warning urgent. Those who are merely heedless might be roused by a reminder; those who couple heedlessness with active disbelief have placed themselves, for now, beyond the reach of ordinary admonition. Yet the verse commands the Prophet to warn them nonetheless — because the warning itself, even if rejected by some, is the means by which others may be awakened, and because the warning fulfills the messenger's duty regardless of the response.

**The Warning That Opens the Door Before It Closes**

There is a profound pastoral wisdom in this verse. The Qur'an does not despair of those who are heedless and unbelieving; it warns them, and the warning itself is an act of mercy. The Day of Regret will be a day of lost opportunity for those who never heeded the warning; but the very existence of the warning, in this life, is the opportunity to avoid that regret. Every human being who hears of the Day of Regret, and is moved by the warning to turn to God before "the matter is decreed," has been granted the very mercy that the heedless squander. The verse is thus a summons to wakefulness: do not live in heedlessness of the Day that is certain to come; do not defer belief until belief is useless. The Day of Regret is real, but it is not yet here; while the matter is still open, while repentance is still possible, the warning invites every hearer to turn, so that on the Day of Regret they may be among those who have nothing to regret. The sūrah, which has proclaimed God's mercy through the prophets, here adds the urgency that mercy demands: the mercy is offered now, and the warning is the bell that calls the heedless to accept it before the Day when the matter is decreed.

---
## Sūrah Maryam 19:40

> **Surely We shall inherit the earth and whatsoever is upon it, and unto Us shall they be returned.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**God as the Inheritor of All Things**

The verse turns to the ultimate end of all earthly possession and power: *innā naḥnu narithu al-arḍa wa-man ʿalayhā* — "Surely We shall inherit the earth and whosoever is upon it." One of the Qur'anic Names of God is *al-Wārith*, "the Inheritor" (15:23; 28:58; 21:89). The name expresses a profound truth about the divine relationship to creation: everything belongs to God, and everything ultimately returns to Him. The metaphor of inheritance is striking because it inverts the ordinary human understanding. In human affairs, an heir inherits from someone who has died — the deceased's possessions pass to the living. But God is the Inheritor in the sense that He alone endures while all that He has created passes away. The "inheritance" is God's because He is the only one who remains: when every earthly owner has died, when every possessor has been dispossessed by death, God remains, and all that seemed to belong to others returns to its true Owner. The verse expresses this in the language of 55:26–27: "All upon it passes away. And there remains the Face of thy Lord, Possessed of Majesty and Bounty," and of 57:5: "Unto Him belongs sovereignty over the heavens and the earth, and unto God are all matters returned."

The phrase "the earth and whatsoever is upon it" (*al-arḍ wa-man ʿalayhā* — literally, "and whosoever is upon it") is significant. God inherits not only the earth and its possessions but the very people upon it — the human beings who thought they owned their lives, their wealth, their accomplishments. The wealth that the idolaters of Makkah boasted of, the houses they took pride in, the station and company they vaunted (verse 73) — all of it will be inherited by God, and its former owners will be stripped of it. The verse is a leveling reminder addressed to every age: no human being is a permanent owner of anything; all are temporary stewards of what belongs ultimately to God.

**"Unto Us Shall They Be Returned"**

The verse concludes with the reason for God's inheritance and the ground of all reckoning: *wa-ilaynā yurjaʿūn* — "and unto Us shall they be returned." Human beings do not simply cease to exist; they are returned to their Creator. The earth is inherited by God, and those who dwelt upon it are gathered back to Him for judgment. The return is both the completion of the divine inheritance and the occasion of accountability. Every human being who lived upon the earth — the prophets and the tyrants, the believers and the disbelievers, the wealthy and the poor — will be returned to God, stripped of all that the world lent them, to give an account. The verse thus stands as a summary of the eschatological teaching of the whole sūrah: the world passes away, God endures, and every soul returns to Him.

**The Perspective That Corrects Earthly Pride**

There is a powerful practical application in this verse for the modern reader. Human beings are prone to a deep illusion of permanent ownership — the belief that what we have acquired, we possess; that what we have built, we control; that our wealth, our positions, our very bodies are finally our own. The verse dismantles that illusion at its root. Nothing is finally ours; we are stewards, not owners, of the earth and of all upon it, and in the end God inherits all and we are returned to Him. This perspective is not morbid but liberating. If everything belongs to God and will return to Him, then the frantic accumulation of this world is a chasing after what will be taken away, and the wise course is to use what we have been given as faithful stewards — to invest it in what will survive the inheritance: faith, righteousness, love of God, and care for others. The verse that announces God's inheritance of the earth is thus a summons to live with the end in view, to hold the things of this world lightly, and to remember that the true Owner of all is God, to Whom every soul shall be returned.

---
## Sūrah Maryam 19:41

> **And remember Abraham in the Book—verily he was truthful, a prophet—**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Command to Remember Abraham**

With verse 41 the sūrah opens a new gallery in its portrait of God's servants. Having recounted the intertwined stories of Zachariah, John, Mary, and Jesus, it now turns to a succession of the great patriarchs and prophets, beginning with Abraham: *wa-dhkur fī al-Kitābi Ibrāhīma* — "And remember Abraham in the Book." The command uses the same verb *dhkur* ("remember") that introduced Mary in verse 16 and that opened the sūrah in verse 2. The repetition is a structural signal: each of the sūrah's prophetic portraits is presented as an act of remembrance, a *dhikr* that keeps the deeds of God's servants alive for the believing community. The command is addressed to the Prophet ﷺ, who is to recite and preserve Abraham's account in the Book; and through him it reaches every reader. Abraham is to be remembered — his faith, his courage, his confrontation with idolatry, and his reward — because his example carries lessons that are as pertinent to the Prophet's community in Makkah as they were to Abraham in his own time.

Abraham is then introduced with a double title of the highest praise: *innahu kāna ṣiddīqan nabiyyan* — "verily he was truthful (ṣiddīq), a prophet." The word *ṣiddīq* is a weighty term, derived from the root *ṣ-d-q* (truth, sincerity), and it denotes one who is truthful and sincere in an exceptional degree — one whose whole being is given over to confirming and embodying the truth. The Qur'an uses this title sparingly and for figures of the highest rank: Joseph (12:46), Idrīs (19:56), and Mary (5:75) are among those called *ṣiddīq*, and the plural *ṣiddīqūn* designates a class of the blessed ranked with the prophets, the witnesses, and the righteous (4:69). Al-Rāzī explains that the *ṣiddīq* is one who is honest by nature and habit, and one who constantly confirms the truth. Applied to Abraham, the title indicates that he was truthful in speech, in promises, and in deeds (Ṭ) — a man whose entire life was a confirmation of the truth he proclaimed.

**The Titles of Prophethood: Nabī and Rasūl**

The second title, *nabiyy* ("prophet"), invites a brief reflection on the Qur'an's vocabulary of prophethood, for the sūrah uses it repeatedly in its portraits. Islamic tradition distinguishes between a *nabī* (prophet), one who receives a message from God to deliver to his people — whether of glad tidings or warning or both — and a *rasūl* (messenger), one who brings a new revelation or religious law for his people. Every messenger is a prophet, but not every prophet is a messenger. In the Qur'an itself the two terms overlap more loosely, but the tradition refined them. Abraham holds the highest rank in this schema: he is considered both a messenger and a prophet in Islamic tradition, though the Qur'an explicitly calls him *nabī* here and only implies his status as *rasūl* through the accounts of the scriptures and revelation granted to him (4:163–65; 53:36–37; 87:19). The double title *ṣiddīq nabiyy* — truthful prophet — places Abraham at the summit of the human figures the sūrah holds up for remembrance.

**Why Abraham Matters to This Sūrah**

The placement of Abraham in Sūrah Maryam is not arbitrary. The sūrah has been establishing, through Zachariah, John, Mary, and Jesus, the theme of God's gifts to His chosen servants — gifts granted after withdrawal, gifts that transcend human impossibility. Abraham's account continues this theme in a new register. His story is one of confrontation and migration: he argued with his idolatrous father, withdrew from his people, and was rewarded by God with Isaac and Jacob (verses 49–50) — the sūrah's pattern of divine gifts following separation. But Abraham's account also carries a pointed relevance for the Makkan audience. Abraham, like the Prophet Muhammad ﷺ, confronted a people steeped in the worship of idols and ancestral tradition, and he did so with argument and courage. The remembrance of Abraham is thus an encouragement to the Prophet and his community: the man who stood alone against the idolatry of his own father and people, and was vindicated and blessed by God, is a model for those who now stand against the idolatry of Makkah. The *ṣiddīq* who remembered God when all around him had forgotten Him is remembered by God in turn — and the verse's command to remember Abraham is part of that divine remembrance.

---
## Sūrah Maryam 19:42

> **when he said unto his father, "O my father! Why do you worship that which neither hears nor sees, nor can avail you in any way?"**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Confrontation with His Father**

The sūrah now gives the first of its Quranic accounts of Abraham's confrontation with idolatry, focused here on his father. *idh qāla li-abīhi yā abati* — "when he said unto his father, 'O my father!'" The address is striking for its tenderness even in the midst of confrontation. Abraham does not shout at his father, does not mock him, does not break the bond of filial respect. He addresses him repeatedly as "O my father" (*yā abati*) — a term of affectionate respect — throughout this passage (verses 42, 43, 44, 45). The name of Abraham's father is given elsewhere as Āzar (6:74); here he is simply "my father." The point of the repeated vocative, as al-Rāzī notes, is that Abraham's words are spoken out of love and earnest filial concern for his father's spiritual well-being, not out of disrespect or hostility (R). Even in challenging his father's religion, Abraham honors his father as a father.

The content of Abraham's question is a model of rational argumentation: *lima taʿbudu mā lā yasmaʿu wa-lā yubṣiru wa-lā yughnī ʿanka shayʾan* — "Why do you worship that which neither hears nor sees, nor can avail you in any way?" The argument is devastating in its simplicity. Abraham examines the objects of his father's worship — idols, false deities — and finds them wanting by three criteria. They cannot hear, so they cannot receive or respond to the supplications of their worshippers. They cannot see, so they cannot distinguish their worshippers from their enemies, nor observe the needs and deeds of those who call upon them. And they cannot avail — they can neither bring benefit nor avert harm (Ṭ; cf. 21:66; 26:73). An object that cannot hear, cannot see, and cannot help is, by any rational standard, unworthy of worship. Abraham's question reduces the whole edifice of idolatry to absurdity in a single stroke.

**The Logic of the Argument Against Idolatry**

Al-Rāzī elaborates the logical force of this argument at length. If the idols cannot see or hear, he reasons, then they cannot distinguish those who worship them from those who do not, and they cannot hear the supplications of their devotees. Moreover, to worship such objects is to worship something less capable than oneself — for the worshipper can see and hear, while the idol cannot. The argument presupposes a principle that the Qur'an returns to repeatedly: that worship must be directed to that which is greater, more knowing, more powerful than the worshipper, and ultimately to the One who hears, sees, and has power over all things. Al-Rāzī also notes a sophistication in the actual beliefs of Abraham's people: they likely did not consider the idols powerful in themselves but may have worshipped them as representatives of the stars, which they thought possessed divine power, or of great people whom they hoped would intercede with God on their behalf. Abraham's argument cuts through all such rationalizations: whatever the idol represents, the worship is in fact directed to that which can neither hear nor see nor avail, and such worship is logically bankrupt.

**The Relevance to Every False Worship**

The verse's argument against idolatry has a relevance that transcends the worship of carved idols. Human beings, in every age, "worship" things that cannot hear, see, or avail them: wealth that cannot hear their prayers, status that cannot see their need, passions that cannot save them in the hour of trial, false gods of tribe, nation, or ideology that promise much and deliver nothing. Abraham's question — "Why do you worship that which neither hears nor sees, nor can avail you in any way?" — is as piercing today as it was when first uttered to his father. It invites every person to examine the objects of their ultimate devotion and ask whether those objects can truly hear, see, and avail. The question is designed to lead the honest inquirer to the One who does hear, does see, and can avail — the God who alone is worthy of the worship that no deaf, blind, powerless thing can receive. Abraham's confrontation with his father is thus a standing summons to intellectual honesty about the true object of worship.

---
## Sūrah Maryam 19:43

> **O my father! Verily knowledge has come unto me that has not come unto you. So follow me, and I shall guide you upon a sound path.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**Knowledge That Has Come: The Claim of Prophethood**

Abraham's argument now advances from the negative critique of idolatry to a positive summons. He addresses his father again with the affectionate vocative: *yā abati innī qad jāʾanī mina al-ʿilmi mā lam yaʾtika* — "O my father! Truly knowledge has come to me that has not come to you." The "knowledge" (*al-ʿilm*) that Abraham claims is the knowledge of revelation and prophecy — the spiritual knowledge of the reality of God and the Hereafter that has been granted to him as a prophet (Q). The claim is remarkable in its modesty and its firmness. Abraham does not claim to be wiser than his father by his own effort or age; he claims that knowledge has *come to him* — it is a gift, an unveiling that has been granted to him and not to his father. The knowledge is not his own achievement but a divine bestowal. Yet the firmness is unmistakable: Abraham knows what his father does not know, and that difference in knowledge obliges him to speak.

The verse is one of the Qur'an's clearest inversions of the tribal principle that authority follows age and lineage. In a society where the wisdom of elders and ancestors was the supreme authority, a son stands before his father and says, in effect: knowledge has come to me that has not come to you; therefore you should follow me. The inversion is deliberate and radical. It makes the point that spiritual authority, unlike tribal authority, is not determined by age, lineage, or precedence, but by the reception of truth from God. A young man who has received divine knowledge outranks, in matters of truth, an elder who has not — and the elder is obliged to follow the younger when the younger speaks truth. This stands as an implicit answer to those who rejected the prophets on the grounds of deference to their "fathers" and ancestral tradition (cf. 2:170; 5:104). The idols of the fathers do not become true by virtue of their age.

**"So Follow Me": The Call to Guidance**

Abraham's summons is direct: *fa-ttabiʿnī ahdika ṣirāṭan sawiyyan* — "So follow me, and I shall guide you upon a sound (sawiyy) path." The word *sawiyy* denotes something level, straight, and sound — free of crookedness and defect. Abraham promises to guide his father upon a sound path, the path of correct religion by which salvation is attained (Q). The offer is made to his own father, the man who raised him and to whom he owes filial respect; and it is made in love, not in arrogance. Abraham is not lording his knowledge over his father but inviting his father to share in it, to be guided to the very good that Abraham himself has found. This is the spirit of true religious guidance: the one who has received the truth does not hoard it or despise those who lack it, but invites them, with patience and affection, to walk the sound path together.

The verse models the difficult task of counseling a loved one who is in error. Abraham's father was not a stranger but his own parent — the person to whom he owed the deepest filial duty. And yet Abraham could not remain silent before his father's idolatry; love itself compelled him to speak. The verse shows that true love for another person includes the willingness to challenge what harms them, even when the challenge is difficult and may be rejected. Abraham spoke to his father with respect (the repeated "O my father"), with honesty (knowledge has come to me that has not come to you), and with a genuine offer of help (follow me, and I shall guide you). He did not despise his father, did not abandon him prematurely, did not soften the truth. This is the balance that the Qur'an holds up: the truth spoken in love, the correction offered with respect, the guidance extended to the very one who may reject it — a model for every believer who must address error in those they love.

---
## Sūrah Maryam 19:44

> **O my father! Worship not Satan; surely Satan is disobedient toward the Compassionate.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**Idolatry as the Worship of Satan**

Abraham's argument now takes a deeper turn. He has shown that the idols cannot hear, see, or avail (verse 42). Now he names the reality behind idolatry: *yā abati lā taʿbudi al-Shayṭān* — "O my father! Worship not Satan." The commentators are careful to explain the sense of this. Abraham's people did not literally bow down to a being they called Satan; they worshipped idols. But the Qur'an and the commentators insist that obeying another being in disobedience to God is tantamount to worshipping that being (Q, Th). Since the worship of idols is an act of disobedience to God, and since Satan is the great instigator of disobedience — the one who "prompts" human beings to sin and who is described as their open enemy — to worship anything other than God is, in effect, to follow and obey Satan. Al-Rāzī and others note this principle, which the Qur'an also states in 9:31, where those who take their rabbis and monks as lords in disobedience to God are said to have been commanded to worship God alone.

The equation is sharp and uncomfortable. Abraham is telling his father that his idol-worship, whatever he imagines he is doing, is in reality service to Satan — because Satan is precisely the one who leads human beings away from the worship of God into the worship of false things. The verse thus exposes the hidden architecture of idolatry: behind every false object of worship stands the deceiver who engineered the deviation. The worshipper of idols may think he is merely following ancestral custom or reverencing symbols, but he is in fact, by the logic of disobedience, serving the enemy of his own soul.

**"Surely Satan Is Disobedient Toward the Compassionate"**

Abraham grounds his warning in the nature of Satan: *inna al-Shayṭāna kāna lil-Raḥmāni ʿaṣiyyan* — "surely Satan is disobedient (ʿaṣiyy) toward the Compassionate." The word *ʿaṣiyy* denotes one who is persistently, characteristically disobedient. Satan's defining trait, from the moment of his refusal to bow to Adam (2:34; 7:11; 15:31–32; 38:74), has been his disobedience to God — his rebellion against the divine command out of pride. The verse describes this disobedience with a pointed irony: Satan is disobedient *toward the Compassionate* (al-Raḥmān). The Name "the Compassionate," so central to this sūrah, is juxtaposed with Satan's rebellion to underscore the enormity of his ingratitude: he rebelled against the very One whose mercy sustains him and all creation. And the use of *al-Raḥmān* here ties the whole passage into the sūrah's dominant Name, reminding the reader that idolatry and the Satanic disobedience behind it are offenses against Mercy itself — a turning away from the compassionate God who created and sustains all things.

**The Logic of the Warning**

The logic of Abraham's warning to his father is cumulative and powerful. He has shown that the idols are deaf, blind, and powerless (verse 42). Now he reveals what actually lies behind the worship of such things: Satan, the disobedient one, who delights in turning creatures away from their Merciful Lord. If his father follows the idols, he is following Satan; and if he follows Satan, he is allying himself with one whose entire nature is defined by rebellion against the Compassionate. The conclusion is inescapable: to continue in idolatry is to serve the enemy of God and the enemy of one's own soul. The warning is delivered, as always, with the affectionate address "O my father" — Abraham is not gloating over his father's error but trying, with all the resources of argument and love, to pull him back from the brink. This is the pattern of genuine religious concern: to name the true nature of the error clearly, to reveal what lies behind it, and to do so out of love for the one being warned.

---
## Sūrah Maryam 19:45

> **O my father! Truly I fear that a punishment from the Compassionate will befall you, such that you will become a friend of Satan."**

**Expanded Commentary**

**Fear for the Father's End**

Abraham's appeal to his father reaches its most tender and urgent point: *yā abati innī akhāfu an yamassaka ʿadhābun mina al-Raḥmāni* — "O my father! Truly I fear that a punishment from the Compassionate will befall you." The dominant emotion here is not anger at the father's idolatry but fear for the father's soul. Abraham loves his father, and that love expresses itself as dread of the father's final fate. The phrase "a punishment from the Compassionate" (*ʿadhābun min al-Raḥmān*) is striking in its paradox: punishment proceeding from the Merciful. The commentators wrestle with this juxtaposition, and their resolution is instructive. The punishment is "from the Compassionate" in the sense that it is decreed and executed by the same God whose mercy governs all — the God whose justice is itself an aspect of His mercy, for a world in which evil went unpunished would be a world in which the innocent had no refuge. The juxtaposition of the Name with punishment also deepens the tragedy of the father's situation: he is turning away from Mercy itself, and the consequence of that turning is the very punishment Abraham fears.

The commentators differ on whether Abraham's "I fear" expresses mere apprehension or certain knowledge (Q, Ṭ, Th). Some read it literally as fear — Abraham hoping against hope that his father might yet turn, but dreading that persistence in idolatry would lead to ruin. Others read it as Abraham's certain knowledge that unrepented idolatry ends in punishment. Whichever reading one adopts, the verse conveys Abraham's profound concern: he does not want his father to suffer the fate that his father's choices are earning.

**"You Will Become a Friend of Satan"**

The verse closes with the specific fate Abraham fears for his father: *fa-takūna lil-Shayṭāni waliyyan* — "such that you will become a friend (walī) of Satan." The word *walī* denotes a close bond of allegiance, support, or friendship. The commentators explain that the state of being a *walī* (friend) of Satan stands in direct contrast to the state of being a friend of God (*walī Allāh*; 10:62–64), which designates those whose devotion to God has brought them near to Him and who may serve as channels of His grace. By analogy, those who persist in following Satan become his friends — channels of his influence and temptation in this world, and ultimately his companions in the Hereafter. When one becomes a friend of Satan, one is separated from and repudiated by God (R, Ṭ), and this is the fate Abraham fears will befall his father if he persists in idolatry.

Some commentators note that Abraham's fear may mean that his father will end as a companion of Satan in Hellfire (Q, R) — not that Satan will be his friend in a literal sense, for Satan is described throughout the Qur'an as the enemy of human beings (2:168; 6:142; 7:22), and in the Hereafter Satan will abandon his own followers (14:22). The "friendship" is thus a terrible irony: those who befriend Satan by following him will find, at the end, that their friend deserts them and becomes their accuser. Abraham's warning is a desperate effort to spare his father from this fate. And the whole passage — with its repeated "O my father" — reveals, as al-Rāzī stresses, that every argument Abraham makes is born of love and earnest concern for his father's spiritual welfare, not of disrespect or contempt (R). The son who fears for his father's soul is the truest son; and the verse holds up Abraham as the model of loving admonition, speaking the hardest truths in the gentlest voice, because he fears for the one he loves.

---
## Sūrah Maryam 19:46

> **He said, "Do you reject my gods, O Abraham? If you cease not, I shall surely stone you. Take leave of me for a long while!"**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Father's Rejection of His Son**

Abraham's loving admonition is met with rejection. His father responds with a mixture of incredulity and anger: *qāla arāghibun anta ʿan ālihatī yā Ibrāhīm* — "He said, 'Do you turn away from my gods, O Abraham?'" The father frames Abraham's monotheism as a turning away from his gods — the family's and community's deities, the object of ancestral devotion. The question is rhetorical and reproachful: the father is stunned that his own son should reject the religion of his fathers. In his father's eyes, Abraham is the apostate, the one who has broken faith with the gods of the ancestors. The roles are exactly reversed from Abraham's perspective: Abraham sees his father as lost in idolatry; his father sees Abraham as lost to the ancestral gods. The confrontation is a clash of two worlds, the world of inherited tradition and the world of revealed truth, and neither can initially understand the other.

The father's threat follows swiftly and with menace: *la-in lam tantahi la-arjumannaka* — "if you cease not, I shall surely stone you." The commentators debate the precise meaning of "stone you" (*arjumannaka*). Most understand it as a threat to curse Abraham or to "stone him with words" — to heap abuse and vilification upon him (Q, Ṭ, Th). Others allow the possibility of physical stoning, a real danger for one who publicly rejected the gods of his community (Q; cf. 26:116). Either way, the threat is severe: Abraham's father is warning him that his continued preaching of monotheism will bring down upon him the punishment of the community — whether verbal abuse and cursing or actual violence. The father who could not be won by his son's loving arguments now resorts to threats. The threat of stoning was the fate of many who challenged the ancestral religion — including, tragically, prophets who came after Abraham and faced the same opposition from their own people.

**"Take Leave of Me for a Long While!"**

The father's final word is expulsion: *wa-hjurnī maliyyan* — "and take leave of me for a long while!" The verb *hajara* conveys separation, abandonment, and avoidance. The father is telling Abraham to leave him alone, to go away, to sever the daily intimacy of their relationship for a long time. The commentators explain that the father may mean, "Leave me, so that you may be safe from my punishment!" (Q, Ṭ, Th) — an acknowledgment, perhaps, that his own anger or the anger of the community posed a danger to Abraham, and that separation was the safest course. Or the father may simply be dismissing his son in fury, unwilling to hear more. Whichever shade one adopts, the verse records the painful rupture: the son who sought to guide his father with love is now told to go, threatened with stoning, and banished from his presence.

**The Cost of Truth: When Guidance Is Rejected**

The verse holds up the sobering reality that the prophets — and all who call others to truth — do not always succeed, and often pay a heavy price for their faithfulness. Abraham did everything right: he spoke with respect, argued with reason, and warned with love. And yet his father rejected him, threatened him, and expelled him. The Qur'an records this failure without softening it, because it is a truth every caller to truth must face: not every seed planted will grow; not every loved one admonished will respond; not every faithful effort will bear visible fruit in this life. Abraham's example teaches that one must speak the truth with love and reason, and then accept, with patience and without abandoning one's own integrity, that the response is not in one's hands. The father's rejection did not make Abraham's message false, nor did it excuse Abraham from faithfulness. It simply meant that Abraham's path now lay elsewhere — as the next verses will show, in withdrawal and continued trust in God.

---
## Sūrah Maryam 19:47

> **He said, "Peace be upon you! I shall seek forgiveness for you from my Lord. Verily He has been gracious unto me."**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Response of Peace to a Threat of Violence**

Abraham's reply to his father's threats is one of the most striking acts of forbearance in the entire Qur'an: *qāla salāmun ʿalayka* — "He said, 'Peace be upon you!'" Confronted with rejection, threats of stoning, and expulsion, Abraham does not retaliate, does not curse, does not return anger for anger. He responds with peace. The commentators are careful to explain that this is not a greeting in the ordinary sense but an indication that Abraham is ending the confrontation and will not fight his father or seek revenge (Q, Ṭ). He is saying, in effect: you have nothing to fear from me; I will not harm you or contest you further by force. His father has threatened to stone him; Abraham answers with the assurance of peace.

The response is also consistent with a pattern the Qur'an establishes elsewhere. The greeting and stance of peace is the mark of the believers: the servants of the Compassionate "walk humbly upon the earth, and when the ignorant address them, say, 'Peace'" (25:63), and those who hear idle talk turn away saying "Peace be upon you" (28:55). Abraham, facing a father who has become hostile to the truth, embodies this response: he does not sink to his father's level of threat and abuse but maintains the dignity and serenity of one who trusts in God. The peace he offers is genuine — he will not be the one to escalate the conflict — but it is not the peace of compromise with falsehood; it is the peace of one who declines to fight and entrusts the outcome to God.

**"I Shall Seek Forgiveness for You from My Lord"**

Abraham adds a promise of remarkable magnanimity: *sa-astaghfiru laka rabbī* — "I shall seek forgiveness for you from my Lord." Even after his father's threats, Abraham promises to pray for his father's forgiveness. This is the instinct of a prophet whose heart is filled with mercy for his own — even the father who has just threatened him is not beyond his intercession. The commentators note the depth of this act: Abraham, who has been expelled and threatened, responds not with a curse but with a prayer for his father's pardon. The verse thus models the extraordinary moral height of refusing to let another's hostility extinguish one's own goodness. Abraham's love for his father was not conditional upon his father's love in return; it persisted even in the face of rejection and threat.

The commentators also note the qualification that would later be placed on this prayer. The Qur'an records in 9:114 that Abraham sought forgiveness for his father, "but when it became clear to him that he was an enemy of God, he repudiated him" — for Abraham's father died in his idolatry, and prayer for the forgiveness of one who died upon unbelief is not granted. Yet at this moment in the sūrah, when the father is still alive and the outcome unknown, Abraham's seeking of forgiveness is sincere and commendable. The verse teaches that one should pray for the guidance and forgiveness of one's loved ones as long as there is hope, and that such prayer is an act of mercy that befits the believer.

**"Verily He Has Been Gracious unto Me"**

Abraham grounds his hope in his own experience of divine grace: *innahu kāna bī ḥafiyyan* — "verily He has been gracious (ḥafiyy) unto me." The word *ḥafiyy* conveys kindness, graciousness, and care. Abraham's confidence that God will hear his prayer for his father rests upon his own knowledge of God's graciousness — the God who has been gracious to Abraham, who chose him, guided him, and revealed to him, will surely not turn away the prayer that Abraham offers for his father. This is the same logic of hope grounded in remembered faithfulness that Zachariah expressed in verse 4 ("in calling upon Thee I have never been wretched"). Abraham has experienced God's grace, and that experience underwrites his confidence in God's mercy toward others through his intercession.

**The Height of Forbearance**

The verse, taken as a whole, presents Abraham as a model of moral excellence in the face of hostility. When rejected by the very person he loved and admonished; when threatened with stoning by his own father; when expelled from his home — Abraham answers with peace, promises prayer, and grounds his hope in the grace of God. There is no bitterness in his reply, no resentment, no abandonment of the moral high ground. This is the Qur'an's portrait of prophetic character at its most elevated: the one who is wronged does not become a wrongdoer; the one who is threatened does not threaten in return; the one who is rejected continues to pray for the one who rejected him. And in doing so, Abraham shows that the final security of the believer lies not in the approval of family or community but in the grace of God — the One who has been gracious to His servant, and who is gracious to all who turn to Him.

---
## Sūrah Maryam 19:48

> **And I withdraw myself from you and that which you call upon apart from God. And I call upon my Lord; it may be that in calling upon my Lord, I will not be wretched."**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Withdrawal: Abraham Leaves His Father and His Gods**

Abraham now carries out the separation his father demanded, but on his own terms and for his own reasons: *wa-aʿtazilukum wa-mā tadʿūna min dūni Allāh* — "And I withdraw myself from you and that which you call upon apart from God." The verb *aʿtazilu* conveys a deliberate, principled withdrawal — a separating of oneself. Abraham withdraws from his father and, more importantly, from everything his father calls upon besides God — the idols and false deities that his people worship. The withdrawal is the response of a man who has done all he could through argument and admonition and has been rejected; having failed to turn his father and his people from idolatry, he now separates himself from them rather than remain complicit in their worship. This is the Qur'anic principle of *al-baraʾ*, the disassociation from falsehood and those who persist in it after the truth has been clearly presented — the same principle that underlies the verse "I am quit of that which you associate [with God]" (cf. 6:78; 60:4).

The commentators understand this withdrawal as Abraham's taking leave of his family to migrate to the Holy Land (Th). His separation is both spiritual and physical: spiritually, he cuts his ties with the idolatry of his people; physically, he leaves their company and their land. The withdrawal is not an abandonment of his duty but the fulfillment of it in a new mode — since he could not turn his people from falsehood by remaining among them, he removes himself to preserve his own faith and to seek, in a new land, the family and community that the idolaters could not provide. The verse thus marks the hinge of Abraham's life: the point at which faithfulness to God required separation from kin and country, and Abraham accepted the cost.

**"And I Call upon My Lord"**

Having renounced his father's gods, Abraham affirms his own devotion: *wa-adʿū rabbī* — "and I call upon my Lord." The verb *adʿū* is the same root used for supplication and invocation. Abraham's worship is not left empty by his rejection of the idols; it is filled by his calling upon the true Lord. The contrast is complete: his father's people call upon idols that cannot hear; Abraham calls upon his Lord, who hears. The withdrawal from falsehood is accompanied by a turn to truth — one does not merely leave the false gods but turns to the true God. This is the complete movement of faith: *lā ilāha* (no god) paired with *illā Allāh* (except God), the negation of false worship joined to the affirmation of true worship.

**"I Will Not Be Wretched in Calling upon My Lord"**

The verse closes with a phrase that echoes Zachariah's declaration in verse 4: *ʿasā allā akūna bi-duʿāʾi rabbī shaqiyyan* — "it may be that in calling upon my Lord, I will not be wretched." The word *shaqiyy* (wretched) is the same used by Zachariah and by Jesus (verse 32). The commentators explain that, as with Zachariah, the phrase means that Abraham trusts God will answer his prayers and not disappoint him (Ṭ). Abraham, now alone — separated from his father, his people, and his homeland — declares his confidence that in calling upon his Lord he will not be wretched, that his isolation will not be his ruin because he is not alone. He has lost his family, but he has his Lord; and the one who calls upon the Lord is never finally wretched.

In the context of this verse, Abraham is said to have called upon his Lord to grant him family and children who would strengthen him, now that he was alone and separated from his father and his people (Q). The answer to that prayer is recounted in the very next verse, where he is given Isaac and Jacob. This reading ties Abraham's account into the sūrah's larger theme: God's Compassion in bestowing blessed children upon His servants who have withdrawn from the world — John upon Zachariah, Jesus upon Mary, and now Isaac and Jacob upon Abraham. Abraham gave up his father's house for God; God gave him in return a house of prophets.

**The Freedom of Faithful Withdrawal**

The verse carries a profound lesson about the cost and the reward of faithfulness. Following God sometimes requires withdrawing from those we love and from the communities in which we were raised — when those communities persist in what we know to be false, and when our continued presence would compromise our faith. Abraham models this difficult withdrawal: he did it respectfully (offering peace, seeking forgiveness), he did it decisively (leaving rather than compromising), and he did it trustingly (calling upon his Lord, confident he would not be wretched). The believer who must, for the sake of truth, separate from family or community that opposes God is not abandoning the human bonds of love; Abraham continued to love and pray for his father. But there are limits beyond which loyalty to falsehood must yield to loyalty to truth, and Abraham — who left his father's house and was given the fatherhood of a nation of believers — shows that the God who receives those who leave all for Him repays the loss a hundredfold.

---
## Sūrah Maryam 19:49

> **So when he had withdrawn from them and that which they called upon apart from God, We bestowed upon him Isaac and Jacob, and each We made a prophet.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Withdrawal Completed, the Gift Bestowed**

The verse marks the direct consequence of Abraham's separation: *fa-lammā iʿtazalahum wa-mā yaʿbudūna min dūni Allāhi wahabnā lahu Isḥāqa wa-Yaʿqūb* — "So when he had withdrawn from them and that which they worshipped apart from God, We bestowed upon him Isaac and Jacob." The conjunction *fa* ("so") and the phrase *lammā* ("when") bind cause and effect: it was precisely after Abraham's withdrawal from his father, his people, and their idols that God gave him Isaac and Jacob. The sequence is the sūrah's clearest statement of its recurring law: the gift follows the withdrawal. Abraham had given up his father's house for God; God gave him in return a house greater than any he had left — a lineage of prophets. The commentators note that Isaac and Jacob were bestowed upon Abraham to compensate him for the loss of his father and his people (Ṭ), and al-Ṭabarī observes that the gift of these two prophets was far better than what Abraham had lost. Abraham lost an idolatrous father; he gained Isaac and Jacob, patriarchs of faith.

The verse connects Abraham's story to the parallel accounts that have preceded it in the sūrah. God granted John to Zachariah after his secret prayer in the sanctuary, Jesus to Mary after her withdrawal to the eastern place, and now Isaac and Jacob to Abraham after his separation from his people. The pattern is consistent and deliberate: throughout the sūrah, God's Compassion bestows blessed children and successors upon His servants precisely at the point of their greatest renunciation and solitude. The commentators also tie this to Abraham's prayer recorded in verse 48 — that in calling upon his Lord he would not be wretched — noting that the answer to that prayer was the gift of Isaac and Jacob (Q). The one who left all for God and called upon his Lord in his solitude was not made wretched; he was given a family of prophets.

**"And Each We Made a Prophet"**

The verse concludes: *wa-kullan jaʿalnā nabiyyan* — "and each We made a prophet." The "each" is understood by the commentators to include Abraham himself along with Isaac and Jacob (Ṭ) — all three were made prophets by God. The statement thus confirms the divine election of the whole line: Abraham the *ṣiddīq nabiyy*, and his son Isaac and grandson Jacob, all prophets, all inheritors of the covenant that God had made with Abraham. Isaac and Jacob are identified as prophets elsewhere in the Qur'an (6:84–89; 21:72–73; 29:27), and the genealogy of prophethood that runs through them is the sūrah's demonstration that the spiritual heritage Abraham preserved by his withdrawal was passed on, generation after generation, through his purified descendants.

**The Law of the Gift After the Withdrawal**

The verse's lesson is one of the most important in the sūrah, and it speaks directly to every believer who has ever faced the choice between faithfulness and the comfort of family and community. Abraham's story teaches that renunciation for God is never ultimately a loss. When a person withdraws from what opposes God — even at the cost of family, home, and belonging — God does not leave that person empty. He compensates the loss with gifts that exceed what was surrendered, just as He gave Abraham a lineage of prophets to replace the father and people he had left. The withdrawal may be painful and lonely; Abraham experienced the solitude of separation. But the sūrah insists that the solitude is the seedbed of the divine gift, and that the one who leaves all for God finds in God more than all he left. This is the deep consolation of Abraham's account, and it is the reason the sūrah holds him up for remembrance: his story is the proof, enacted in sacred history, that no one who gives up anything for God is ever ultimately a loser.

---
## Sūrah Maryam 19:50

> **And We bestowed upon them something of Our Mercy, and ordained for them a sublime, faithful renown.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**Something of Our Mercy: The Gifts Beyond Children**

The verse adds a further dimension to the gifts God gave Abraham, Isaac, and Jacob: *wa-wahabnā lahum min raḥmatinā* — "and We bestowed upon them something of Our Mercy." Having given them Isaac and Jacob, God also bestowed upon them "something of His Mercy." The commentators offer complementary readings. Some understand the mercy as bounty and provision in this world — wealth and children and the good things of life (Ṭ, Th). Others understand it as the higher gifts of prophethood and revelation (Th), the spiritual graces that attended their prophetic calling. The phrase "something of Our Mercy" (*min raḥmatinā*) is deliberately modest and open-ended: the mercy of God is vast beyond measure, and what was given to Abraham and his descendants was but a portion of it. The verse thus points beyond the particular gifts to the infinite mercy from which they flow — the same mercy with which the sūrah opened in verse 2 and which is invoked throughout by the Name al-Raḥmān.

The combination of the two gifts is worth noting. Abraham was given both the earthly good of children (Isaac and Jacob) and the spiritual good of prophethood and revelation. The gift of children might have been merely a worldly consolation for the family he had lost; but joined to the gift of prophethood, it became something far greater — a lineage through which the mercy of God would flow to countless generations. The verse teaches that God's gifts are not merely replacements for what is lost but elevations of it: Abraham did not receive just a new family to replace his old one; he received a family of prophets, a living channel of divine mercy to the world.

**"A Sublime, Faithful Renown"**

The verse then names a third and extraordinary gift: *wa-jaʿalnā lahum lisāna ṣidqin ʿaliyyan* — "and We ordained for them a sublime, faithful renown." The phrase is an idiomatic rendering of *lisāna ṣidqin ʿaliyyan*, which literally means "a sublime tongue of truthfulness." The commentators all indicate that the phrase refers to the high reputation and sincere praise that Abraham and his family enjoyed, and still enjoy, among people of many creeds (Q, Ṭ, Th, Ṭs) — Jews, Christians, Muslims, and even the pre-Islamic pagan Arabs, who revered Abraham as their venerable forefather. Abraham's name is spoken with praise by billions across the centuries and across the religious traditions; his "renown" is indeed sublime — raised high above the ordinary fame of worldly figures.

The commentators connect this gift to Abraham's own supplication recorded elsewhere: "And make for me faithful renown (lisān ṣidq) among later generations" (26:84). God answered that prayer by granting Abraham precisely what he asked — and more. The "tongue of truthfulness" is not merely fame but *truthful* fame, a reputation earned by and founded upon truth: Abraham is praised because he was truthful (*ṣiddīq*), and the renown God gave him is a renown that will never be tarnished by falsehood, because it rests upon the solid foundation of his actual character and deeds. The gift of true renown is thus the fitting reward for a life lived in truth.

**The Sufi Reading: Withdrawal and Its Fruits**

The Sufi commentator Ibn ʿAjībah draws from this verse a teaching of enduring relevance to the spiritual path. He interprets the verse to mean that all those who remove themselves from worldly and created things and withdraw to the "realm of Truth" in order to witness the Truth will be rewarded with spiritual knowledge and "holy gifts."
