He explains that withdrawing from creatures is a necessary condition at the beginning of the Sufi path, but that when seekers reach a certain level, it becomes incumbent upon them to mingle with people and be in the world — for at that point their encounters with others will increase their knowledge and understanding. He further records that Shaykh Abū'l-Ḥasan al-Shādhilī (d. 656/1258) cited this verse when commenting that the practice of solitude leads to "the lifting of veils, the descent of Mercy, the realization of love, and 'a truthful tongue' in speech" (Aj).

This Sufi reading universalizes Abraham's experience. The withdrawal that Abraham practiced physically — leaving his father, his people, and his land — is, in the spiritual life, the interior withdrawal from all that distracts the soul from God. And the promise attached to that withdrawal is the promise Abraham received: the descent of mercy, the gift of spiritual children (the knowledge and graces that bear fruit), and a "truthful tongue" — a life that speaks truth and is remembered truthfully. The verse thus holds out to every seeker the assurance that the path of renunciation, though it passes through solitude, ends in the very gifts that make solitude worthwhile — the mercy, the remembrance, and the renown that come from God to those who leave all for Him.

---
## Sūrah Maryam 19:51

> **And remember Moses in the Book. Verily he was devoted, and he was a messenger, a prophet.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**Moses Added to the Gallery of Remembrance**

With verse 51 the sūrah continues its parade of prophets, turning now to Moses: *wa-dhkur fī al-Kitābi Mūsā* — "And remember Moses in the Book." The command repeats the formula used for Mary (verse 16) and Abraham (verse 41). Moses is one of the most frequently mentioned figures in the entire Qur'an — his full story is told in Sūrahs 2, 7, 20, 26, 27, 28, and elsewhere — but here the sūrah presents him with great brevity, sketching only the essential features of his character and the signal moments of his calling. The brevity is itself significant: the sūrah's portraits are arranged so that each prophet contributes a particular facet to its theme, and Moses' contribution here is concentrated in the two attributes named in this verse and developed in the two that follow — his devotedness and the intimate manner of his call.

Moses is introduced with the title *mukhliṣ*, translated "devoted": *innahu kāna mukhliṣan* — "verily he was devoted." The root *kh-l-ṣ* conveys purity and sincerity — the act of being single-minded and free from admixture. The commentators explain the sense in two complementary ways, and both are noted as valid by al-Ṭabarī. Read as *mukhliṣ*, it means that Moses was devoted to the One God, worshipping Him alone, without hypocrisy or the admixture of any false motive (IK, Q, Ṭ, Ṭs, Z). Read as *mukhlaṣ*, it means that Moses was "specially chosen" and purified — that God had selected him and made him sincere (IK, Q, Ṭ, Ṭs, Z). The two readings together give the full picture: Moses was devoted because he was chosen; his sincerity was itself a divine gift that he realized in his life of worship. For the Sufis, this sincerity (*ikhlāṣ*) came to mean the worship of God only for God's sake, not for the sake of any worldly or otherworldly reward (Aj) — a purity of intention that Moses embodied.

**"A Messenger, a Prophet"**

The verse continues with Moses' double title: *wa-kāna rasūlan nabiyyan* — "and he was a messenger, a prophet." Here the distinction between the two titles, which Islamic tradition refined (as noted in the commentary on verse 41), is explicit. Moses is a *rasūl* (messenger) because he brought his people a new scripture and law — the Torah — and a *nabī* (prophet) because he received and conveyed divine messages. He is thus one of the "messengers of strong will" (ulu al-ʿazm) in the Islamic tradition, a prophet who brought a comprehensive divine law to his people. The pairing *rasūlan nabiyyan* recurs for Moses and other great prophets in the Qur'an, and it signals the highest rank of prophethood: one who both receives divine communication and is charged with delivering a new revelation and law to a people.

**The Place of Moses in the Sūrah's Architecture**

Moses' placement in Sūrah Maryam, brief as his portrait is, serves the sūrah's larger purposes. Like the other prophets in the chapter, Moses received his prophetic gifts after withdrawal and separation — he left the court of Pharaoh, fled to Midian, and encountered God on the Mount in his solitude. And like Abraham, Moses confronted a tyrant and a people in error with the truth, enduring rejection before his eventual vindication. His brief appearance here connects him to the sūrah's two central demonstrations: the power of God to choose and raise His servants regardless of human circumstance, and the Mercy of God that descends upon those who turn to Him in sincerity and devotion. The verse that names Moses "devoted, a messenger, a prophet" is thus a compressed summary of a life that the Qur'an elsewhere unfolds at length — and the two verses that follow will focus on the two signal moments of that life: his call on the Mount and the gift of his brother Aaron.

---
## Sūrah Maryam 19:52

> **We called out to him from the right side of the Mount, and drew him nigh in intimate discourse.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Call from the Right Side of the Mount**

The verse recalls the moment of Moses' prophetic commissioning: *wa-nādaynāhu min jānibi al-Ṭūri al-ayman* — "And We called out to him from the right side of the Mount." The Mount (*al-Ṭūr*) is Mount Sinai (*Ṭūr Sīnīn*), the sacred mountain where God spoke to Moses; the commentators note that the right side refers to the side of the mountain that was on Moses' right as he approached from Midian and saw the burning bush in the distance (IK, Q, Ṭ, Ṭs; cf. 20:10; 27:7; 28:29). The call came to Moses at the moment of his approach to the burning bush — the bush that burned but was not consumed, the sign of the divine presence that Moses encountered in his solitude in the wilderness. The verse recalls one of the most pivotal moments in sacred history: the moment when a fugitive shepherd, tending his flocks far from the civilization of Egypt, was summoned by God to become the deliverer of His people.

The "right side" of the Mount carries symbolic resonance in the spiritual tradition. In the Qur'an, the right side is repeatedly associated with blessing, favor, and the auspicious — those of the right hand are the blessed (56:8, 27, 38, 90). The call from the right side of the Mount is thus a call from the side of blessing and favor. In the Islamic mystical tradition, the "right side of the Mount" is sometimes used as a symbol for the innermost dimension of the heart (K), which the Sufis describe as the place of divine self-revelation and the locus of intimate discourse with God. Moses' call from the right side of the physical Mount mirrors the interior reality that every seeker of God is called from the deepest chamber of the heart, where the divine presence may be encountered.

**"And Drew Him Nigh in Intimate Discourse"**

The verse concludes with the extraordinary manner of Moses' call: *wa-qarrabnāhu najiyyan* — "and drew him nigh in intimate discourse (najiyy)." The word *najiyy* denotes one who converses secretly and intimately — a confidant. Moses was drawn near to God for private, intimate converse. The commentators describe the nearness in various ways. Some say God drew Moses so close that he could hear the scratching of the pen upon the tablets of the Torah, or hear the direct speech of God (IK, Q, Ṭ, Ṭs; cf. 7:143). Others understand the nearness as elevation to a noble station — the station of one with whom God speaks directly (Ṭs). Still others offer mystical descriptions: there are, it is said, seventy thousand veils of light and darkness between the Throne and the earth, and Moses came so close that only a single veil remained between himself and God (Ṭ, Th). Whatever the precise imagery, the verse points to the unique honor of Moses: he was one with whom God spoke directly and intimately, earning him the title *Kalīm Allāh* — "the one to whom God spoke" (cf. 4:164).

The commentators are careful about the theological implications of this nearness. Some insist that the verse should not be read as describing physical proximity, since God does not occupy place or space; rather, the "drawing nigh" refers to the honor of direct converse and the elevation of Moses to a station of nobility and purity (Ṭs). Others note that Moses' experience of nearness was unique among the prophets in its directness — he heard God's speech without an intermediary, in a manner granted to no prophet before him in the same way. The verse thus records the intimacy that is possible between God and His chosen servant — a nearness achieved not by Moses' own effort but by God's drawing him nigh, a gift of divine favor rather than an attainment of human merit.

**The Pattern: Called in Solitude, Raised in Intimacy**

Moses' call fits the sūrah's recurring pattern with precise symmetry. Like Zachariah in his sanctuary, Mary in her eastern place, and Abraham in his withdrawal from his people, Moses was in solitude — alone in the wilderness of Midian, far from Egypt — when God called him. And the call was not a distant summons but an intimate drawing near. The verse teaches that the soul that is alone with God, withdrawn from the noise of the world, is the soul that God can approach with intimacy. Moses had fled from Pharaoh's court into the desert; in that desert solitude, God drew him nigh. The believer who creates the solitude of prayer and remembrance, who withdraws from the distractions that crowd out the divine voice, opens the door to the same kind of nearness — the intimate converse with God that is the deepest longing of the human soul and the greatest gift the soul can receive.

---
## Sūrah Maryam 19:53

> **And We bestowed upon him, from Our Mercy, his brother, Aaron, a prophet.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Gift of a Brother-Prophet**

The account of Moses closes, as the earlier accounts have closed, with the gift of a companion and helper from God's Mercy: *wa-wahabnā lahu min raḥmatinā akhāhu Hārūna nabiyyan* — "And We bestowed upon him, from Our Mercy, his brother Aaron, a prophet." The pattern is now unmistakable, for it is the fourth occurrence in the sūrah. God granted John to Zachariah from His Mercy (verses 2–7); Jesus to Mary; Isaac and Jacob to Abraham (verse 49); and now Aaron to Moses. The gift is explicitly said to be *min raḥmatinā* — "from Our Mercy" — the same phrase used of the gifts to Abraham (verse 50) and echoed throughout the chapter. Aaron, the elder brother of Moses, was himself a prophet, and God's bestowal of Aaron upon Moses is understood to mean that God gave Moses the gift of Aaron's prophethood and his support (Ṭ).

The gift of Aaron answers a specific need and a specific prayer. The Qur'an records that Moses prayed: "And appoint for me a helper from among my family, Aaron, my brother" (20:29–30), and "my brother Aaron is more eloquent than me in speech. So send him with me as a helper" (28:34). Moses, aware of his own limitations in speech and the enormity of the task of confronting Pharaoh, asked God for a helper; and God, in His Mercy, gave him Aaron — a prophet who would support him in his mission, speak on his behalf, and stand beside him before Pharaoh and the people. The commentators note that Aaron was a prophet though not a messenger, since he served to support the revelation brought by Moses rather than bringing a separate one (cf. 21:48; 23:45; 37:117).

**The Companionship of the Prophets**

The verse teaches something profound about God's way of working with His servants. God does not ordinarily send His prophets into the world alone; He provides them with companions, helpers, and supporters to ease their mission. Moses was given Aaron; the Prophet Muhammad ﷺ was given his Companions and, in a special sense, Abū Bakr as his companion in the cave during the Hijrah; and the Qur'an repeatedly emphasizes the mutual support of the believers. The gift of Aaron to Moses is a model of divine providence: God knows the needs of His servants and provides for them from His Mercy — not always by removing the difficulty but by sending companions to share it. Aaron did not remove the burden of confronting Pharaoh; he shared it, strengthening Moses with his presence and his eloquence.

In certain Sufi commentaries, Aaron is understood to represent the esoteric dimension of the revelation given to Moses (Rb), and his position is compared to that of ʿAlī ibn Abī Ṭālib in relation to the Prophet Muhammad ﷺ, as in the famous hadith in which the Prophet said to ʿAlī: "Your position in relation to me is as the position of Aaron in relation to Moses, except that there shall be no prophet after me" (al-Bukhārī and Muslim). The comparison underscores the role of the faithful companion and supporter who stands beside the prophet and shares his mission without claiming his office. The verse's quiet teaching — that God gives His servants the companions they need, from His Mercy — is a source of comfort to every believer who feels alone in a difficult task: the God who gave Moses Aaron will provide the support His servants require, at the time and in the manner He knows best.

---
## Sūrah Maryam 19:54

> **And remember Ishmael in the Book. Verily he was true to the promise, and he was a messenger, a prophet.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**Ishmael: The Prophet of the Promise**

The sūrah's gallery now turns to Ishmael, the firstborn son of Abraham: *wa-dhkur fī al-Kitābi Ismāʿīl* — "And remember Ishmael in the Book." The command again uses the recurring formula of remembrance. Ishmael, the son of Abraham and Hagar, is identified by the commentators as both a messenger and a prophet, since he was responsible for bringing Abrahamic monotheism to the people of Arabia — especially to the tribe of Jurhum, among whom he and his mother settled in Makkah after Abraham, at God's command, had left them there (Ṭs). The Qur'an elsewhere describes Ishmael and his mother at the well of Zamzam and the building of the Kaʿbah with his father Abraham (2:125–129; 14:37). Ishmael is thus the patriarch of the Arabs and, in the Islamic tradition, the ancestor of the Prophet Muhammad ﷺ — a figure of the highest significance whose mention here links the earlier prophets to the Arabian setting of the final revelation.

Ishmael's defining attribute is stated in a single phrase: *innahu kāna ṣādiqa al-waʿd* — "verily he was true to the promise." The commentators explain that this refers to his reputation for always keeping his word (Ṭ, Ṭs). Many relate an account in which someone asked Ishmael to wait for him at a specified location and then forgot about him; Ishmael is said to have waited there — either for several days or, according to some reports, for an entire year — without leaving the spot (IK, Q, Ṭs, Z). A similar account is told of the Prophet Muhammad ﷺ before his prophetic mission (IK, Q). For those who consider Ishmael, rather than Isaac, to have been the son that Abraham nearly sacrificed in Sūrah 37 (37:102), the description of Ishmael as "true to the promise" also alludes to his steadfastness during that supreme ordeal (Q) — the son who, when his father told him of the command to sacrifice him, replied, "You shall find me, God willing, among the patient" (37:102).

**The Virtue of Keeping One's Word**

The description of Ishmael as "true to the promise" elevates a seemingly ordinary virtue to prophetic rank. Faithfulness to one's word is presented in the Qur'an as a mark of the highest moral character — the Qur'an praises those who "fulfill their covenant" and "keep their promises" (2:177; 17:34; 23:8). Ishmael's whole characterization is summed up in this one quality: he was a man whose word could be trusted absolutely, to the point of waiting a year for someone who had forgotten him rather than break his commitment. The example is striking for its application to ordinary life. The keeping of promises is one of the foundational virtues of human society — the glue of trust upon which families, friendships, businesses, and communities depend — and the Qur'an dignifies it by making it the defining trait of a prophet. If Ishmael could wait a year for a forgotten promise, how much more should the believer honor the ordinary commitments of daily life — the appointments kept, the debts repaid, the words honored?

**"A Messenger, a Prophet"**

The verse closes with Ishmael's double title: *wa-kāna rasūlan nabiyyan* — "and he was a messenger, a prophet." As with Moses (verse 51), the pairing signals the highest rank of prophethood. Ishmael is understood to have been a messenger because he was charged with conveying the message of monotheism to a people — the Arabs among whom he settled — and a prophet because he received divine revelation. The verse thus honors Ishmael as a full prophet and messenger in his own right, not merely as the son of Abraham. His placement in the sūrah, with his unique quality of truthfulness to his word, adds yet another facet to the chapter's composite portrait of prophetic character: Zachariah the prayerful, John the pure, Jesus the servant, Abraham the truthful and devoted, Moses the intimate with God, and Ishmael the keeper of the promise.

---
## Sūrah Maryam 19:55

> **He used to bid his people to prayer and almsgiving, and he was pleasing unto his Lord.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Prophet as Teacher of His People**

The portrait of Ishmael is completed by a description of his ministry: *wa-kāna yaʾmuru ahlahu bi-al-ṣalāti wa-al-zakāti* — "and he used to bid his people (ahlahu) to prayer and almsgiving." The word *ahlahu* can mean his family or his people — those over whom he had responsibility and influence. Ishmael's prophethood was not confined to his own devotion; he actively commanded those around him to prayer (*ṣalāh*) and almsgiving (*zakāh*). The pairing of prayer and almsgiving is, as noted in the commentary on verse 31, one of the most frequent couplings in the Qur'an for describing the complete religious life — the vertical worship of God joined to the horizontal care for others. Ishmael is presented as one who did not merely practice these duties himself but taught them to his people, bidding them to the worship of God and the purification of charity.

The commentators note the character of Ishmael's teaching. Some say he used to command his people to perform prayer at night and to give charity during the day (Ṭs), tying the two duties to the two great divisions of the day. Others see in his bidding his "people" the model of a prophet and a father who took responsibility for the religious formation of those in his charge. The verse echoes the instruction given to the Prophet Muhammad ﷺ to "bid thy family to prayer" (20:132), and it establishes a pattern that runs through the Qur'an: the one who has received guidance is not only to live by it but to teach it to those around him, beginning with his own family and people.

**"He Was Pleasing unto His Lord"**

The verse concludes with Ishmael's standing before God: *wa-kāna ʿinda rabbihi marḍiyyan* — "and he was, with his Lord, pleasing (marḍiyy)." The word *marḍiyy* comes from the same root (*r-ḍ-w*) as the *raḍiyy* ("well-pleasing") that Zachariah prayed his son would be in verse 6. Ishmael was pleasing unto his Lord as a result of his obedience, righteousness, purity, and his avoidance of all ugliness in his conduct (Ṭ, Ṭs). The description connects Ishmael to the larger theme of divine contentment that runs through the sūrah and the Qur'an: the supreme good is to be one of whom God is content, of whom it is said, "God is content with them, and they are content with Him" (5:119). Ishmael attained that state — he was pleasing to his Lord.

The verse also draws a quiet connection between Ishmael and the other figures of the sūrah. Zachariah prayed for a son who would be *raḍiyy* (well-pleasing) to God (verse 6); John was described as reverent and dutiful (verses 13–14); and now Ishmael is described as *marḍiyy* — pleasing to his Lord. The echo of the root across the chapter ties the prophets together in their common orientation: each lived so as to be pleasing to God, and God's acceptance of them is the seal of their prophethood. For the believer, the example of Ishmael — who both practiced and taught prayer and charity, and who was pleasing to his Lord — holds up the ideal of a life that is at once devout, charitable, and accepted by God: a life of worship and giving that earns the supreme reward of divine contentment.

---
## Sūrah Maryam 19:56

> **And remember Idrīs in the Book. Verily he was truthful, a prophet.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**Idrīs: The Prophet of Antiquity**

The sūrah's gallery reaches now to the most ancient of the prophets it mentions: *wa-dhkur fī al-Kitābi Idrīs* — "And remember Idrīs in the Book." Idrīs is one of the more mysterious figures of the Qur'an, mentioned here and in two other places (21:85, with Ishmael and Dhu'l-Kifl among the patient; and 19:56–57). The commentators identify Idrīs most commonly with the Biblical Enoch, the great-grandfather of Noah (Q, Ṭs, Th), though he is sometimes also identified with the Quranic Elias (Ilyās) (see 6:85; 37:123). Given his antediluvian setting, the tradition attributes to Idrīs the origins of several arts and sciences: he is said to have been the first to write with a pen, to have had knowledge of astronomy and mathematics, and to have made sewn clothing from animal skins (Q, Ṭs, Z). In Islamic philosophical and metaphysical literature he is often called *Abū al-Ḥukamāʾ*, "father of the philosophers [or sages]," a title reflecting the tradition that he was the first among the children of Adam to receive the divine wisdom that the philosophers and sages would later inherit. Some say he was given thirty scrolls (Q, Z).

The etymological associations of the name Idrīs attracted comment. Some suggested that the name derives from the root *d-r-s*, meaning "to study," because of Idrīs's great love of studying and learning (Q, Th, Ṭs), though al-Zamakhsharī cast doubt on this interpretation. Whether or not the etymology is sound, the association captures the traditional portrait of Idrīs as a figure of knowledge and learning — one who studied the world and the signs of God and was honored by God with prophethood.

**"Truthful, a Prophet"**

Idrīs is introduced with the same double description applied to Abraham in verse 41: *innahu kāna ṣiddīqan nabiyyan* — "verily he was truthful (ṣiddīq), a prophet." The repetition of the exact phrase is deliberate; it places Idrīs in the same rank of truthfulness as Abraham. As noted in the commentary on verse 41, *ṣiddīq* denotes one who is truthful and sincere in the highest degree — one who constantly confirms and embodies the truth (R). The recurrence of the phrase for Idrīs suggests that this ancient prophet, whose historical details are so scant, is honored above all for the quality that the Qur'an prizes most: a thoroughgoing truthfulness that made him worthy of prophecy. The pairing *ṣiddīq nabiyy* is thus the sūrah's compact formula of prophetic excellence, applied here to the most ancient of its prophets, linking the beginning of the prophetic line to the figures of the recent past.

**The Remembrance of the Ancient**

The command to "remember Idrīs in the Book" carries a particular poignancy, for Idrīs belongs to a time before the memory of most peoples. He was one of the first prophets after Adam, living before the flood of Noah, in a world of which almost nothing else is recorded. Yet the Qur'an preserves his name and his honor. The verse teaches that God does not forget His faithful servants, however ancient, however little the world remembers them. Just as Idrīs is remembered in the Book that God revealed centuries after his time, so the faithfulness of God's servants — in every age, however obscure — is preserved in the divine remembrance. The verse thus extends a quiet promise: the believer who is truthful and devoted to God, even if the world never knows his name, is remembered by the One whose remembrance alone endures.

---
## Sūrah Maryam 19:57

> **And We raised him to a sublime station.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**Raised to a Sublime Station**

The account of Idrīs closes with a single verse of extraordinary import: *wa-rafaʿnāhu makānan ʿaliyyan* — "And We raised him to a sublime (ʿaliyy) station." The commentators explain this "raising" in several ways. Some say it means that God raised Idrīs bodily or in spirit to the fourth or sixth heaven (Q, Ṭ, Ṭs, Z) — a claim supported in the tradition by the report that the Prophet Muhammad ﷺ encountered Idrīs in the fourth heaven during his miraculous Night Journey and Ascension (*al-miʿrāj*) through the seven heavens (Q). Others hold that Idrīs has not yet died, having been raised alive or taken directly by God (Ṭ), much like Jesus (4:158). Still others understand the "sublime station" more modestly as referring simply to the high rank of prophethood that God granted him (Z). Whatever the precise interpretation, the verse records that God honored Idrīs with an elevation beyond the ordinary — a raising to a station of eminence that distinguishes him even among the prophets.

The theme of elevation connects Idrīs to the sūrah's preoccupation with divine sovereignty over human destiny. Just as God "raises" in rank whom He wills, and just as He bestowed prophetic gifts upon the aged, the barren, the virgin, and the child throughout this chapter, so He raised Idrīs to a station that no human effort could have attained. The elevation is a gift, not an achievement: Idrīs is described in verse 56 as truthful and a prophet, but the raising in this verse is something God does to him — *rafaʿnāhu*, "We raised him." The believer's elevation is ultimately God's act, the reward that God bestows upon His faithful servants in the measure of His own generosity.

**The Nearness of the Raised Servant**

The verse also invites reflection on the dignity that God confers upon those who love Him. Idrīs, remembered for his truthfulness and his devotion to learning, was raised to a sublime station — drawn near to the divine presence in a manner that set him apart. The tradition's accounts of his being raised alive, or of his encounter by the Prophet in the fourth heaven, all point to the same truth: that for the servant who is truthful and devoted, death is not the end but a passage, and nearness to God is the reward of faithfulness. The verse holds out to every believer the hope that the truthfulness and devotion practiced in this life — the very qualities for which Idrīs is remembered — lead ultimately to an elevation beyond this world. The servant who lives in the remembrance of God is raised by God to a station that the world cannot give and cannot take away.

---
## Sūrah Maryam 19:58

> **They are those whom God has blessed among the prophets of the progeny of Adam, and of those whom We carried with Noah, and of the progeny of Abraham and Israel, and of those whom We guided and chose. When the signs of the Compassionate were recited unto them, they would fall down, prostrate and weeping.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Assembly of the Blessed Prophets**

With verse 58 the sūrah draws together the prophets it has been presenting and gathers them into a single company: *ulāʾika alladhīna anʿama Allāhu ʿalayhim mina al-nabiyyīna* — "They are those whom God has blessed among the prophets." The "they" refers to all the prophets and sacred figures mentioned to this point in the sūrah (Ṭ, Z): Zachariah, John, Jesus, Mary, Abraham, Isaac, Jacob, Moses, Aaron, Ishmael, and Idrīs. The verse then organizes this company according to their genealogies: the "progeny of Adam" (which includes all of them, or refers specifically to Idrīs, who predates Noah and Abraham); "those whom We carried with Noah" (Abraham and his descendants, who are traced through Noah); "the progeny of Abraham and Israel" (that is, Jacob); and "those whom We guided and chose." Each prophet is situated within the line of divine election that runs from Adam, through Noah, through Abraham and Jacob, to the prophets of Israel — a single continuous thread of divine guidance through the ages.

The verse emphasizes that these prophets were *blessed by God* — *anʿama Allāhu ʿalayhim* — and that they were "guided and chosen." Their prophethood was not their own attainment but the result of God's guidance and choice. The repetition of genealogical categories underscores the unity of the prophetic message: though they lived in different ages and among different peoples, the prophets of Adam's progeny, of Noah's descendants, of Abraham's line, and of Israel's children were all recipients of the one divine guidance, all chosen by the one God. The verse thus weaves the individual portraits of the sūrah into a single tapestry of divine election stretching from the first prophet to the time of the Prophet Muhammad ﷺ.

**"When the Signs of the Compassionate Were Recited unto Them"**

The verse then describes the defining spiritual response of this prophetic company: *idhā tutlā ʿalayhim āyātu al-Raḥmāni kharrū sujjadan wa-bukiyyan* — "When the signs of the Compassionate were recited unto them, they would fall down, prostrating and weeping." The "signs" (*āyāt*) are the verses and indications of God recited to them from the revealed Books (Ṭ). And the response of the prophets to the recitation of God's signs was total and overwhelming: they would *fall down*, prostrate themselves, and *weep*. The two verbs together express a complete physical and emotional surrender — the body prosternating in worship and the heart dissolving in tears. This is not the measured, controlled response of detached listeners; it is the response of souls overwhelmed by the reality of the divine words, moved to the depth of their being.

The weeping of the prophets upon hearing God's signs is given particular emphasis, and it connects to a broader theme in the Islamic tradition. Weeping is indicated in several hadiths as the appropriate response to hearing the Qur'an; according to one hadith, the Prophet enjoined: "Recite the Qur'an and weep. And if you do not weep, then make yourself weep" (recorded by al-Zamakhsharī among others). The response of the prophets is the model of this weeping — a heart so moved by the divine speech that it cannot contain itself, a soul so aware of its Lord that the recitation of His signs dissolves it into tears of awe, love, and longing.

**The Prostřration of the Heart and Body**

The verse is also one of the fifteen *sajdah* (prostration) verses of the Qur'an — verses upon hearing which Muslims are enjoined to prostrate physically. (The others include 7:206; 13:15; 16:49–50; 17:107–9; 22:18; 22:77; 25:60; 27:26; 32:15; 38:24; 41:37–38; 53:62; 84:21; 96:19.) The command to prostrate at this verse is rooted in the very scene it describes: the prophets fell prostrate when the signs of the Compassionate were recited to them, and the believer who recites or hears this verse follows their example in physical prostration. The bodily prostration is the outward expression of an inward reality — the prostration of the heart before the truth it has realized (Qu). Al-Qushayrī notes that the spiritual quality of "tenderness of heart" (*riqqat al-qalb*), which this verse exemplifies, was shared by all the prophets.

Ibn ʿAjībah adds that such a response to the recitation of the Qur'an is characteristic of the first stage of the love of God (*maḥabbah*), which is later replaced by joy at hearing the Words of the Beloved as one draws spiritually nearer to Him. The verse thus charts the growth of the spiritual life: what begins as tears of overwhelming awe at the recitation of God's signs matures, in those who draw near, into the joy of intimate communion. For the believer, the example of the prophets holds up the ideal response to the Qur'an: not indifference, not mere intellectual appreciation, but a heart that is genuinely moved — that falls down in prostration and weeps at the beauty and power of God's words, and that allows that response to deepen, over time, into the abiding joy of those who dwell in the presence of the Compassionate.

---
## Sūrah Maryam 19:59

> **Then they were succeeded by a generation who neglected prayer and followed base desires. So they shall meet [the reward of] error,**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Successors Who Lost the Way**

The verse marks a tragic turn in the sūrah's narrative: *fa-khalafa min baʿdihim khalfun* — "Then they were succeeded by a generation (khalf)." The word *khalf* (successor, those who come after) is used here in a distinctly negative sense — it denotes a succeeding generation that was unworthy of its predecessors. The commentators note the linguistic nuance: while a worthy successor is called a *khalaf*, this debased generation is called a *khalf*, a successor who fell short. The prophets who wept at the recitation of God's signs were succeeded by a generation that did not follow their example. The commentators differ on who this generation was: Ibn ʿAbbās identified them as the Jews (Ṭs, Z), while Mujāhid and Qatādah said the verse refers to the Muslim community at the end of time (Ṭs). The verse can be read as referring to either or both — any community that, after receiving the guidance of prophets, neglects its duties and follows its desires.

The two sins of this succeeding generation are named precisely: *aḍāʿū al-ṣalāta* — "they neglected prayer" — *wa-ittabaʿū al-shahawāti* — "and they followed base desires." The neglect of prayer (*ṣalāh*) is the outward sign of the loss of connection with God; the following of base desires (*shahawāt*) is the inward corruption that fills the space left by that loss. The commentators debate the precise meaning of "neglected prayer": some say it refers to abandoning prayer altogether (Ṭ, Z), while others say it means failing to perform the prayers at their appointed times or delaying them (Ṭ, Ṭs, Z). Either way, the neglect is a grave failure — for prayer is the very bond between the servant and God that the prophets had maintained with devotion. The two sins are causally linked: the one who neglects the worship of God is left to the tyranny of his own desires, and the one who follows his desires finds himself further and further from the worship of God.

**"So They Shall Meet [the Reward of] Error"**

The verse closes with the consequence of this double failure: *fa-sawfa yalqawna ghayyan* — "so they shall meet (the reward of) error (ghayy)." The commentators explain that the phrase means they will meet the requital or punishment for their error (Ṭs, Z); some thus gloss *ghayy* (error, misguidance) as "evil" (Ṭs). Others report that *ghayy* is the name of a valley, well, or river in Hell (Ṭ, Ṭs, Z) — a place of punishment prepared for those who have gone astray. The warning is stark: the generation that neglected prayer and followed base desires did not simply drift into harmless decline; it ran headlong toward a terrible reckoning. The verse serves as a warning to every community that inherits the guidance of prophets: the inheritance is not automatic protection; it must be actively maintained by prayer and self-restraint, and the failure to do so leads to the meeting with *ghayy*.

**The Lesson for Every Inheritor of Guidance**

The verse holds a lesson of urgent relevance for every generation that has inherited religious guidance. The prophets who wept at God's signs were succeeded by those who neglected prayer and followed desires — and this pattern has repeated throughout history, in every community that received prophets. The warning is not abstract but personal: every believer who has inherited the Qur'an and the example of the prophets is, in a sense, in the position of that succeeding generation, standing at a fork. Will they be the *khalaf*, the worthy successors who maintain prayer and restrain desire, or the *khalf*, the unworthy successors who neglect worship and follow their appetites? The verse leaves no middle ground, and it warns of the meeting with *ghayy* that awaits those who choose the latter path. But the very next verse will open the door of hope — for the *khalf* who neglected prayer and followed desire are not beyond redemption if they turn back.

---
## Sūrah Maryam 19:60

> **save for those who repent and believe and work righteousness. It is they who shall enter the Garden, and they shall not be wronged in the least:**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Exception That Opens the Door of Hope**

The warning of verse 59 is immediately qualified by a door of hope: *illā man tāba wa-āmana wa-ʿamila ṣāliḥan* — "save for those who repent and believe and work righteousness." Even for the generation that neglected prayer and followed base desires, the way back is open. The commentators note that the door of repentance remains open until death (cf. 4:17–18); no matter how far a person has fallen — into the neglect of worship, into the service of desire — the turning back to God is never barred in this life. The verse names three conditions of salvation, in a precise order that the Qur'an repeats throughout: *repentance* (turning away from sin), *belief* (turning toward God in faith), and *righteous works* (living out that faith in action). The order is itself meaningful: one cannot rightly believe while persisting in unrepented sin, and one cannot claim to believe while refusing to act upon it. The three together constitute the complete return to God.

The pairing of belief and righteous works is one of the most constant themes of the Qur'an. Throughout the Book, "those who believe and perform righteous deeds" are promised the Garden (2:25; 3:57; 4:57; 7:42, and many others), for Muslims and for the followers of other faiths who sincerely believe and act rightly (cf. 2:62; 5:69). The repeated coupling indicates the interconnectedness and indispensability of both faith and good works: faith without works is sterile, and works without faith are not directed to God. The verse's placement here — as the exception to the condemnation of the neglectful generation — emphasizes that even the worst negligence can be redeemed by this threefold return, but that the return must be genuine and complete.

**"They Shall Enter the Garden, and They Shall Not Be Wronged in the Least"**

The verse then states the reward of those who repent, believe, and work righteousness: *fa-ulāʾika yadkhulūna al-Jannata wa-lā yuẓlamūna shayʾan* — "It is they who shall enter the Garden, and they shall not be wronged in the least." The Garden is their destination, and the assurance that they will not be wronged "in the least" guarantees the absolute justice of their reward. The phrase points to a central Qur'anic doctrine: God does not wrong anyone, even by a single atom's weight. The Qur'an repeats this assurance in many places (2:272, 281; 3:25, 161; 4:49, 77, 124; 6:160; 8:60; 17:71; 23:62; 39:69), always insisting that divine justice is perfect — every good deed will be fully rewarded, every genuine effort fully credited.

**The Justice of Perfect Accounting**

The assurance "they shall not be wronged in the least" is profoundly reassuring and profoundly sobering at once. It is reassuring because it promises that the righteous will receive the fullness of their reward — nothing of their good deeds will be lost, discounted, or miscredited. It is sobering because it implies the same standard of perfect accounting applies to all — that no one will be credited with more than they earned, and that the scales of divine justice are exact. The verse thus calls the believer to genuine repentance, belief, and works — not to superficial gestures — because the accounting is precise. But for the one who genuinely turns, believes, and acts rightly, the promise is absolute: the Garden, and a reward in which nothing is wronged. This is the hope held out even to the neglectful generation of verse 59, and by extension to every sinner who reads it: the door of return is open, and the reward of those who return is certain.

---
## Sūrah Maryam 19:61

> **Gardens of Eden, those which the Compassionate promised His servants in the Unseen. Verily His Promise shall come to pass.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**Gardens of Eden: The Highest of Paradises**

The verse specifies the nature of the Garden promised to those who repent, believe, and work righteousness: *jannāti ʿadnin* — "Gardens of Eden." The phrase *jannāt ʿadn* (Gardens of Eden) designates the most elevated and abiding of the paradisal gardens of the Hereafter, a term that recurs throughout the Qur'an (9:72; 13:23; 16:31; 18:31; 20:76; 35:33; 38:50; 40:8; 98:8). The word *ʿadn* connotes permanence, abiding residence, and delight — the Gardens of Eden are the eternal dwelling-places of the blessed, gardens that never fade and from which there is no departure. The mention of the Gardens of Eden here connects the reward of the repentant believers to the highest station the Qur'an promises, emphasizing the generosity of God toward those who turn back to Him: the neglectful generation of verse 59, if they repent, believe, and work righteousness, are not merely spared punishment but admitted to the Gardens of Eden.

**"Which the Compassionate Promised His Servants in the Unseen"**

The verse adds a striking qualification: the Gardens of Eden are those *allatī waʿada al-Raḥmānu ʿibādahu bi-al-ghayb* — "which the Compassionate promised His servants in the Unseen." The commentators explain that the Gardens are "in the Unseen" because they remain hidden to human beings while they are in this world (Ṭ) — no eye has seen them, no ear has heard of their full reality. The Qur'an states this explicitly elsewhere: "No soul knows what comfort is kept hidden for it as a recompense for that which they used to do" (32:17). The promise of the Gardens is made in the Unseen — accepted by faith, not by sight — and its full reality will only be unveiled in the Hereafter. This is a profound point about the nature of faith: the believer acts upon a promise whose object he cannot yet perceive, trusting in the One who made the promise.

**"Verily His Promise Shall Come to Pass"**

The verse closes with the assurance that seals the promise: *innahu kāna waʿduhu maʾtiyyan* — "verily His Promise shall come to pass." The phrase *maʾtiyyan* (that which is come to, that will surely arrive) emphasizes the certainty of the divine promise's fulfillment. The Qur'an repeatedly affirms that God's promise is true (4:122; 10:4; 18:21): what God has promised will not fail, will not be delayed beyond its time, and will not be altered. In the present verse, "His Promise" refers to the promise that God's servants will enter the Garden (Ṭ) — the very reward just described. The assurance is absolute because it rests upon the character of God: the One who makes the promise is the One who cannot break a promise, whose word is truth and whose power guarantees its fulfillment.

**The Anchor of Faith**

The verse, taken as a whole, is an anchor for the believer's hope. The reward of repentance, belief, and righteous works — the Gardens of Eden — lies in the Unseen, unseen and unimaginable in this life. The believer cannot verify it by the senses or prove it by the evidence of this world; the Gardens remain hidden until the promise comes to pass. Yet the verse grounds the believer's confidence in two realities: the One who made the promise is *the Compassionate* (al-Raḥmān) — the Merciful whose very nature inclines Him to fulfill His gracious promises; and His promise *shall come to pass* — it is certain, fixed, and guaranteed. The believer who repents, believes, and works righteousness in this world, without seeing the Gardens, is like one who has been promised a treasure by the most trustworthy of givers: the promise is unseen, but the One who made it is known, and that knowledge is sufficient ground for confident hope. This is the very structure of faith in the Unseen that the Qur'an prizes — and this verse both describes the unseen reward and provides the ground for trusting in it.

---
## Sūrah Maryam 19:62

> **They shall hear no idle talk therein, but only "Peace!" And therein they shall have their provision, morning and evening.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**No Idle Talk: The Purity of Paradise**

The verse turns to a description of the experience of the Gardens of Eden: *lā yasmaʿūna fīhā laghwan illā salāman* — "They shall hear no idle talk therein, but only 'Peace!'" The word *laghw* denotes idle, vain, or frivolous speech — the empty chatter that fills so much of human life in this world. The people of Paradise will hear none of it; their ears will be free of the noise of vanity that plagues earthly existence. The Qur'an emphasizes this in several places (52:23; 56:25; 78:35; 88:10–11), always presenting the absence of idle talk as one of the delights of Paradise — the purity of the environment in which the blessed dwell. In this world, even the best conversations are laced with the vain, the trivial, and the hurtful; in Paradise, speech itself is purified.

The only word the people of Paradise hear is *salām* — "Peace!" The greeting of Paradise, exchanged between its inhabitants and extended to them by the angels, is the salutation of peace (7:46; 10:10; 13:24; 14:23; 16:32; 33:44; 36:58; 39:73; 56:26). The commentators note the significance of this single word. Where earthly speech is full of *laghw* (idle talk), the speech of Paradise is full of *salām* (peace). The greeting of peace is the fitting language of a realm in which all conflict has ceased, all anxiety has ended, and all beings dwell in harmony. The word *salām* is also one of the Names of God and the root of *islām* (submission, peace); the greeting of Paradise echoes the peace that comes from total harmony with the divine will. The one word that replaces all idle talk in Paradise is the word that expresses the deepest reality of the blessed state: peace.

**"Their Provision, Morning and Evening"**

The verse adds that the people of Paradise will have their provision there: *wa-lahum rizquhum fīhā bukratan wa-ʿashiyyan* — "and therein they shall have their provision, morning and evening." Their *rizq* — provision, sustenance, all that they desire (Ṭ) — will be granted to them continuously, morning and evening. The commentators discuss how "morning and evening" applies in a realm where, according to some traditions, there is no sun or night. Some explain that there is no night or midday in the Garden, as there is no darkness; "morning" and "evening" are thus measured not by the rising and setting of the sun but by the lowering and lifting of veils and the closing and opening of gates (Ṭ) — markers of time by which the blessed experience the continuous renewal of their provision. Whatever the precise mechanics, the verse conveys the ceaseless and abundant sustenance of Paradise: the blessed never lack, never wait, never fear the interruption of their provision. Where the believer in this world must toil for daily bread and often worries about tomorrow, the people of Paradise receive their provision morning and evening without labor and without anxiety.

**The Contrast with Earthly Life**

The verse paints the blessed state by contrast with the trials of earthly existence. In this world, human beings are surrounded by idle talk that distracts and wounds, and they must labor anxiously for their provision. In Paradise, the idle talk is replaced by the single salutation of peace, and the anxious labor is replaced by the ceaseless gift of provision. The description holds up the peace and plenty that await those who repent, believe, and work righteousness — the reward that God has promised in the Unseen. The believer who endures the noise and toil of this world, who resists idle speech and trusts in God's provision, is being prepared for a realm in which both are transcended: a realm of peace without vanity, and of provision without labor. The verse is thus at once a description of the reward and a summons to live now in a way befitting those destined for it — guarding one's speech from *laghw* and trusting one's provision to God, so that one's present life already anticipates the peace and plenty of the Gardens of Eden.

---
## Sūrah Maryam 19:63

> **This is the Garden We shall bequeath unto those among Our servants who were reverent.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**"This Is the Garden": The Summary of the Reward**

The verse draws the description of Paradise to a summarizing point: *tilka al-Jannatu allatī nūrithu min ʿibādinā man kāna taqiyyan* — "This is the Garden We shall bequeath unto those among Our servants who were reverent." The demonstrative "this" (*tilka*) points back to the Gardens of Eden just described — the gardens free of idle talk, full of peace, with ceaseless provision. The verse then names the qualification for this inheritance: the Garden is bequeathed to those among God's servants who were *reverent* (*taqiyy*). The word *taqiyy*, from the root *w-q-y*, denotes one possessed of *taqwā* — the God-consciousness, protective mindfulness, and reverence that the Qur'an places at the heart of righteousness (cf. 2:2). The qualification recalls the recurring emphasis of the sūrah: John was described as *taqiyy* (verse 13), and the whole chapter has been oriented toward the reverent servants of God.

The use of the verb "bequeath" (*nūrithu*, from *w-r-th*, to inherit) is significant and connects to verse 40, where God is described as the Inheritor of the earth. Here the direction is reversed: God bequeaths the Garden to His servants. The commentators note the richness of the image. A bequest or inheritance is something given to heirs — and the reverent servants of God are, as it were, the heirs of the Garden. The Qur'an speaks elsewhere of the righteous "inheriting" the Garden (cf. 7:43; 23:10–11; 43:72), an image that conveys both the permanence of their possession and the fittingness of the reward: the Garden is given to the reverent as an inheritance, as what rightly belongs to them and what they have been prepared to receive.

**"Among Our Servants": The Inclusive Company**

The phrase "among Our servants" (*min ʿibādinā*) emphasizes that the Garden is given to those who are God's servants — a status that is open to all who choose it. The reverent are not a separate elite but a class within the wider company of God's servants; the qualification is not lineage, wealth, or nation, but *taqwā*, reverence. The verse thus completes the teaching that runs through the whole sūrah: from Zachariah's secret prayer to John's purity to Jesus' servanthood to Abraham's devotion to the repentance of the neglectful generation, the path to the Garden is the path of reverence — the God-conscious life of worship and self-restraint. And the "bequest" of the Garden to the reverent is the culmination of all the promises the sūrah has made: the answer to the Mercy with which it opened, the reward of the faithful servants it has held up for remembrance, and the destination of those who, like the prophets and the repentant, turn to God with reverence and trust.

**The Key to the Garden**

The verse, in its summary brevity, gives the "key" to the Garden that the sūrah has been describing in such rich detail. The Gardens of Eden — free of idle talk, full of peace, with ceaseless provision — are not given to the clever, the wealthy, the powerful, or the well-connected. They are bequeathed to the *reverent* — to those among God's servants who live with the awareness of God that guards them from sin and turns them toward worship and righteousness. This is the egalitarian heart of the Qur'anic vision: the qualification for Paradise is not any worldly distinction but the inner quality of *taqwā*, which is open to every human being who chooses it. The verse holds out the great and simple promise that concludes this section of the sūrah: whoever among God's servants is reverent is an heir of the Garden — a promise as inclusive as it is demanding, for reverence is a path walked by the will, open to all who would walk it.

---
## Sūrah Maryam 19:64

> **"We descend not, save by the Command of thy Lord. Unto Him belongs that which is before us and that which is behind us, and whatsoever lies between that, and thy Lord is not forgetful—"**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Verse of Revelation's Interruption**

Verse 64 addresses a matter of direct concern to the Prophet ﷺ and his community, and the commentators record its occasion. It was reportedly revealed during an unusually long interval in the descent of revelation — an interval reported variously as twelve, fifteen, or forty nights (Ṭ, Z) — during which no revelation came to the Prophet through the Archangel Gabriel (Ṭ). During this period the Prophet was being questioned about the inhabitants of the cave and Dhu'l-Qarnayn (the subjects of Sūrah 18) and about the Spirit; he did not know the answers and hoped for revelation to guide him. When the interval stretched, the idolaters began to suggest that God had abandoned him (Z). When Gabriel finally returned, the Prophet reportedly asked: "What prevents you from visiting us more than you do?" (Ṭ, Ṭs). This verse is understood to be Gabriel's direct response.

The verse begins with Gabriel's words: *wa-mā natanazzalu illā bi-amri rabbika* — "And We descend not, save by the Command of thy Lord." The "We" refers to Gabriel and the angels who descend with revelation (Z). The angel's answer is gentle but firm: he does not come at his own pleasure or by his own choice; he descends only upon the Command of God. The intervals in revelation were not signs of abandonment but the result of the divine ordering of revelation — God sends His angels and His revelation at the times He wills, not at the bidding of any creature. The verse thus reassures the Prophet that the gap in revelation was not a withdrawal of divine favor but a providential pause, decreed by the Lord who sends revelation in the measure and at the time He knows best.

**"Unto Him Belongs That Which Is Before Us and Behind Us"**

Gabriel then affirms the totality of God's sovereignty over time and space: *lahu mā bayna aydīnā wa-mā khalfanā wa-mā bayna dhālika* — "Unto Him belongs that which is before us, and that which is behind us, and whatsoever lies between that." The commentators offer various interpretations of these three dimensions. Some understand them temporally: the affairs of this world and the Hereafter, or the time before the creation of human beings and the time after their annihilation, or simply the past and the future (Ṭ, Ṭs). Others read them spatially, as referring to the earth and the heavens and what lies between (Ṭs). Still others understand "before us" and "behind us" as referring to the two ends of existence and "what lies between" as the present course of earthly life, or the period between the two blowings of the trumpet (Ṭs; cf. 39:68). Whatever the precise referent, the cumulative point is that God possesses all that is before, behind, and between — that He encompasses all of time and all of space, past and future, the seen and the unseen.

**"Thy Lord Is Not Forgetful"**

The verse closes with the assurance that addresses the Prophet's deepest concern: *wa-mā kāna rabbuka nasiyyan* — "and thy Lord is not forgetful." The commentators note that this is meant to assure the Prophet that God has not "forgotten" him (cf. 93:3, where the Prophet is assured that he has not been forsaken). The interval in revelation, the apparent silence of heaven, was not the result of divine forgetfulness or abandonment. God does not forget His servants, does not forget His promises, and does not forget His Messenger. Any interval between revelations was providential and for the Prophet's ultimate benefit (Z).

The phrase "thy Lord is not forgetful" carries a double reassurance that extends far beyond the historical occasion. First, it assures the Prophet and every believer that God's apparent silences are not absences: when heaven seems quiet, when prayers seem unanswered, when guidance seems withheld, God has not forgotten. Second, it grounds the reliability of revelation itself: the Book that God sent down, and the promises it contains, will not be neglected or forgotten by the God who sent them. The verse is thus a word of profound comfort for every soul that has ever felt, in a season of spiritual dryness or unanswered prayer, that God has forgotten it — the assurance that the One who is not forgetful is ever mindful of His servants, and that His silences, like His gifts, come at the time He knows to be best.

---
## Sūrah Maryam 19:65

> **the Lord of the heavens and the earth and whatsoever is between them. So worship Him and be steadfast in His worship. Dost thou know any who can be named alongside Him?"**

**Expanded Commentary**

**Lord of the Heavens and the Earth**

The verse completes the description of God that began in verse 64: *rabbu al-samāwāti wa-al-arḍi wa-mā baynahumā* — "the Lord of the heavens and the earth and whatsoever is between them." This is a title of God that recurs throughout the Qur'an (13:16; 17:102; 18:14; 21:56; 26:24; 37:5; 38:66; 43:82; 44:7; 45:36; 78:37). The title proclaims the universality of God's lordship: He is not the Lord of one people, one region, or one domain, but of the heavens, the earth, and everything between them — of all that exists, seen and unseen, known and unknown. The verse thus grounds the preceding affirmation (that God possesses all that is before, behind, and between) in the concrete reality of God's sovereignty over the whole of creation. The One who sends revelation at His Command is the One who is Lord of all the heavens and the earth — His ordering of revelation is but one instance of His universal governance.

**"So Worship Him and Be Steadfast in His Worship"**

The proclamation of God's universal lordship issues in a double command: *fa-ʿbudhu wa-iṣṭabir li-ʿibādatihi* — "So worship Him and be steadfast (iṣṭabir) in His worship." The first command, worship (*ʿibādah*), is the natural response to the recognition of God as Lord of all: the One to whom all sovereignty belongs is the One who deserves all worship. The second command, to be steadfast in worship (*iṣṭabir*), is the distinguishing demand. The verb *iṣṭabir* is an intensified form of patience and perseverance: it means to be resolutely patient, to endure with constancy. The command to be steadfast in worship is addressed with particular point to the Prophet during the interval of revelation — the very context of verse 64. He is told: do not waver in your worship when revelation is delayed, do not let the silence of heaven shake the constancy of your devotion. Worship God, and remain steadfast in that worship, whatever the apparent intervals in divine communication.

The pairing of worship with steadfastness is a profound teaching about the nature of religious life. Worship is not a matter of occasional fervor but of abiding constancy. The believer who worships only in seasons of spiritual brightness, and abandons worship in seasons of dryness, has not learned the lesson of this verse. Steadfastness in worship means continuing to pray, to remember God, and to serve Him whether the heart feels near or far, whether revelation seems fresh or silent, whether prayers seem answered or deferred. It is the worship of constancy, the devotion that persists through every condition, that God commands. And the command is grounded in the nature of God just proclaimed: the One who is Lord of all the heavens and the earth, who is not forgetful, is worthy of a worship that does not forget Him either.

**"Dost Thou Know Any Who Can Be Named Alongside Him?"**

The verse closes with a rhetorical question of devastating finality: *hal taʿlamu lahu samiyyan* — "Dost thou know any who can be named (samiyy) alongside Him?" The word *samiyy* means a namesake or a peer who could be named with another. The commentators explain the question's force: "Is there any who is His equal or His like in generosity and bounty?" (Ṭ), or "Is there any partner or associate who can be named alongside Him?" (Ṭ). The question is purely rhetorical, for the answer is obviously "no." No one shares God's name in the sense of His nature; no one is His equal; no one can be named alongside the Lord of the heavens and the earth. Some commentators add that the verse establishes that no one other than God should be called *al-Raḥmān* ("the Compassionate") (R, Z) — a Name used so often for God in this sūrah — since the Name belongs exclusively to Him.

The rhetorical question *hal taʿlamu lahu samiyyan* is the sūrah's pointed challenge to every form of idolatry and associationism. The idolaters of Makkah named their gods alongside God, calling them His partners or even His daughters. The verse challenges the basis of such naming: is there any being that can truly be named as God's equal, any reality that shares His lordship, His attributes, or His right to worship? The question answers itself — there is none. The One who is Lord of the heavens and the earth, who is not forgetful, who possesses all that is before, behind, and between, has no peer and no namesake. The command that follows from this recognition is absolute: worship Him alone, and be steadfast in that exclusive worship. The question strips every false god of its claim by asking simply whether it can be named alongside God — and finding that none can, the believer is left with the single, steadfast worship of the One who has no equal.

---
## Sūrah Maryam 19:66

> **Man says, "When I am dead, shall I be brought forth alive?"**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Question That Denies Resurrection**

The sūrah now turns from the prophets and the promise of the Garden to the polemical heart of its message — the confrontation with the idolaters of Makkah who denied the resurrection. The verse records their characteristic utterance: *wa-yaqūlu al-insānu a-idhā mā mittu la-sawfa ukhraju ḥayyan* — "And man says, 'When I am dead, shall I be brought forth alive?'" The word *al-insān* ("man") here refers, in the context of the sūrah's revelation, to the idolatrous opponents of the Prophet, who dismissed the idea of resurrection as absurd. Their question is rhetorical and mocking: how could a dead man, whose body has decayed and scattered, be brought forth alive again? The denial of resurrection was one of the chief doctrines the Makkan idolaters rejected, and the Qur'an repeatedly confronts their disbelief (cf. 17:49–51; 36:78–79; 50:3).

The verse captures the tone of the denial — a mixture of genuine incomprehension and willful mockery. The idolaters found the idea of bodily resurrection incredible, treating it as a return to life after the total dissolution of death. Their question presupposes a particular assumption: that death is the absolute end, that the dissolution of the body is the final word, and that the power that created life in the first place could not, or would not, bring it forth again. It is precisely this assumption — that the first creation does not guarantee the possibility of a second — that the following verse will dismantle.

**The Question That Every Age Repeats**

The denial recorded here is not confined to the idolaters of Makkah. In every age, human beings have asked the same question in one form or another: when I am dead, shall I be brought forth alive? The materialist who sees death as the extinction of consciousness, the cynic who regards the hope of an afterlife as a delusion, the hedonist who lives only for the present because he believes nothing follows it — all are asking, in different idioms, the question of this verse. The Qur'an does not mock the question but takes it seriously, because it is one of the most fundamental questions a human being can ask: is death the end, or is there life beyond it? And the Qur'an's answer, developed in the verses that follow, is built upon an argument that turns the question back upon the questioner: the same power that created you from nothing can certainly raise you from the dead.

---
## Sūrah Maryam 19:67

> **Does man not remember that We created him before, when he was naught?**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Argument from First Creation**

The Qur'an answers the denial of resurrection with an argument that recurs throughout its pages and is here stated with perfect economy: *awalā yadhkuru al-insānu annā khalaqnāhu min qablu wa-lam yaku shayʾan* — "Does man not remember that We created him before, when he was naught?" The reasoning is simple and unassailable. The human being who finds resurrection incredible was himself, once, nothing — and God created him from that nothingness into existence. If God could perform the greater miracle of bringing a human being into being from absolute non-existence, then He can certainly perform the lesser miracle of bringing that same human being back from death. The first creation is standing, walking, breathing evidence of the divine power to create; and the power that created once can create again. The denial of resurrection is therefore a failure of memory and logic — a forgetting of one's own origin.

The argument is stated in several places in the Qur'an in similar terms: the idolaters' rhetorical questions about resurrection are answered by pointing to God's initial act of creation (17:49–51, 98–99; 36:78–79). The logic is that of a perfect syllogism: God created human beings from nothing (a fact attested by their very existence); the power that creates from nothing is not limited by death or dissolution; therefore God can raise the dead. Resurrection is not a greater feat than creation — it is, if anything, a lesser one, for raising a living form from a dead body is less miraculous than bringing existence from pure nothingness. The one who acknowledges that he was created from nothing has, in that acknowledgment, the proof that resurrection is possible.

**The Memory That Heals Denial**

The verse uses the word *yadhkuru* — "does he not remember?" — the same root (*dh-k-r*) that runs through the sūrah and the Qur'an. The denial of resurrection is, at root, a failure of remembrance — a forgetting of the act of creation that every human being embodies. The cure for the denial is the remembrance of one's origin: the recollection that one was once nothing and is now something, and that the power which accomplished that transformation is the power that can accomplish resurrection. The Qur'an repeatedly summons human beings to this remembrance — to look at their own existence and recognize in it the proof of the Creator's power. The man who remembers that he was created from nothing cannot easily maintain that death is the end; the very fact of his existence is a standing refutation of that claim.

**The Practical Import of the Argument**

For the believer, the argument from first creation is a source of confident hope; for the skeptic, it is an invitation to honesty. The one who denies resurrection must account for the fact of his own existence — for the miracle of his creation from nothing that he cannot deny, since he is alive to deny it. The one who affirms resurrection finds in his own being the perpetual evidence that the God who made him from nothing can make him again. The verse thus answers the mockery of verse 66 not with counter-mockery but with a quiet appeal to reason and memory: you ask how the dead can be raised; look at yourself. You were naught, and now you are. The God who did that can do it again. The argument from first creation is the Qur'an's perennial answer to the perennial question of whether death is the end — and it rests upon the undeniable fact that every living human being is himself the product of a creation from nothing, a standing witness to the power of the One who raises the dead.

---
## Sūrah Maryam 19:68

> **And by thy Lord, We shall surely gather them and the satans, and We shall surely bring them around Hell on their knees.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Oath That Seals the Gathering**

The verse opens with a solemn oath that gives the following declaration the force of certainty: *fa-wa-rabbika la-naḥshurannahum wa-al-Shayāṭīna* — "And by thy Lord, We shall surely gather them and the satans." The oath "by thy Lord" (*wa-rabbika*) — sworn by God upon Himself, or upon His Lordship as known to the Prophet — introduces an emphatic assurance. The pronoun "them" refers to the disbelievers who deny the resurrection and reject the message. God swears that they shall indeed be gathered — that the resurrection they mocked is certain, and that they will be assembled for judgment. The gathering includes not only the disbelievers themselves but "the satans" — the evil beings, both jinn and human (cf. 6:112), who inspired and accompanied them in their disbelief. The Qur'an mentions the disbelievers as allies of the satans in several verses (2:14; 6:121; 7:27, 30), for God "makes the satans the friends of those who believe not" (7:27). The gathering of the disbelievers with their satans underscores their inseparable companionship in evil: those who befriended the devils in this life will be gathered with them on the Day of Resurrection.

**"We Shall Surely Bring Them Around Hell on Their Knees"**

The verse then describes the manner of their being brought: *thumma la-nuḥḍirannahum ḥawla jahannama jithiyyan* — "then We shall surely bring them around Hell, on their knees." The image is one of abject helplessness. The word *jithiyy* describes those who are on their knees, a posture that the commentators explain reflects their state of fear, lowliness, and powerlessness in the face of impending judgment (R, Z). The disbelievers and their satans — who in this life stood proudly, boasting of their wealth and station (verse 73) — will be brought to the very brink of Hell on their knees, stripped of every pretense of power. The posture of kneeling is the physical expression of their total subjugation: those who refused to bow to God in worship, or who bowed to false gods, will be brought before the Fire helplessly, unable to stand, unable to flee, unable to resist.

**The Certainty That Answers Denial**

The oath "by thy Lord" is the Qur'an's emphatic response to the denial recorded in verse 66. The man who asked, mockingly, whether he would be brought forth alive after death is here told, with an oath by God Himself, that he will indeed be gathered — together with the satans who deceived him, and brought around Hell on his knees. The resurrection that the idolaters found incredible is declared certain with the strongest possible affirmation. The verse thus stands as a bridge from the argument of verse 67 (the logical proof of resurrection from first creation) to the eschatological descriptions that follow: the gathering is not only possible and certain, it will happen in a specific manner — the disbelievers and their satans herded to the brink of Hell in total helplessness. For the believer, the oath is a reassurance that divine justice is certain; for the denier, it is a warning that the resurrection he mocks will come to pass, and that he will meet it not in pride but on his knees.

---
## Sūrah Maryam 19:69

> **Then indeed We shall pluck out from every group whosoever among them was most insolent toward the Compassionate.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**Plucking Out from Every Group**

The verse continues the description of the Day of Judgment: *thumma la-nanziʿanna min kulli shīʿatin ayyuhum ashaddu ʿalā al-Raḥmāni ʿitiyyan* — "Then indeed We shall pluck out from every group (shīʿah) whosoever among them was most insolent ('itiyy) toward the Compassionate." The word *shīʿah* denotes a group, sect, or party — here understood as every religious community or nation (*ummah*) that went astray (Ṭ), those groups that divided over the truth (cf. 6:159). From each such group, God will "pluck out" (*nanziʿa*) its worst members — those who were most insolent toward the Compassionate. The verb *nanziʿa* conveys the forceful extraction or pulling out of something, as when a ring is pulled off a finger; God will single out, from among the vast assemblies of the erring, precisely those who were most guilty of insolence toward Him.

The word *ʿitiyy* (insolent, obstinate, refractory) is the same root used of extreme old age in verse 8, but here it describes moral insolence — a stubborn, refractory defiance toward God. Those "most insolent toward the Compassionate" are understood by the commentators to be the leaders of the erring communities — those who were not only astray themselves but who led others astray, and who are therefore deserving of greater punishment (R, Ṭs, Z; cf. 11:19–20). Others understand them as those most stubborn in their rejection of God and most defiant in disobedience (R, Ṭ), or those guilty of the worst offenses, such as idolatry and disbelief, who will enter the Fire first (Ṭ). Whatever the precise identification, the verse describes a divine sorting: from every group of the erring, the worst offenders will be singled out and brought to account.

**Justice Proportionate to Leadership and Guilt**

The verse embodies a principle of divine justice that runs throughout the Qur'an: the punishment is proportionate to the guilt, and the guilt is greater for those who not only erred but led others into error. The "leaders" of disbelief and misguidance — those most insolent toward the Compassionate — bear a heavier responsibility than their followers, for they corrupted not only their own souls but the souls of others. The verse thus serves as a severe warning to those in every age who use influence, eloquence, or authority to lead others away from God: they will be singled out on the Day of Judgment, plucked from the crowd of the erring, and held to account for the insolence that marked them. It also reassures the believer that divine justice is precise: God does not treat the stubborn leaders of error and their misled followers identically, but sorts each according to the true measure of their guilt.

**The Insolence Toward the Compassionate**

The phrase "most insolent toward the Compassionate" carries a poignant irony that resonates with the sūrah's dominant Name. Throughout this chapter, God has been invoked as al-Raḥmān, the Compassionate — the Merciful who grants sons to the barren, vindicates the pure, and promises the Garden to the repentant. And yet some of His creatures respond to this boundless mercy with insolence — with refractory defiance against the very One who sustains them. The verse exposes the ugliness of this ingratitude: to be insolent toward the Compassionate is to rebel against mercy itself, to return defiance for grace. The severity of the punishment that awaits the "most insolent" is proportionate to the enormity of their offense — they were insolent not toward a distant tyrant but toward the Compassionate, the Merciful whose mercy they spurned. The verse thus deepens the sūrah's meditation on the relationship between divine mercy and divine justice: the same Mercy that welcomes the repentant holds the insolent to account, and the insolence that most angers God is the insolence of those who receive His compassion and answer with defiance.

---
## Sūrah Maryam 19:70

> **Then We shall surely know those who most deserve to burn therein.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Knowledge That Perfectly Discerns**

The verse follows the divine sorting with a declaration that grounds the justice of the judgment: *thumma la-naḥnu aʿlamu bi-alladhīna hum awlā bihā ṣiliyyan* — "Then We shall surely know those who most deserve to burn therein." The verb "know" (*aʿlamu*) here is not a discovery of new information but an assertion of the perfection of divine knowledge. God already knows, with complete and infallible knowledge, who among the gathered deserve most to burn in the Fire. The commentators note the significance of this knowledge: it means the judgment will be perfectly just, for the One who judges knows every soul's true state — its deeds, its intentions, its degree of guilt — and will assign each precisely what it deserves. There is no error in the divine accounting, no mistake in the assignment of punishment, because the Judge's knowledge is total.

The phrase "most deserve to burn therein" (*awlā bihā ṣiliyyan*) ties the verse to the preceding one: those who were most insolent toward the Compassionate (verse 69) are precisely those who most deserve the Fire. The verse thus completes the logic of divine justice: the sorting is not arbitrary but perfectly calibrated to desert. The insolent leaders who were plucked from their groups will burn because they most deserve to burn; their punishment is not an act of divine caprice but the exact requirement of justice. The word *ṣiliyyan* (to burn, enter into the Fire) denotes the actual entering into and enduring of the Fire, and the verse assigns that fate to those who most deserve it.

**The Reassurance of Perfect Justice**

The verse carries a double message. To the insolent and the guilty, it is a warning: God knows precisely who deserves the Fire, and no one will escape through confusion, concealment, or error in the divine judgment. The most insolent, the leaders of error, will be identified with perfect knowledge and given exactly what they deserve. To the believer, the verse is a reassurance: because God's knowledge is perfect, His justice is perfect. The believer need not fear that the innocent will be punished with the guilty, that the sincere will be mistaken for the insolent, or that the righteous will be wronged. The One who judges knows all, and His judgment is therefore the perfection of justice. The verse thus holds together the two poles of the Qur'anic teaching on the Last Day: the certainty of punishment for those who deserve it, and the assurance of perfect fairness for those who do not.

**Knowledge as the Foundation of Judgment**

The verse's emphasis on divine knowledge as the foundation of judgment is theologically significant. Human courts are fallible because human judges know only what is presented to them — they judge by appearances, by evidence, by what they can see and hear. The divine court is infallible because its Judge knows everything — not only the outward deeds but the inward intentions, not only what was done but why it was done, not only the acts but the degrees of responsibility and guilt. The verse thus points to the deepest ground of the believer's confidence in the Hereafter: the Judge of the Day of Judgment is the all-knowing One, and because His knowledge is perfect, His justice is perfect. Those who most deserve to burn will burn, and no more; those who do not will not be touched. The scale of divine justice is weighed by a hand that never errs, because it is guided by an eye that never misses.

---
## Sūrah Maryam 19:71

> **And there is not one of you, but that he will approach it. It is, with thy Lord, a decree determined.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**Every Soul Approaching the Fire**

The verse introduces a teaching that has engaged the commentators profoundly: *wa-in minkum illā wāriduhā* — "And there is not one of you, but that he will approach (warida) it."
