The word *wārid* (from *w-r-d*) means to arrive at, to come upon, to approach — specifically, to come to a watering-place or to arrive at a place. Here it refers to the approach to the Fire of Hell. The verse states that every human being — *minkum*, "of you" — will approach the Fire. This includes both the righteous and the wicked, the believer and the disbeliever. The universality of the approach is affirmed without exception: *kāna ʿalā rabbika ḥatman maqḍiyyan* — "it is, with thy Lord, a decree determined (*maqḍiyy*)." The approach to the Fire is not a possibility but a decree, already determined and settled in the divine will.

The commentators wrestle with the meaning of this universal approach, for it seems at first to conflict with the promise that the righteous will enter the Garden. Several interpretations emerged. Some commentators state that when the righteous "pass through" the Fire, its flames will subside or become cool for them, so that they will not even be aware of having passed through them (Bḍ, Ṭ, Z). Others say the righteous will enter the Fire but then emerge from it because of their good deeds (Ṭ). Others assert that the Fire will not touch the righteous at all as they pass through Hell (Mw). Still others suggest that the righteous approaching the Fire refers to their suffering fevers and illnesses in this world (Ṭ, Z) — that the "approach" of the Fire is experienced in this life through the trials that purify believers. Some hold that the righteous are made to pass through the Fire so that they may know the extent of God's Bounty and Favor toward them, and can thus enjoy greater happiness in the Garden (Ṭs). Some commentators maintain that only the disbelievers are addressed in this verse (Ṭ).

**The Crossing and the Saving**

The most widely held understanding integrates verse 71 with verse 72, which follows. Verse 71 declares that everyone will approach the Fire; verse 72 declares that God will save the reverent and leave the wrongdoers therein. Read together, the two verses describe a crossing: all souls pass by or approach the Fire, but the reverent are saved from it while the wrongdoers are left within it. The approach of the Fire is thus a testing and a passage — every soul comes to it, but only the guilty remain in it. The commentators support this reading with a hadith in which the Prophet declared that none of those present with the Muslims at the battles of Badr (2/624) and Ḥudaybiyah (6/628) would enter the Fire. When the Prophet's wife Ḥafṣah asked him about verse 71, which indicates that all would approach the Fire, he responded by reciting verse 72, which states that God shall save those who are reverent (Ṭ). The saving of verse 72 is thus the complement of the approach of verse 71: all approach, but the reverent are saved.

**The Decree and the Hope**

The verse, taken together with verse 72, holds in tension two truths that the believer must hold together. First, the approach to the Fire is universal and decreed — no one is exempt from coming before the reality of judgment and punishment; the seriousness of the reckoning is not diminished for anyone. Second, the outcome is not uniform: the reverent are saved, the wrongdoers are left. The verse thus summons every soul to seriousness — the Fire is real, and every soul approaches it — while verse 72 summons every soul to hope — the reverent are saved. The believer who takes verse 71 seriously is moved to reverence; the believer who takes verse 72 to heart is moved to hope; and the two together produce the balanced spiritual life of one who approaches the Fire with the confidence of the reverent rather than the dread of the guilty.

---
## Sūrah Maryam 19:72

> **Then We shall save those who are reverent and leave the wrongdoers therein, on their knees.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Saving of the Reverent**

The verse resolves the tension introduced by verse 71: *thumma nunajjī alladhīna ittaqaw* — "Then We shall save those who are reverent (ittaqu)." After all souls have approached the Fire (verse 71), God will save the reverent from it. The verb *nunajjī* (to save, to deliver) is a strong form indicating a rescue or deliverance from danger. The reverent (*alladhīna ittaqaw* — those who possessed *taqwā*, the God-consciousness and protective mindfulness that runs throughout the Qur'an and this sūrah) will be delivered from the Fire that all approach. Their *taqwā* is precisely what saves them: the life of reverence, worship, and self-restraint that they lived in this world is what delivers them at the approach of the Fire. The saving is God's act — *We shall save* — but it is correlated with their own orientation of reverence, the *taqwā* that distinguished them from the wrongdoers.

The verse thus completes the teaching of verse 71 with its indispensable complement. Verse 71 established the universality of the approach to the Fire — the solemn reality that faces every soul. Verse 72 establishes the discriminating outcome — the reverent are saved, the wrongdoers are left. The two verses together present the full picture of the Last Day's testing: the Fire is approached by all, but it is not the fate of all. The reverent pass through to safety; their reverence, cultivated in this life, becomes their deliverance at the approach of the Fire.

**"And Leave the Wrongdoers Therein, on Their Knees"**

The verse then states the fate of the others: *wa-nadharu al-ẓālimīna fīhā jithiyyan* — "and We shall leave the wrongdoers therein, on their knees." The wrongdoers (*al-ẓālimūn*) — those who wronged themselves by rejecting the truth and following evil — are left in the Fire, and the description "on their knees" (*jithiyyan*) recalls verse 68, where the disbelievers were described as brought around Hell on their knees. The posture of kneeling is the mark of their helplessness and humiliation: the wrongdoers, who in this life stood proud and insolent (verse 69), are left in the Fire in a posture of abject powerlessness, unable to rise, unable to flee. The image is one of total degradation — the pride that marked their earthly life gives way to the crouching helplessness of their eternal punishment.

**The Two Outcomes of the Approach**

The verse, with verse 71, presents the two possible outcomes of every human approach to the Fire. The reverent are saved because their lives were lived in the awareness of God and the guarding of their souls; the wrongdoers are left because they persisted in wrongdoing and insolence toward the Compassionate. The difference between the two outcomes is not arbitrary but is the direct consequence of the lives lived in this world. The verse thus summons every reader to a choice: to live with the *taqwā* that delivers, or to live with the wrongdoing that damns. The approach to the Fire is universal and unavoidable; but whether one is saved from it or left within it depends on the orientation of one's life — whether one is among *alladhīna ittaqaw*, the reverent whom God saves, or among *al-ẓālimīn*, the wrongdoers whom He leaves on their knees.

---
## Sūrah Maryam 19:73

> **And when Our signs are recited unto them as clear proofs, those who disbelieve say unto those who believe, "Which of the two groups is better in station or fairer in company?"**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Recitation of the Clear Proofs**

The verse returns to the confrontation between the believers and the disbelievers in the Makkan context: *wa-idhā tutlā ʿalayhim āyātunā bayyinātun* — "And when Our signs are recited unto them as clear proofs (bayyināt)." The "signs" (*āyāt*) are the verses of the Qur'an and the clear proofs (*bayyināt*) of God's truth — the revelations that demonstrate the reality of God, resurrection, and judgment. The scene is set: whenever the clear signs are recited to the disbelievers, they do not respond with belief or reflection but with a specific form of mockery. The verse captures the characteristic response of the Makkan idolaters to the Qur'anic message — a response measured not by truth but by worldly advantage.

The response is recorded: *qāla alladhīna kafarū lilladhīna āmanū ayyu al-farīqayni khayrun maqāman wa-aḥsanu nadiyyan* — "those who disbelieve say unto those who believe, 'Which of the two groups is better in station (maqām) or fairer in company (nadiyy)?'" The disbelievers challenge the believers with a comparison based entirely on worldly criteria. "Station" (*maqām*) refers to their superior residences and living conditions; "company" (*nadiyy*) refers to their wealthier and more socially prominent friends and associates (Ṭ, Ṭs). The Makkan idolaters — who controlled the trade, the wealth, and the social prestige of the city — point to their own worldly superiority over the early Muslims, who were often poor, low-status, and socially marginalized. Their question implies that the favor of God must surely belong to those whom the world favors: since we are better off, we must be in the right.

**The False Measure of Truth**

The verse exposes the idolaters' fundamental error: they measure the truth of a religion by worldly success. Their implicit logic is that God would surely bless the group He favors with prosperity, prominence, and good company — and since the disbelievers enjoyed these, they must be the favored group. The early Muslims, by contrast, were often the poor, the weak, the slaves, and the marginalized — the very people that the pagan Arab aristocracy despised. The disbelievers' question is thus a challenge built on a false premise: that earthly station and company are reliable indicators of divine favor and truth. The verse records this challenge so that it can be answered — and the following verses will demolish the premise by pointing to the fate of earlier generations who were fairer in furnishings and appearance than the Makkans, yet were destroyed.

**The Measure That Endures**

The verse's relevance extends far beyond its Makkan setting, for the tendency to measure truth by worldly success is perennial. In every age, human beings are tempted to judge religions, causes, and people by their outward prosperity — to assume that the powerful, the wealthy, and the socially prominent must be in the right, and that the poor, the weak, and the marginalized must be in the wrong. The Qur'an repeatedly challenges this assumption, insisting that worldly station is no measure of divine favor: God may test the righteous with poverty and the wicked with prosperity, and the true measure of a person or a cause is not earthly success but righteousness and truth. The verse records the idolaters' boast so that the believer may recognize it whenever it appears — and remember that the criterion of truth is not the size of one's estate or the prominence of one's company, but the reality of one's standing with God.

---
## Sūrah Maryam 19:74

> **How many a generation We destroyed before them who were fairer in [their] furnishings and in outward appearance.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Destruction of the Fairest Generations**

The Qur'an answers the idolaters' boast of worldly superiority with a pointed historical reminder: *wa-kam ahlaknā qablahum min qarnin hum aḥsanu athāthan wa-riʾyan* — "And how many a generation We destroyed before them who were fairer in furnishings (athāth) and in outward appearance (riʾy)!" The exclamation "how many" (*kam*) is designed to overwhelm: countless generations preceded the Makkans, and many of them were far more magnificent than the Makkans could imagine. The word *athāth* denotes furnishings, goods, and material possessions; *riʾy* denotes outward appearance, splendor, and show. The destroyed generations were fairer than the Makkans in both — they possessed finer furnishings and presented a more splendid outward appearance — and yet they were destroyed for their wrongdoing. The verse thus turns the idolaters' boast against them: if superior wealth and appearance had been a protection, the generations that surpassed you in both would not have been destroyed.

The theme of the destruction of previous generations is one of the most recurrent in the Qur'an (6:6; 7:4; 17:17; 19:98; 21:11; 22:45; 28:58; 38:3; 44:37; 47:13; 50:36), and it is almost always directed as a warning to the Makkan Quraysh. The verse argues by historical induction: again and again, peoples with greater wealth, more imposing cities, and more splendid civilizations than the Makkans were destroyed for their rejection of God's messengers. The pattern is consistent and well attested — the ruins of earlier civilizations were visible to the Arabs in their desert travels, silent monuments to the fate of those who trusted in their earthly splendor rather than in God. The Makkans, who boasted of their station and company, are warned that the very criteria of their boast are the criteria by which earlier peoples were judged and found wanting.

**The Warning to the Boastful**

The verse's rhetorical force lies in its reversal of the idolaters' criterion. The disbelievers asked which of the two groups was better in station and company (verse 73), implying that worldly superiority indicated divine favor. The verse replies: superiority in station and company is precisely the measure that did not save the generations before you. The peoples who had more than you were destroyed; your wealth and appearance will no more protect you than theirs did. The verse thus dismantles the false criterion at its root: worldly prosperity is not a sign of divine favor but a test, and those who trust in it rather than in God are precisely those whom God destroys. The boasting of the Makkans is answered by the ruins of the past — the silent evidence that earthly splendor is no shield against divine justice.

**The Lesson for Every Age of Prosperity**

The verse holds a warning for every age and every society that equates prosperity with virtue. Civilizations that measure their worth by their wealth, their buildings, their technology, and their outward splendor — while neglecting the worship of God and justice toward others — repeat the error of the destroyed generations. The verse invites every prosperous people to look at the ruins of the past and ask whether the splendor they trust in is any more durable than the splendor of those who were destroyed. The criterion that endures is not the fineness of furnishings or the fairness of appearance but righteousness — the worship of God and the doing of justice. The verse's reminder — "how many a generation We destroyed before them who were fairer in furnishings and in outward appearance" — is a standing warning that no amount of earthly magnificence can protect a people from the consequences of abandoning God, and an invitation to build upon the only foundation that does not crumble: faith and righteousness.

---
## Sūrah Maryam 19:75

> **Say, "Whosoever is in error, the Compassionate will extend his term till, when they see that which they have been promised, be it the punishment or the Hour, they will know whose position is worse, and whose host is weaker."**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Extension of the Erring**

The verse instructs the Prophet to respond to the idolaters' boast with a different measure of things: *qul man kāna fī al-ḍalālati fa-l-yamdud lahu al-Raḥmānu maddan* — "Say: 'Whosoever is in error, the Compassionate will extend for him an extension.'" The commentators understand the "extension" (*madd*) in two ways, and the ambiguity is instructive. The apparent meaning is that God gives the erring a respite, prolonging their lives and their terms. But the verse may also be read as a prayer or declaration of what will happen: "Say, 'Whosoever is in error, let the Compassionate extend for him an extension'" — that is, let the erring be given rope, allowed to continue in their error until its consequences catch up with them. Either way, the point is that the extension of the erring is not necessarily a blessing.

The Qur'an warns repeatedly that the prolonging of the lives of the disbelievers may not be a sign of favor. Although God may give some long life as an opportunity to reflect and return (35:37), here, as in many verses (2:15; 3:178; 6:110; 7:182–83), the prolonging of the lives of the obstinate may be an opportunity for them to wander even farther astray and increase in sin. Moreover, the respite will only make their being seized in the end all the more sudden and unexpected (13:32; 22:44). The verse thus inverts the idolaters' logic once more: they point to their prosperity and long lives as signs of favor; the Qur'an warns that the very prolonging of their term may be the divine indulgence that allows them to accumulate the guilt that will overwhelm them.

**"Till They See That Which They Have Been Promised"**

The verse then describes the point at which the true measure of the two groups will become clear: *ḥattā idhā raʾaw mā yūʿadūn immā al-ʿadhāba wa-immā al-Sāʿata* — "till, when they see that which they have been promised — be it the punishment (al-ʿadhāb) or the Hour (al-Sāʿah) — they will know whose position is worse, and whose host is weaker." The erring are allowed their extension until the moment they witness the fulfillment of the promise: either the earthly punishment that destroys their community or the universal onset of the Hour and the Last Day. At that point — when the punishment descends or the Hour arrives — the truth of the matter will become undeniable, and they will know, with a knowledge that is too late, "whose position is worse, and whose host is weaker."

The verse directly answers the idolaters' boast of verse 73. They had asked which group was better in station and fairer in company, measuring by the present. The verse replies that the true comparison will be made at the end: when the punishment or the Hour comes, it will be seen whose position is truly worse and whose host is truly weaker. The boasting of the disbelievers is measured against their own future humiliation; the apparent superiority of their station and company in this life will be shown to be nothing when the reality they were promised arrives. The reversal is complete: the group that seemed stronger and better-placed in this world will be revealed, at the appointed time, as the weaker and the worse.

**The Measure of the End**

The verse's teaching is a profound corrective to the human tendency to measure by the present. The idolaters measured the two groups by their present station and company; the Qur'an insists that the true measure is the end — what happens when the punishment or the Hour arrives. In this life, the appearance of things is often deceptive: the wrongdoer may prosper, the believer may suffer, the false may flourish, and the true may be marginalized. The verse summons the believer to a longer view — to measure by the outcome rather than the present appearance, by the Hour rather than the hour. Those who are in error may be given an extension, but the extension only postpones the moment when their position will be exposed as worse and their host as weaker. And the believer, who may appear weaker and worse-placed in this world, is invited to trust that the true reckoning is yet to come — when the Compassionate, who has extended the erring their term, will reveal whose position is truly worse and whose host is truly weaker.

---
## Sūrah Maryam 19:76

> **God increases in guidance those who are rightly guided. And that which endures—righteous deeds—are better in reward with thy Lord, and better in return.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**Guidance That Increases Guidance**

The verse introduces a principle of the divine economy that has profound implications for understanding free will and divine sovereignty: *wa-yazīdu Allāhu alladhīna ihtadaw hudan* — "And God increases in guidance those who are rightly guided." The idea that God increases the guidance of those who have already accepted guidance is repeated or implied elsewhere (18:13; 47:17), and it is closely related to the Qur'an's statements that God increases in faith those who believe (9:124; 48:4; 74:31), while for those in whose hearts is a disease, He increases them in disease (2:10). The verse points to a dynamic relationship between human choice and divine assistance: the one who chooses guidance and acts upon it is given more guidance; the guidance already received becomes the seed of further guidance.

The commentators explain the operation of this increase in several ways. God may increase people in guidance by helping them in acts of obedience and granting them success in their efforts to please Him through righteous deeds (Ṭs, Z), or by bestowing various favors that lead them toward virtue. The increase may also mean that God gives those who are guided greater certainty and resolve regarding the guidance they have already received, and continues to increase them in certitude by revealing new signs and verses (Q). Some commentators read the verse historically, as referring to God's guiding the believers through successive revelations during the Prophet's lifetime — including the abrogation of earlier rulings by later ones as the needs of the community evolved (Ṭ, Ṭs; cf. 2:106).

**The Nexus of Divine and Human Will**

The verse embodies a subtle but crucial teaching about the relationship between God's will and human effort. It is a law of the moral universe that the commentators repeatedly draw out: those who respond to the guidance they have been given are given more; those who respond to light receive more light, and those who turn from it are left to increasing darkness. The same principle appears in the saying that whoever acts upon what he knows, God teaches him what he does not know, and in the Qur'an's statement that God guides those who strive in His way (29:69). The guidance is thus not a static possession but a living, growing reality that increases as it is used, like a muscle that strengthens with exercise or a light that brightens the more it is followed.

This teaching has a direct practical application. The believer who acts upon the guidance of the Qur'an and the example of the Prophet — who prays, gives charity, restrains desire, and seeks knowledge — finds that guidance opening before him, that clarity increasing, that the path becoming easier to discern. Conversely, the one who neglects the guidance he has received finds it receding, obscured by the very neglect. The verse is thus a summons to act upon what one knows, with the assurance that God will increase the knowledge and guidance of those who do. The first step is the believer's; the increase is God's gift; and the two cooperate in the growth of the rightly guided.

**"Righteous Deeds: Better in Reward and Better in Return"**

The verse then sets the enduring value of righteous deeds against the perishable goods of this world: *wa-al-bāqiyātu al-ṣāliḥātu khayrun ʿinda rabbika thawāban wa-khayrun maraddan* — "And that which endures — righteous deeds (al-bāqiyāt al-ṣāliḥāt) — are better in reward with thy Lord, and better in return." The phrase *al-bāqiyāt al-ṣāliḥāt* — literally, "the enduring, righteous deeds" — designates the good works that endure and outlast the perishable goods of this life. The commentators gloss it variously as the obligatory prayers, or the supererogatory acts of worship and the remembrance of God, or generally all righteous deeds. Whatever the precise referent, the point is that righteous deeds *endure*: they survive death, outlast the world, and return to their doer on the Day of Judgment, whereas the station and company of which the idolaters boasted (verse 73) perish with the world.

The verse directly answers the idolaters' boast. They measured superiority by station and company; the Qur'an measures by what endures. The righteous deeds of the believer are better in reward with God and better in return than all the worldly goods of the idolaters, because they lead to reward in both this world and the next (Ṭs). The theme recurs throughout the Qur'an (18:46; 28:60; 42:36): the things of this world are perishable, while righteous deeds are the enduring treasure. The verse thus invites the believer to invest in what lasts — to build a treasure of righteous deeds that will be better in reward with the Lord and better in return than any earthly station, any worldly company, any perishable possession.

---
## Sūrah Maryam 19:77

> **Hast thou not considered the one who disbelieves in Our signs, and says, "I shall be given wealth and children."**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Portrait of the Arrogant Disbeliever**

The verse turns to a specific portrait of arrogance and false confidence: *a-faraʾayta alladhī kafara bi-āyātinā wa-qāla la-ūtayanna mālan wa-waladan* — "Hast thou not considered the one who disbelieves in Our signs, and says, 'I shall certainly be given wealth and children.'" The verse opens with the Qur'anic formula *a-faraʾayta* — "hast thou not considered?" — which introduces a pointed reflection. The person described disbelieves in God's signs (the verses and proofs of His power, including the resurrection) and yet claims, with complete confidence, that he will be given wealth and children — that is, that he will be rewarded in the Hereafter with the good things of this world, or that his present wealth and children will follow him into the next life. His confidence in future reward is based not on faith but on presumption — he assumes that God owes him the good things he expects, even while he disbelieves in the signs that would make such reward meaningful.

The commentators record the occasion of this verse's revelation. It was reportedly revealed in response to a Makkan leader and idolater, al-ʿĀṣ ibn Wāʾil (the father of ʿAmr ibn al-ʿĀṣ), who owed money to one of the Companions of the Prophet. When resurrection was mentioned to him in connection with a request to repay his debt, al-ʿĀṣ mockingly replied that if he were indeed resurrected, he would be resurrected possessing wealth and children, and so would be able to repay his debt then (Ṭ, Ṭs, W). Some say the disbeliever was al-Walīd ibn al-Mughīrah (Ṭs). The statement might also be read as conveying an attitude common among the disbelievers and thus applying generally: the assumption that the good things of this world will continue into the Hereafter, and that one's present wealth and children are guarantees of future reward.

**The Confidence Built on Presumption**

The verse exposes the logical and moral bankruptcy of this confidence. The disbeliever denies the signs of God — the very evidence that there is a Hereafter at all — and yet claims certainty about his reward in that Hereafter. His confidence is entirely without foundation: he expects to be rewarded by a God whose signs he rejects, for a life lived in disbelief, in a Hereafter he mocks. The verse sets up the rhetorical question of the following verse: how can such a person claim knowledge of the Unseen or a pact with God? The presumption of the arrogant disbeliever is shown to be hollow — a confidence built on nothing but wishful thinking and the assumption that God's favor is owed to him because of his worldly standing. The believer, by contrast, bases hope on faith, on the signs of God, and on righteous deeds — not on the presumption that wealth and children in this world guarantee reward in the next.

**The Perennial Arrogance of the Presumptuous**

The portrait in this verse is perennial. In every age there are those who disbelieve in God's signs yet presume upon His favor — who assume that their wealth, their children, their worldly success guarantee them a blessed future, while they reject the very realities that would give such a future meaning. The verse exposes the contradiction at the heart of this presumption: it claims the rewards of faith while refusing the faith itself. The believer is invited to recognize and avoid this error — to build hope not on worldly presumption but on genuine faith and righteous deeds, and to remember that wealth and children in this world are no guarantee of anything in the next. The verse also invites reflection on the true foundation of hope: not the assumption that one is owed reward, but the humble faith in the God whose signs point to a Hereafter in which He alone, and no worldly presumption, determines the outcome.

---
## Sūrah Maryam 19:78

> **Has he penetrated the Unseen, or made a pact with the Compassionate?**

**Expanded Commentary**

**Two Rhetorical Questions That Expose the Claim**

The verse challenges the arrogant claim of verse 77 with two devastating rhetorical questions: *a-ṭṭalaʿa al-ghayba am ittakhadha ʿinda al-Raḥmāni ʿahdan* — "Has he penetrated the Unseen (al-ghayb), or made a pact (ʿahd) with the Compassionate?" The two questions offer the only possible grounds on which the disbeliever could claim certainty about his reward in the Hereafter — and both are shown to be impossible. Either he has somehow gained access to the Unseen, seeing for himself the reality of the Hereafter and his own reward in it; or he has made a pact with God guaranteeing that reward. The verse asks, in effect: on what basis do you claim this? Have you seen the Unseen? Has God promised you this? Since neither is possible for a disbeliever, his claim is exposed as pure presumption.

The word *al-ghayb* (the Unseen) refers to all that lies beyond ordinary human knowledge — the realities of the next world, the hidden knowledge that belongs to God alone. The commentators explain that the question is whether the disbeliever has somehow gained knowledge of the Unseen, specifically of his own fate in the Hereafter. The Qur'an is emphatic that knowledge of the Unseen belongs to God alone: "God alone possesses the keys of the Unseen; none knows them but He" (6:59), and "None in the heavens or on the earth know the Unseen, save God" (27:65). The disbeliever who claims certainty about his reward in the Hereafter must be claiming access to the Unseen — a claim that only a prophet could make, and only by revelation from God, neither of which applies to him.

**"Or Made a Pact with the Compassionate"**

The second question offers the alternative ground: has he made a pact (*ʿahd*) with the Compassionate? The commentators explain that the "pact" would be an agreement or covenant with God guaranteeing his reward — either a pact of belief and obedience that would merit such reward (Ṭ) or a specific promise of entry into the Garden (Ṭs). The question implies that such a pact can only be made through faith and righteous deeds — through the covenant that God makes with the believers. The disbeliever, who rejects God's signs, has made no such pact; he has not believed, not obeyed, not entered into the covenant that alone could guarantee reward. His claim to be given wealth and children in the Hereafter therefore rests on nothing.

The verse's rhetorical force lies in the impossibility of both alternatives for the disbeliever. He cannot have penetrated the Unseen, for that knowledge belongs to God alone and is only granted through revelation to prophets — which he has rejected. He cannot have made a pact with the Compassionate, for such a pact is made through faith and obedience, which he has refused. Therefore his confident claim of future reward is baseless — built on nothing but presumption. The verse thus completes the demolition of the arrogant disbeliever's confidence begun in verse 77, and it does so by forcing the question of the true foundation of hope: hope can only rest on knowledge (which comes from God) or on a covenant (which is made through faith) — and the disbeliever has neither.

**The Foundation of True Hope**

The verse's questions have a constructive as well as a destructive purpose. By exposing the baselessness of the presumptuous claim, it points the believer to the true foundations of hope. The believer's hope in the Hereafter rests on the two things the disbeliever lacks: knowledge of the Unseen as revealed by God through His messengers, and the pact that God has made with those who believe and work righteousness. The believer does not presume upon God's favor; he relies on the revealed promises of God and fulfills the conditions of the covenant — faith and obedience. The verse thus contrasts two kinds of confidence: the hollow presumption of the disbeliever, who claims reward without the grounds for it, and the grounded hope of the believer, who builds upon the revelation of the Unseen and the covenant with the Compassionate. The questions that expose the one point the other to its true foundation.

---
## Sūrah Maryam 19:79

> **Nay, but We shall record that which he says, and We shall prolong for him the punishment.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Rejection of the Claim**

The verse answers the rhetorical questions of verse 78 with a decisive negative: *kallā* — "Nay!" The word *kallā* is a strong particle of negation and rebuke, used to reject and dismiss a claim. Here it negates both possibilities raised in verse 78: the disbeliever has neither penetrated the Unseen nor made a pact with the Compassionate (Ṭ). His claim to be given wealth and children in the Hereafter is therefore rejected outright. The confident presumption of verse 77 is swept aside by the single word *kallā* — a word that carries the force of "by no means!" The verse then states what will actually happen to the disbeliever and his claim, replacing his false confidence with the true consequence of his words.

**"We Shall Record That Which He Says"**

The first consequence is that God will record what the disbeliever says: *sa-naktubu mā yaqūlu* — "We shall record that which he says." The verse points to the recording of deeds and words that the Qur'an describes as a central feature of divine accountability. Every word spoken, every claim made, every piece of mockery uttered against the signs of God, is recorded — preserved in the divine account that will be presented on the Day of Judgment. The disbeliever's mocking claim that he will be given wealth and children, his denial of the signs, his presumptuous speech — all of it is written down and will be brought against him. The Qur'an describes the recording of human deeds in many places (e.g., 45:28–29; 50:17–18), and the verse here emphasizes that even speech — the very words of arrogance and denial — is recorded and will be held to account.

**"We Shall Prolong for Him the Punishment"**

The second consequence follows from the first: *wa-namuddu lahu mina al-ʿadhābi maddan* — "and We shall prolong for him the punishment (a prolonging)." The word *madd* (prolonging, extension) echoes the "extension" (*madd*) that the Compassionate grants the erring in verse 75. But here the prolonging is of punishment, not of life: God will prolong the punishment of the disbeliever (Ṭ). The commentators explain that the punishment is prolonged because the disbeliever has compounded his disbelief with lies and vain claims (Ṭ) — the arrogance and mockery of verses 77–78 add to his guilt and thus to his punishment. The claim that was meant to secure him reward secures him instead an extended punishment: every mocking word, every presumptuous boast, is recorded and contributes to the length of his torment.

**The Reversal of Presumption**

The verse completes a powerful reversal. The disbeliever presumed that he would be given wealth and children in the Hereafter — an expectation of reward. The verse answers that he will instead be given punishment, prolonged because of his presumption and his lies. His confidence in future reward is replaced by the certainty of future punishment; his boastful claim to be granted the good things of the next life is answered by the record of his words and the prolonging of his torment. The verse thus warns against the specific sin of presumptuous arrogance — of claiming God's favor while disbelieving in His signs and mocking His messengers. Such presumption does not earn reward; it compounds guilt. The believer is warned to hope in God through faith and righteous deeds, not to presume upon His favor through arrogance and denial — for the presumptuous claim is recorded, and its reward is the prolonging of punishment, not the granting of wealth and children.

---
## Sūrah Maryam 19:80

> **And We shall inherit from him that which he claims, and he will come unto Us alone.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Inheritance of That Which He Claims**

The verse continues the description of the disbeliever's end: *wa-narithuhu mā yaqūlu* — "And We shall inherit from him that which he says (claims)." The verb *narithu* (to inherit) recalls verse 40 and the divine Name *al-Wārith*, the Inheritor. The "that which he claims" refers to the wealth and children the disbeliever boasted he would be given — the good things of this world upon which he built his presumption. The verse declares that God will "inherit" this from him: his wealth and his children (Ṭ, Ṭs), the very possessions in which he trusted, will be taken from him and returned to their true Owner. He will be stripped, in death and judgment, of everything he had and everything he expected. The God who is the Inheritor of all things will inherit from the disbeliever precisely what he most relied upon.

The reversal is stark and complete. The disbeliever claimed that he would be given wealth and children in the Hereafter; the verse answers that he will instead be deprived of his wealth and children in this world and the next. The possessions he trusted to secure his future will not follow him; they will be inherited by God, who owns them all along. The children he boasted of will not intercede for him or save him; they too will be inherited, and he will face his Lord stripped of all that gave him confidence. The verse thus demolishes the very foundation of the disbeliever's presumption — the wealth and children that seemed to guarantee his blessed future are shown to be the perishable goods that he will lose entirely.

**"He Will Come Unto Us Alone"**

The verse closes with the ultimate stripping: *wa-yaʾtīnā fardan* — "and he will come unto Us alone." The word *fardan* (alone, solitary) describes the condition of the human being before God on the Day of Judgment — alone, stripped of all company, all possessions, all helpers. The disbeliever, who in this life was surrounded by his wealth and his children, who prided himself on his station and company (verse 73), will come unto God utterly alone — devoid of the wealth and children that were his sources of honor and security in this world (Ṭ, Ṭs). The Qur'an describes this universal aloneness in many places (cf. 6:94; 19:95), emphasizing that on the Day of Resurrection every soul comes to God alone, without the possessions, family, or helpers that accompanied it through life.

**The Stripping of Every False Support**

The verse presents the complete reversal of the disbeliever's confidence. Everything he relied upon — his wealth, his children, his station, his company — will be stripped from him, inherited by God, and he will stand before his Lord alone. The image of arriving alone (*fardan*) is one of the most sobering in the Qur'an's eschatological teaching: it is the moment when every human being discovers that the supports upon which they built their lives were temporary, and that they face God with nothing but what they themselves are. The verse thus summons the believer to a radical honesty about what truly endures: not wealth, not children, not station — but the state of one's soul before God. The one who lives for the perishable goods of this world will find them inherited by God and will stand alone before Him; the one who lives for God, building a treasure of faith and righteous deeds, will find that what alone survives the stripping is precisely the relationship with God that the disbeliever neglected.

---
## Sūrah Maryam 19:81

> **And they have taken gods apart from God, that they might be a strength for them.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Gods Taken as a Source of Strength**

The verse turns from the individual portrait of the presumptuous disbeliever to the general practice of the idolaters: *wa-ittakhadhū min dūni Allāhi ālihatan li-yakūnū lahum ʿizzan* — "And they have taken gods apart from God, that they might be a strength (ʿizz) for them." The word *ʿizz* denotes might, strength, honor, and support. The idolaters of Makkah took gods besides God — their idols, their false deities — hoping that these would be a *strength* for them. The commentators explain what they hoped for from these gods: that they would defend them against or deliver them from God's punishment, or protect them from any harm or evil God might bring upon them (Ṭ), or that they would be their intercessors with God (Ṭs). The idols were, in the minds of their worshippers, sources of power and protection — beings to whom they could turn for help, security, and intercession.

The verse exposes the psychology of idolatry: human beings take false gods because they want strength, protection, and support. The idolater does not worship the idol for its own sake but for what it can do for him — he wants an ally, a protector, a source of *ʿizz* to whom he can turn in need. The tragedy is that the objects chosen for this role are utterly incapable of fulfilling it — they are deaf, blind, and powerless (as Abraham argued in verse 42), and they cannot be a strength for anyone. The search for strength through false gods is a search that can never succeed, because the gods are nothing. Yet the verse records that human beings continue to make this error — taking false supports in place of the true source of strength, which is God alone.

**The Misplaced Search for Security**

The verse's relevance extends to every age and every form of false reliance. Human beings are perpetually seeking *ʿizz* — strength, security, protection — and perpetually inclined to seek it from sources other than God: from wealth, from power, from status, from other human beings, from ideologies and institutions, from the many "gods apart from God" that each age invents. The verse exposes the futility of this search: whatever is taken as a support in place of God cannot ultimately be a strength, because everything other than God is ultimately powerless before God. The true source of *ʿizz* — the might and honor that endure — belongs to God alone (cf. 4:139; 35:10: "All might belongs to God"). The verse thus invites the believer to examine the objects of reliance and to place trust only where trust can truly rest — in the God who alone is the source of all strength and honor.

**The Strength That Cannot Save**

The verse sets up the reversal that the following verse will deliver. The idolaters took gods apart from God hoping they would be a *strength* for them — defenders and intercessors. The next verse will reveal the bitter irony of this hope: far from being a strength to their worshippers, the false gods will disavow their worshippers and become their opponents. The strength the idolaters sought from their gods will prove to be nothing, and the gods themselves will turn against them. The verse thus records the misplaced hope so that the believer may learn where true strength lies — not in the false gods that cannot help, but in God alone, who is the true source of *ʿizz*, the Defender of those who rely upon Him, and the One who can truly save.

---
## Sūrah Maryam 19:82

> **Nay, but they will disavow their worship, and they will become an opponent unto them.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Reversal of the Hope for Strength**

The verse delivers the crushing reversal of the idolaters' hope: *kallā sa-yakfurūna bi-ʿibādatihim wa-yakūnūna ʿalayhim ḍiddan* — "Nay, but they will disavow their worship, and they will become an opponent (ḍidd) unto them." The particle *kallā* ("Nay!") rejects and dismisses the expectation that the false gods will be a strength to their worshippers. Instead of helping those who worshipped them, the false gods will *disavow their worship* — they will deny and repudiate the worship that was offered to them. The verb *sa-yakfurūna* (they will disbelieve in / disavow) uses the same root as *kufr* (disbelief, denial); the false gods will "disbelieve in" or deny the worship their devotees offered, refusing to acknowledge it or be associated with it. On the Day of Judgment, the idols will disown their worshippers entirely.

The commentators explain that the false gods will disavow their worship either because they themselves will be brought to judgment and will deny that their worshippers truly served them, or because the whole basis of the worship — the false belief that the idols could help — will be exposed as groundless. Either way, the reversal is complete: the gods that were taken as a source of strength (*ʿizz*, verse 81) will become the accusers and opponents of their former worshippers. The Qur'an describes this scene in several places (2:166–67; 6:94; 7:37; 28:63–64; 40:74, 84), always with the same bitter irony: those who worshipped false gods in the hope of help will find those very gods turning against them on the Day of Judgment.

**The Gods Become Opponents**

The verse adds that the false gods will *become an opponent (ḍidd) unto them* — not merely indifferent to their worshippers but actively opposed to them. On the Day of Judgment, the idols and false deities will not only refuse to help those who worshipped them; they will testify against them, exposing the futility of their worship and confirming their error. The commentators note an alternate reading: the verse may mean that the idolaters themselves will disavow and oppose their idols on the Day of Judgment, in an attempt to avoid punishment (Ṭs) — but too late to undo the worship they offered. Whichever reading one adopts, the result is the same: the hoped-for allies become adversaries, and the idolaters are left with neither gods nor help, facing the judgment alone.

**The End of Every False Reliance**

The verse's teaching has a universal application. Whatever human beings take as a support in place of God — the wealth, the power, the status, the false gods of every age — will ultimately fail them. Far from being a strength, the false object of reliance will, at the end, expose and oppose its devotee. The wealth that was trusted will be revealed as powerless; the power that was relied upon will be shown to be nothing; the false deity that promised help will disavow its worshipper and turn against him. The verse thus completes the demolition of misplaced reliance begun in verse 81, and it points the believer to the only reliance that does not fail: trust in God alone. The One who is taken as a true Support is never disavowed and never becomes an opponent; He is the Defender of those who rely on Him, and the only source of *ʿizz* that endures. The verse's stark image of the gods becoming opponents is a warning against every false reliance and an invitation to place one's trust where alone it is safe — in God.

---
## Sūrah Maryam 19:83

> **Hast thou not considered how We unleash the satans on the disbelievers, to incite them cunningly?**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Unleashing of the Satans**

The verse invites reflection on a mysterious aspect of the divine governance of the world: *a-lam tara annā arsalnā al-Shayāṭīna ʿalā al-kāfirīna taʾuzzuhum azzan* — "Hast thou not considered how We unleash (arsalnā) the satans upon the disbelievers, inciting them (taʾuzzuhum) cunningly?" The verse states that God "sends" or "unleashes" the satans upon the disbelievers. The commentators explain this in a way that preserves both divine sovereignty and human responsibility. Some say it means simply that God does not hold the satans back from the disbelievers (Z) — that as a consequence of their disbelief, God permits the satans to assail them. Others say it refers to God's giving the satans authority over the disbelievers (R). The unleashing of the satans is thus not an arbitrary divine action but the natural consequence of the disbelievers' own rejection of God: by turning from God, they open themselves to the influence of the evil beings whom God allows to work upon them.

The verse raises the perennial question of how evil operates within a world governed by God. The Qur'an's answer is that the satans do not act independently of God's permission; they are "unleashed" upon the disbelievers as part of the divine order. The disbelievers who rejected God's guidance are not protected from the satans who seek to lead them further astray; having chosen to turn from God, they are left to the influence of those who will accelerate their fall. The verse thus preserves divine sovereignty (nothing happens without God's permission) while affirming human responsibility (the disbelievers' own choice opened the door to the satans' influence).

**"To Incite Them Cunningly"**

The purpose or effect of the satans' activity is described: *taʾuzzuhum azzan* — "inciting them cunningly." The verb *taʾuzzu*, from a root conveying violent agitation and stirring, describes the satans' constant prodding and incitement of the disbelievers — stirring them up, agitating them, driving them on. The commentators understand this as the satans' misleading, tempting, and seducing of the disbelievers into disobedience to God (R, Ṭ, Z) — the endless whispering and provocation by which the satans incite their victims to sin. The verse recalls God's words to Iblīs in 17:64: "Incite (astafziz) whomsoever thou canst among them with thy voice" (Q, R) — the standing permission granted to Satan to tempt and incite human beings, which he exercises most effectively upon those who have already turned from God.

**The Consequence of Turning from God**

The verse's teaching is sobering and profound. Those who disbelieve do not simply drift in a neutral state; they are assailed by the satans, who are unleashed upon them as a consequence of their disbelief and who incite them ever further into error. The verse thus describes a kind of spiritual law: the further one turns from God, the more one is exposed to the influence of those who lead away from God; the more one rejects guidance, the more the whisperers of evil gain access to one's heart. The verse stands as a warning to every soul about the stakes of spiritual choice — turning from God is not merely a passive loss but an active exposure to the satans' incitement. Conversely, it implies, the one who turns to God and remains in His remembrance is protected from the satans' full assault — for the satans are unleashed upon the *disbelievers*, and the believer who seeks refuge in God is not left to them. The verse thus summons the believer to the very thing the disbelievers have abandoned: the remembrance and worship of God that keeps the satans at bay.

---
## Sūrah Maryam 19:84

> **So hasten not against them; verily We are counting for them a number.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Command Not to Hasten**

The verse addresses the Prophet and the believers, who might naturally wish for the swift destruction of the disbelievers who opposed them and mocked the message: *fa-lā taʿjal ʿalayhim* — "So hasten not against them." The commentators understand this as a command not to hasten the disbelievers' demise, not to seek their punishment or destruction eagerly (R, Ṭ). When the believers saw the disbelievers' arrogance and hostility, and knew of the punishment that awaited them (verses 83 and 75), they might have wished for it to come quickly. The verse counsels patience: do not hasten against them. The destruction of the disbelievers is in God's hands and will come at God's appointed time; the believers need not — and must not — try to rush it.

The command not to hasten is rooted in a deeper wisdom that the verse goes on to explain. God may be postponing the destruction of the disbelievers for reasons that are not immediately apparent — to give them the opportunity to manifest their ultimate character and destiny (see 19:75), to allow the believers to be tested and strengthened, or to wait until the appointed hour. The believer who hastens against the wrongdoer is, in effect, trying to take the timing of divine justice into his own hands; the verse reminds him that the timing belongs to God. Patience in the face of apparent divine delay is thus a mark of trust: the believer trusts that God's timing is right, even when it is not the believer's own.

**"We Are Counting for Them a Number"**

The verse grounds the command to patience in the certainty of the divine accounting: *innamā naʿuddu lahum ʿaddan* — "verily We are counting for them a number." The commentators explain that God is taking full account of all the disbelievers' deeds, so that He may recompense them accordingly (R, Ṭ), or that their breaths, years, and terms are numbered (R, Ṭ) — finite and ever nearing their end. The "counting" implies that their time is not unlimited: every day they are allowed, every breath they draw, is counted and brings them closer to the appointed reckoning. Their seeming reprieve is not an escape; it is a numbered extension, and the counting is already underway.

**The Certainty Beneath the Patience**

The verse's teaching unites patience with certainty. The believer is told not to hasten against the disbelievers — and the reason is not that nothing is being done, but that the divine accounting is already in progress. God is counting; the number is fixed; the end is certain. The apparent delay in the punishment of the wrongdoers is not a sign that justice will not come but a sign that it is being measured and prepared. This is a profound consolation for the believer who suffers at the hands of the wrongdoers and longs for justice: the wrongdoers' days are numbered, their deeds are being counted, and their reckoning is certain even if delayed. The believer is freed from the anxiety of having to secure justice by his own haste; he can be patient, trusting that the God who is counting will bring the account to its due conclusion at the appointed time. The verse thus counsels the believer to leave the timing of justice to God, secure in the knowledge that nothing escapes the divine accounting and that the number is already being counted.

---
## Sūrah Maryam 19:85

> **On the Day We shall gather the reverent to the Compassionate as an honored delegation,**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Gathering of the Reverent**

The verse turns to the contrasting fates of the reverent and the guilty on the Last Day, beginning with the blessed: *yawma naḥshuru al-muttaqīna ilā al-Raḥmāni wafdan* — "On the Day We shall gather the reverent (al-muttaqīn) to the Compassionate as an honored delegation (wafd)." The *yawm* (Day) is the Last Day, the Day of Resurrection and Judgment. On that Day, the reverent — those who possessed *taqwā*, the God-consciousness that runs throughout the Qur'an and this sūrah — will be gathered to the Compassionate. The word *wafd* (delegation) is significant: it denotes an honored delegation or deputation, a group that is received with respect and dignity. The reverent are not herded or driven; they come as an honored delegation to the presence of the Compassionate, received with the honor befitting their station.

The commentators describe the manner of this gathering with vivid imagery. Some describe the reverent arriving as if riding on splendid camels rather than walking on foot (IK, R, Ṭ) — the honored manner of arrival befitting a noble delegation. The image conveys the dignity and ease of the reverent's journey to their Lord: they are not afraid, not harried, not humiliated, but gathered with honor to the presence of the One they worshipped and revered in this life. The verb "gather" (*naḥshuru*) is the same used of the disbelievers in verse 68, where they are gathered with the satans and brought around Hell on their knees; the contrast between the two gatherings is total — the reverent gathered with honor to the Compassionate, the guilty herded to Hell.

**The Reverent and Their Lord**

The verse's use of the divine Name *al-Raḥmān* ("the Compassionate") in connection with the gathering of the reverent is deliberate and resonant. Throughout the sūrah, the reverent have been described in relation to the Compassionate — they are the servants of the Compassionate who remember His signs and weep (verse 58), who are promised the Gardens of Eden by the Compassionate (verse 61), and who are saved by God at the approach of the Fire (verse 72). Now they are gathered *to the Compassionate* as an honored delegation. The gathering to the Compassionate is the culmination of the reverent's whole life of worship: the servants who turned to the Merciful in this life are gathered to His presence with honor in the next. The verse thus presents the Last Day not only as a day of judgment but as a day of reunion — the day on which the reverent are brought into the presence of the very One they loved and served, received not as trembling supplicants but as an honored delegation.

**The Honor That Awaitsthe Reverent**

The verse holds out to the believer the hope that defines the whole of the spiritual life: the hope of being gathered to God with honor. The reverent do not approach the Last Day with the terror of the guilty (verse 86) but with the dignity of an honored delegation; their lives of *taqwā*, of worship and self-restraint and remembrance, are rewarded by a reception befitting their devotion. The verse thus summons the believer to live as one who hopes to be among the *wafd* — the honored delegation gathered to the Compassionate. It is the ultimate dignity: not the honor of the world, which perishes, but the honor of being received into the presence of the Merciful on the Day of Gathering, as one who is welcomed, not judged as an enemy, but honored as a servant who kept faith with his Lord.

---
## Sūrah Maryam 19:86

> **and We shall drive the guilty into Hell as a thirsty herd.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Driving of the Guilty**

The verse completes the contrast by describing the fate of the guilty: *wa-nasūqu al-mujrimīna ilā jahannama wirdan* — "and We shall drive the guilty (al-mujrimūn) into Hell as a thirsty herd (wird)." The word *nasūqu* (We shall drive) describes the forceful herding of animals — the guilty are not gathered with honor like the reverent but driven, like cattle, toward their destination. The verb stands in pointed contrast to the gathering of the reverent in verse 85: the reverent are gathered with dignity as an honored delegation, while the guilty are driven with force toward Hell. The word *al-mujrimūn* (the guilty, the criminals, those who have committed *jurm*, crime and sin) designates those who have sinned gravely and persisted in their guilt — the wrongdoers who rejected the message and lived in disobedience.

The word *wird* is rich in meaning and the commentators unpack its significance. It denotes a thirsty herd being driven to water, or the state of being thirsty and seeking water. The guilty are driven into Hell *as a thirsty herd* — like animals driven to a watering place in their thirst (JJ), searching for water (Bḍ) they will never find. The image is one of degradation and futile longing: the guilty, who in this life were proud and insolent, are driven to Hell like thirsty beasts; and the "water" they seek — relief, mercy, an end to their torment — they will never find. Some commentators note the bitter irony of the image: the guilty are "herded" like the animals they resembled in their lives, driven to a place where their thirst for relief will never be quenched.

**The Total Reversal of Earthly State**

The verse completes the starkest contrast in the sūrah's eschatological teaching. Verse 85 described the reverent gathered to the Compassionate as an honored delegation; verse 86 describes the guilty driven into Hell as a thirsty herd. The two verses together present the two destinies of the Last Day in their full opposition: honor and humiliation, gathering and herding, nearness to the Compassionate and consignment to Hell. The contrast is designed to move the reader to reflection: which gathering will one be part of? The reverent, whose lives were marked by *taqwā* and worship, are honored; the guilty, who lived in sin and rejection, are herded to their punishment. The verses summon every soul to choose the path that leads to the honored delegation rather than the thirsty herd.

**The End of Pride and the Futility of the Search for Relief**

The verse carries a warning that strikes at the pride of the guilty. In this life, the *mujrimūn* — the guilty who boasted of their station and company (verse 73) — stood proud and self-important. On the Last Day, they are driven to Hell like thirsty cattle, stripped of every pretense of dignity. The image of the thirsty herd also conveys the agony of their state: like animals desperately seeking water and finding none, the guilty will seek relief from their torment and find none — their thirst for mercy, for escape, for an end to suffering, will remain eternally unquenched. The verse thus warns the proud and the guilty of the reversal that awaits them, and it summons the believer to live so as to be gathered with the reverent rather than driven with the guilty — to choose in this life the path of *taqwā* that leads to the honored delegation, rather than the path of sin that leads to the thirsty herd of Hell.

---
## Sūrah Maryam 19:87

> **They have no power of intercession, save the one who has made a pact with the Compassionate.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Limitation of Intercession**

The verse addresses the question of intercession (*shafāʿah*) on the Day of Judgment: *lā yamlikūna al-shafāʿata illā mani ittakhadha ʿinda al-Raḥmāni ʿahdan* — "They have no power of intercession, save the one who has made a pact (ʿahd) with the Compassionate." The pronoun "they" refers to the false gods and idols that the idolaters worshipped (verses 81–82), or more broadly to those whom the idolaters hoped would intercede for them. The verse declares that these have no power of intercession at all. The idols that the disbelievers hoped would intercede for them and be a strength for them (verse 81) are utterly incapable of interceding — they can neither help themselves nor anyone else. The hoped-for intercessors of the idolaters are stripped of all power.

The verse then states the sole exception: intercession is possible only for "the one who has made a pact with the Compassionate." The commentators explain that the pact (*ʿahd*) refers to the covenant of faith and obedience — those who believe in the One God and His messengers and follow His commands (R, Ṭ, Z), and who have thus entered into the covenant that God has made with the believers. The verse thus establishes that intercession is not a matter of influence, status, or the favor of false gods, but is governed by God's own ordering: it is only available to those who have made the pact of faith with the Compassionate, and it can only operate by God's permission.

**The Qur'anic Teaching on Intercession**

The verse is one of several in the Qur'an that define the limits of intercession in the Hereafter. Elsewhere the Qur'an states that none may intercede except those who have received God's permission (2:255; 20:109), or those with whose word God is content (20:109), or those who have borne witness to the truth knowingly (43:86). The Qur'an consistently balances two truths: intercession is real and beneficial, but it is entirely subject to God's will and is not an independent power wielded by any being. The idols of the idolaters have no power of intercession at all, because they are nothing; even the true intercessors — such as the angels and the prophets — can only intercede by God's permission and for those who have made the pact of faith.

Despite the strict limitation, the commentators note that the verse affirms a real hope: even those guilty of major sins may benefit from the intercession of those who have made a pact with the Compassionate (R). The intercession is not a license for sin but a mercy extended, by God's permission, to those within the covenant of faith. The verse thus corrects two errors at once. Against the idolaters, it denies that any false god or idol can intercede. Against despair, it affirms that intercession is available within the pact of faith — that the believer who sins is not beyond the hope of intercession, provided he remains within the covenant made with the Compassionate.

**The Pact That Opens the Door**

The verse points the believer to the true foundation of hope: the pact with the Compassionate. The intercession that the idolaters sought from their false gods is unavailable to them, because they made no pact with God; the intercession that gives hope is available to those who have entered the covenant of faith and obedience. The verse thus summons every soul to make and keep the pact with the Compassionate — to believe in the One God, follow His messengers, and obey His commands — for it is this pact alone that opens the door of intercession and mercy on the Day of Judgment. The idols that promised to intercede will be silent; the pact made with the Compassionate in this life is the only intercession that avails in the next.

---
## Sūrah Maryam 19:88

> **And they say, "The Compassionate has taken a child."**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Claim That the Sūrah Exists to Refute**

With verse 88 the sūrah returns to the theological assertion that frames its central polemic: *wa-qālū ittakhadha al-Raḥmānu waladan* — "And they say, 'The Compassionate has taken a child.'" The claim that God has taken a child is attributed here to unnamed claimants ("they say"). In the context of the Qur'an's revelation, the claim encompasses several groups: the pagan Arabs, who said that the angels were God's daughters; and, by extension, the Christians, who claimed that Jesus was the son of God (cf. 21:26; 6:100–102). The verse records the claim without naming its origin, because the refutation that follows applies to every form of the claim, whatever its source. The sūrah's use of the Name *al-Raḥmān* ("the Compassionate") in connection with the claim is pointed: the claim that the Compassionate has taken a child is made against the very Name that expresses God's boundless mercy and incomparability.

The entire sūrah has been building toward this moment of explicit refutation. Throughout the chapter, God has been presented as the Compassionate who grants sons to the barren, vindicates the pure, and promises the Garden to the repentant — the Lord who creates by His word "Be!" and it is. It is precisely this God, the Compassionate whose mercy and power the sūrah has been proclaiming, whom some now claim has taken a child. The claim is not merely a theological error; it is an offense against the very nature of the One whose compassion and creative power the sūrah has celebrated. The attribution of a child to God is, in the Qur'anic view, a fundamental failure to understand who God is.

**The Gravity of the Claim**

The verse's flat recording of the claim — "they say" — sets the stage for the sūrah's most intense theological passage. The claim is stated baldly, without elaboration, precisely so that it can be met with the full weight of the refutation that follows in verses 89–92. The commentators emphasize the gravity of the claim: to say that the Compassionate has taken a child is to attribute to God the very thing that is incompatible with His nature — need, dependence, generation, and partnership. The claim will be answered by the sūrah's insistence on divine transcendence: God is not diminished or multiplied by any relationship; He does not beget and is not begotten; everything in the heavens and the earth comes to Him as a servant. The verse thus records the false claim so that the true doctrine of divine incomparability can be stated with the greatest force, and so that every reader who hears of the claim will also hear its definitive refutation.

**The Sūrah's Climactic Refutation**

Verse 88 stands at the climactic point of the sūrah's theological argument. Having demonstrated God's creative power through the miraculous births of John and Jesus — and having shown, through the infant Jesus' own words, that he was a servant of God and not a divine son (verses 30–36) — the sūrah now confronts the claim of divine parenthood directly. The claim that the Compassionate has taken a child is refuted not only by argument but by the whole structure of the chapter: the very miracles that might seem to support the idea of a divine son (the virgin birth) are shown to be instances of God's ordinary creative power, by which He says "Be!" to whatever He wills, and not evidence of any divine offspring. The reader who has followed the sūrah through the accounts of the prophets is prepared, by verse 88, to receive the full force of the refutation that follows.

---
## Sūrah Maryam 19:89

> **You have indeed asserted a terrible thing.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Terribleness of the Assertion**

The sūrah's response to the claim that the Compassionate has taken a child opens with a word of the utmost severity: *laqad jiʾtum shayʾan iddan* — "You have indeed asserted a terrible (idd) thing." The word *idd* denotes something monstrous, terrible, and abominable — a thing so grave that it is almost beyond description. The commentators note that the assertion is called *idd* because of its enormity: to attribute a child to God is not a minor error but a monstrous offense against the divine nature. The verse addresses the claimants directly — "you have indeed asserted" — holding them responsible for the gravity of what they have said. The word *jiʾtum* ("you have brought/asserted") suggests that they have produced and introduced this claim into the world, and the sūrah tells them plainly what it is: a terrible thing.

The use of the word *idd* here connects to the earlier use of *fariyy* in verse 27, where Mary's people accused her of bringing "an amazing thing" (*shayʾan fariyyan*). The two words both denote something grave and unprecedented, but they are used to opposite effect. The accusation of *fariyy* against Mary was false — she had not committed the enormity her people imagined; she had been vindicated by the miracle of her son's speech. But the claim that the Compassionate has taken a child is a genuine *idd* — a real enormity, a true offense against the divine nature. The reader is invited to see the contrast: the false accusation against the pure servant was refuted, while the true enormity of attributing a child to God is here condemned.

**The Enormity of Misplacing God**

The verse's condemnation of the claim as a *terrible thing* strikes at the root of the theological error. The assertion that God has taken a child is terrible not merely because it is factually wrong but because it misplaces God — it drags the Creator of the heavens and the earth down to the level of creatures who need offspring, who reproduce, who are subject to the processes of generation. It imports into the divine nature the very neediness and dependency that define creatures. The sūrah will spend the following verses demonstrating the impossibility and unfittingness of this claim, but here it first establishes the moral and spiritual gravity of the assertion: those who make it are not engaging in harmless speculation but asserting a *terrible thing*, an enormity that offends against the majesty of God.

**The Warning Against Careless Speech About God**

The verse carries a warning that reaches far beyond the specific claim it condemns: the warning against careless speech about God. The assertion that God has taken a child is an extreme instance of a broader human failing — speaking about God without proper reverence, attributing to Him what is unworthy of Him, and treating the divine nature as a subject for casual or presumptuous speculation. The verse's condemnation of this claim as a *terrible thing* reminds the believer that speech about God carries the gravest weight: one does not speak of the Creator of the heavens and the earth as one speaks of ordinary beings. The proper posture toward God is reverence, and the proper speech about God is speech that acknowledges His majesty, His incomparability, and His transcendence of every creaturely limitation. The verse thus calls the believer to guard the tongue when it turns to the divine, and to reject, wherever it appears, every assertion that would diminish the majesty of the One who alone is worthy of worship.

---
## Sūrah Maryam 19:90

> **The heavens are well-nigh rent thereby, and the earth split asunder, and the mountains made to fall down in ruins,**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Universe Recoils at the Claim**

The verse expresses the enormity of the claim that God has taken a child in the most striking poetic image in the sūrah: *takādu al-samāwātu yatafaṭṭarna minhu wa-tanshaqqu al-arḍu wa-takhirru al-jibālu haddan* — "The heavens are well-nigh rent thereby, and the earth split asunder, and the mountains made to fall down in ruins." The claim that the Compassionate has taken a child is so terrible that the entire created order recoils from it: the heavens are almost torn apart, the earth almost split asunder, and the mountains almost fall down in ruins. The verse describes the reaction of creation to the enormity in cosmic terms — as if the universe itself, in its very fabric, cannot bear the assertion. The image conveys, with overwhelming force, the gravity of attributing a child to God.

The commentators explain the meaning of this cosmic reaction. The heavens, the earth, and the mountains are described as recoiling because all creatures, except certain human beings and jinn, are rooted in the awareness of God's Oneness and are terrified of *shirk* — the associating of partners with God (IK, Ṭ). The mountains nearly fall in ruins "in anger for God's sake" (Ṭ). The verse draws on the Qur'an's teaching that all of creation has an existential awareness of God and a relationship with Him: the heavens, the earth, and everything in them glorify Him, even if human beings do not understand their praise (17:44). Stones crash down for fear of God (2:74), and the whole of creation hymns His praise. The claim that God has a child is so offensive to this universal awareness of God's Oneness that the very fabric of creation trembles at it.

**The Anthropomorphic Image and Its Meaning**

The verse's image of the heavens being rent and the earth split asunder is metaphorical and must be understood as such. The commentators are careful to explain that the verse is not describing an actual, literal tearing of the heavens in response to the claim — for the claim is uttered by human beings and the physical universe does not literally respond with emotion. Rather, the image is a vivid and powerful way of conveying the enormity of the offense: the claim that God has taken a child is so grave, so contrary to the truth of God's Oneness that pervades all creation, that it is as though the very heavens would split apart at the utterance. The image is designed to make the reader feel the weight of the claim — to understand that attributing a child to God is not a small error but an offense that sets the whole cosmos, in its true awareness of God, shuddering.

**The Awareness of Creation and the Insensibility of Some Humans**

The verse also points to a striking contrast. The heavens, the earth, and the mountains — the inanimate creation — are described as almost rent, split, and toppled by the claim against God's Oneness, because they are suffused with the awareness of God. Yet some human beings — those who make the claim — remain unmoved, uttering the enormity without the recoil that the rest of creation experiences. The verse thus exposes the peculiar condition of those who assert that God has taken a child: they have become insensible to the truth of God's Oneness that the whole of creation proclaims, so deaf to the universal hymn of praise that they can utter the most terrible claim without trembling. The verse summons the reader to recover the awareness that all creation possesses — the deep recognition of God's incomparability — and to recoil, as the heavens themselves recoil, from every claim that would diminish the majesty of the One God.

---
## Sūrah Maryam 19:91

> **that they should claim for the Compassionate a child.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Ground of the Cosmic Recoil**

The verse completes the sentence begun in verse 90, giving the reason for the cosmic reaction: *an daʿaw lil-Raḥmāni waladan* — "that they should claim for the Compassionate a child." The heavens are well-nigh rent, the earth split asunder, and the mountains brought down in ruins *because* they claimed for the Compassionate a child. The whole of the preceding image — the tearing heavens, the splitting earth, the toppling mountains — is anchored to this single cause: the claim that the Compassionate has a child. The verse makes explicit what verse 90 implied: the enormity that unsettles the cosmos is precisely the attribution of a child to the Compassionate. The created order, in its universal awareness of God's Oneness, cannot bear the claim; it is as though the very fabric of reality protests against it.

The verse's repetition of the divine Name *al-Raḥmān* ("the Compassionate") in connection with the claim is deliberate and central. The claim that is so terrible that the heavens are nearly rent is not merely that "God" has a child in some abstract sense, but specifically that *the Compassionate* has a child — that the very Name which expresses God's boundless and incomparable mercy has been degraded by the attribution of an offspring. The juxtaposition is the sūrah's way of intensifying the enormity: it is the Merciful, the Compassionate, whose incomparable majesty is assaulted by the claim that He has taken a child. The Name that the whole sūrah has used to express God's mercy and power is here used to express the depth of the offense against His transcendence.

**The Impossibility Grounded in the Divine Nature**

The verse's emphasis on the claim's being made "for the Compassionate" grounds the refutation in the very nature of God. The Compassionate is the One whose mercy encompasses all things, whose creative power brings being from nothing by His word, whose Oneness admits no partner and no offspring. To claim that the Compassionate has taken a child is to contradict everything that the Name expresses: the One who is utterly self-sufficient, who needs nothing, who creates by fiat, cannot be said to have begotten a son. The sūrah has established this throughout — through the accounts of the miraculous births that were accomplished by God's creative power without any need for divine offspring, and through the infant Jesus' own declaration of servanthood. The claim that the Compassionate has a child is thus not merely false but incoherent with the divine nature, and it is this incoherence that the cosmic recoil of verses 90–91 dramatizes.

**The Transcendence That No Claim Can Diminish**

The verse, with verse 90, delivers the sūrah's most forceful statement of divine transcendence. The claim that the Compassionate has taken a child is met not with argument alone but with the image of the entire creation recoiling from the enormity — the heavens nearly rent, the earth split, the mountains toppled. The image conveys that the truth of God's Oneness is woven into the very fabric of existence, that all creation bears witness to it, and that no claim against it, however confidently uttered, can alter the reality of God's incomparability.
