The Compassionate remains what He is — the One, the Self-Sufficient, without partner and without offspring — and the claim that He has taken a child, however terrible, cannot diminish His majesty by a hair's breadth. The verse thus both condemns the claim and affirms the unassailable transcendence of the God against whom it is made.

---
## Sūrah Maryam 19:92

> **It is not fitting for the Compassionate to take a child.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Definitive Rejection**

The verse states the sūrah's central theological conclusion with lapidary finality: *wa-mā yanbaghī lil-Raḥmāni an yattakhidha waladan* — "And it is not fitting (yanbaghī) for the Compassionate to take a child." After the dramatic cosmic image of verses 90–91, the verse delivers the plain, definitive statement: taking a child is not fitting for the Compassionate. The word *yanbaghī* conveys what is proper, fitting, and becoming; its negation — *mā yanbaghī* — states that the taking of a child is not proper, not fitting, not consistent with the nature of the Compassionate. The verse thus moves from the vivid image of the creation's recoil to the sober statement of the principle: the attribution of a child to God is not merely shocking; it is *unfitting* — it does not belong to the divine nature at all.

The commentators explain the force of "not fitting" in the strongest terms. Some say it means that such a thing is impossible and inconceivable (R) — not merely improper but impossible, because it contradicts the very nature of God. To say that God does not take a child "because it is not fitting for Him" is to say that the divine nature is such that offspring are inconceivable for God: God is One, Self-Sufficient, without need, without partner, and without peer. The verse connects to the Qur'an's statement in Sūrah 112, which the tradition regards as the essence of divine unity: "He begets not, nor was He begotten" (112:3), and to the rejection of divine parenthood in 6:100–102 and elsewhere. The taking of a child is not fitting for God because everything that fatherhood implies — need, process, dependence, reproduction — is alien to the divine nature.

**The God Who Does Not Beget**

The verse's principle rests on the Qur'an's consistent doctrine of divine transcendence and self-sufficiency. God is the One (*al-Aḥad*), the Self-Sufficient (*al-Ṣamad*) to Whom all turn and Who turns to none; He begets not and is not begotten (112:1–4). The idea of God taking a child imports into the divine nature the neediness and processuality that are the marks of creatures. A being who begets a child does so, in every case, out of some need or through some process — and God is above all need and all process. He does not reproduce because He does not diminish or die; He does not need offspring because He is complete; He does not generate because He creates — and His creation is accomplished by the word "Be!" and not by generation. The verse's statement that it is "not fitting" for the Compassionate to take a child is thus the positive expression of the same truth that the whole sūrah has been demonstrating: God's creative power renders divine parenthood both unnecessary and impossible.

**The Conclusion of the Sūrah's Long Argument**

The verse stands as the conclusion of the sūrah's long theological argument, which has unfolded over its entire course. From the accounts of the miraculous births of John and Jesus, which demonstrated God's power to create without ordinary means; through the infant Jesus' own declaration that he was a servant of God and not a divine son (verses 30–36); through the rejection of the idolaters' false gods who could not intercede (verses 81–87); and through the dramatic image of creation recoiling from the claim (verses 89–91) — the sūrah has been building toward this plain statement: it is not fitting for the Compassionate to take a child. The statement is the doctrinal keystone of the chapter, the point at which all its demonstrations of divine power and mercy converge on the affirmation of divine transcendence. The God who gives sons to the barren and the virgin is the God who Himself takes no son; His giving of children to His servants is the very proof that He needs no child Himself.

**The Consequence for Faith**

For the believer, the verse establishes the non-negotiable core of the understanding of God: the Compassionate does not and cannot take a child. Every claim that God has an offspring — whether the pagan claim that the angels are God's daughters, or the Christian claim that Jesus is the son of God — is rejected as unfitting to the divine nature. The believer's worship is therefore directed to the One God alone, who is above all partnership and offspring, whose Oneness is the first and final truth. And the believer's confidence rests on this same truth: the God who is One, Self-Sufficient, without need and without peer, is the God whose power is absolute and whose mercy is boundless — the Compassionate who needs no child because He lacks nothing, and who gives to His servants because His generosity is complete.

---
## Sūrah Maryam 19:93

> **There is none in the heavens and on the earth, but that it comes unto the Compassionate as a servant.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**All Creation as Servants of the Compassionate**

The verse grounds the rejection of divine parenthood in the universal servanthood of creation: *in kullu man fī al-samāwāti wa-al-arḍi illā ātī al-Raḥmāni ʿabdan* — "There is none in the heavens and on the earth, but that it comes unto the Compassionate as a servant (ʿabd)." The verse declares that every being in the heavens (the angels) and on the earth (human beings and jinn, and by extension all of creation) comes unto the Compassionate as a servant. The word *ʿabd* (servant) is the fundamental designation of the creature in relation to God. To say that everything in the heavens and the earth comes to the Compassionate as a servant is another way of saying that God is the Lord (*rabb*) of all things, and that all things are therefore servants in relation to Him (see the commentary on verse 2).

The verse's point is devastating to the claim that God has taken a child. If every being in the heavens and the earth — including the angels, whom the pagans called God's daughters, and including Jesus, whom the Christians called God's son — comes unto the Compassionate *as a servant*, then none of them can be God's child in the sense of a divine offspring. Servanthood and sonship (in the divine sense) are mutually exclusive: a being is either a servant of God or a child of God, and the verse affirms that every being in the heavens and the earth is a servant. The Qur'an elsewhere makes this explicit with regard to Jesus, whom it calls "a servant of God" (19:30; 4:172) and a "servant of God" is precisely what the angels are not ashamed to be (4:172). The claim of divine sonship is refuted by the universal servanthood of all creation.

**The Universality of Servanthood**

The verse affirms the universality and totality of divine lordship. There is no being in the heavens or on the earth that is exempt from servanthood to God — no angel so exalted, no prophet so honored, no human being so powerful, no creature so humble, that it is not a servant of the Compassionate. The highest creatures — the angels who surround the Throne, the prophets who received revelation, the very beings that some have elevated to divinity — are all servants. The Qur'an states this theme throughout: "I did not create jinn and mankind, save to worship Me" (51:56), and the angels and all creation glorify God and worship Him (17:44). The verse thus establishes a cosmic egalitarianism under God: whatever their rank, all creatures share the fundamental status of servanthood to the Compassionate, and none can claim the rank of divine offspring.

**The Dignity of Servanthood**

The verse, in affirming that all creation comes to the Compassionate as servants, also elevates the concept of servanthood itself. In the previous sūrah's accounts, the highest human figures — Zachariah, John, Jesus, Abraham, Moses — were all described as servants of God; Jesus' first words in this sūrah were "I am a servant of God" (verse 30). The verse now generalizes this: all of creation, from the angels of the highest heaven to the humblest creature on earth, is a servant of the Compassionate. Servanthood is thus not a degradation but the universal and dignified relationship of every creature to its Creator. The believer who understands this does not seek to escape servanthood or to claim any higher status, but embraces it as the very meaning of existence — coming to the Compassionate as a servant, as all creation does, in worship and submission. And the one who would claim that any being is more than a servant — that God has taken a child — has misunderstood the fundamental order of creation, in which every being in the heavens and the earth is the servant of the Compassionate, and nothing more.

---
## Sūrah Maryam 19:94

> **He has taken account of them, and numbered them exactly.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Divine Knowledge of Every Servant**

The verse affirms the perfection of God's knowledge of all His servants: *laqad aḥṣāhum wa-ʿaddahum ʿaddan* — "He has taken account of them, and numbered them exactly." The two verbs convey the totality and precision of divine knowledge. *Aḥṣā* means to count, enumerate, and take full account of; *ʿadda* means to number. God has taken account of every being in the heavens and the earth and numbered them exactly — He knows the number of all His creatures (Ṭ) and, by implication, their nature, their destiny, and their essence. The verse follows the affirmation of verse 93 (that all creation comes to God as servants) with the assurance that God knows every one of His servants completely — none is forgotten, none is lost, none escapes the divine accounting.

The commentators note the theological significance of this verse, particularly with regard to God's knowledge of particulars. Some Islamic philosophers, such as Ibn Sīnā (Avicenna), argued that God's knowledge, while all-encompassing, was general rather than particular — that God knew particulars only in a universal way. This view was rejected by the theologians (*mutakallimūn*), who insisted that God has knowledge of all particulars as particulars. The verse is cited as evidence for the theological position: God has "taken account of them and numbered them exactly" — a numbering of each individual servant implies knowledge of each individual, not merely of the class (R). The verse thus affirms that the divine knowledge extends to every single creature, in its particularity, and that nothing in creation is unknown to its Creator.

**The Divine Knowledge That Comprehends All**

The verse's emphasis on God's counting and numbering connects to the broader Qur'anic teaching on the comprehensiveness of divine knowledge. The Qur'an states that God knows what is on land and sea; that no leaf falls but that He knows it; that no seed in the dark recesses of the earth, nor anything moist or dry, escapes His knowledge, but that all is inscribed in a clear Book (6:59). The counting of the servants is part of this universal knowledge: God knows each of His servants, their number, their deeds, their times, and their ends. The verse also carries an eschatological resonance: because God has taken account of all His servants and numbered them exactly, the reckoning of the Day of Judgment will be complete and precise — every servant will be accounted for, and nothing will be omitted from the divine reckoning.

**The Comfort and the Warning of Being Counted**

The verse holds together comfort and accountability. It is a comfort to the believer to know that God has taken account of every servant and numbered each exactly: no sincere act of worship is overlooked, no genuine devotion is forgotten, no good deed, however small, escapes the divine accounting. The believer who feels unnoticed or forgotten by the world can rest in the knowledge that the Creator has counted him — that he is known, accounted for, and remembered by God. But the verse is also a warning: because God has numbered all His servants exactly, every soul will be held to account, and none can slip through the reckoning unnoticed. The counting that assures the righteous of their reward is the same counting that assures the guilty of their punishment. The verse thus summons the believer to live in the awareness that he is numbered — that the Creator who has taken account of all His servants knows him completely and will hold him to account with perfect precision.

---
## Sūrah Maryam 19:95

> **And each of them shall come unto Him on the Day of Resurrection, alone.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**Every Servant Coming Alone**

The verse completes the trilogy on divine knowledge and the servanthood of creation with the universal destiny: *wa-kulluhum ātīhi yawma al-Qiyāmati fardan* — "And each of them shall come unto Him on the Day of Resurrection, alone (fardan)." Just as every being in the heavens and the earth is a servant of the Compassionate (verse 93), and just as God has taken account of each and numbered them exactly (verse 94), so each of them will come unto Him on the Day of Resurrection *alone*. The word *fardan* (alone, solitary) describes the condition of every soul at the Resurrection: stripped of all companions, all possessions, all helpers, and all the supports of this world, each being comes unto God by itself. The verse echoes verse 80, where the presumptuous disbeliever was told that "he will come unto Us alone," and generalizes it to every being in the heavens and the earth (Ṭ).

The commentators explain the meaning of coming "alone." Each being — whether angel, human, or jinn — will be resurrected and brought before God without anyone to aid, defend, or intercede on its behalf except by God's permission, and without any of the worldly companions, family, wealth, or status that accompanied it through life (Ṭ). The verse thus presents the ultimate solitude of the Resurrection: the moment when every soul discovers that it stands before God alone, with nothing but what it truly is. The angels who worshipped God, the prophets who served Him, the believers who did righteousness, and the guilty who did evil — each comes alone, stripped of every support except the relationship with God that it cultivated or neglected in this life.

**The Universal Solitude Before God**

The verse's teaching on the aloneness of every soul before God is one of the most profound and sobering in the Qur'an's eschatology. In this life, human beings are surrounded by others — by family, friends, communities, institutions — and they draw their sense of identity and security from these relationships. The verse declares that at the Resurrection, all of these supports fall away, and each soul stands alone before its Creator. The solitude is universal — no one is exempt, no one is accompanied, no one can bring anyone else. The implications are searching: the relationships and possessions that seem so solid in this life are ultimately supports that will fall away, and what alone will remain is the soul's own relationship with God. The believer is thus summoned to cultivate, in this life, the relationship that will survive the solitude — the relationship with God that alone can accompany the soul when it comes before Him alone.

**The Accounting That Awaits the Alone**

The verse also points to the accountability that awaits each solitary soul. Coming before God alone, stripped of all company and all intercessors (save by God's permission, as verse 87 established), each being will give account of its own life. The verses that follow in the sūrah — the promise of love for those who believe and work righteousness (verse 96), the assurance of the Qur'an's ease upon the tongue (verse 97), and the closing warning over the destroyed generations (verse 98) — all point to this accounting. The one who comes alone before God brings nothing but his deeds, his faith, and his relationship with his Lord. The verse thus summons the believer to live with the end in view — to build the faith and righteousness that will stand when all else falls away, so that when he comes unto the Compassionate on the Day of Resurrection alone, he comes as a servant who kept faith with his Lord, and not as one who is stripped of everything that could have saved him.

---
## Sūrah Maryam 19:96

> **Surely those who believe and perform righteous deeds, for them shall the Compassionate ordain love.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**Love Ordained by the Compassionate**

The verse turns from the solitude of the Resurrection to a promise of love: *inna alladhīna āmanū wa-ʿamilū al-ṣāliḥāti sa-yajʿalu lahumu al-Raḥmānu wuddan* — "Surely those who believe and perform righteous deeds, for them the Compassionate shall ordain love (wudd)." The word *wudd* denotes love, affection, and deep friendship. The verse promises that God will "ordain" (*yajʿalu*) love for those who believe and work righteousness — that He will cause them to be loved. The commentators explain that this means God grants that such people will be loved in the world, particularly by God's other believing servants (Ṭ) — that their faith and righteousness will draw to them the love of others, and that this love is a manifestation of God's love for them.

The choice of the word *wudd* rather than the more common *ḥubb* (love) is worth noting, though the commentators generally understand the two as synonymous (Ṭ). *Wudd* carries a connotation of warm, deep-seated affection and friendship. The promise that God will ordain *wudd* for the believers suggests a love that is not fleeting or superficial but deep and enduring — a love placed in the hearts of others that draws them to the believer and binds the believing community together. The verse thus ties the individual's faith and righteousness to a social reality: the righteous believer is loved, and that love is itself a gift of God, woven into the fabric of the believing community.

**The Love of God Manifested in the Hearts of Others**

The commentators elaborate the operation of this ordained love. Al-Sulamī states that those who believe and work righteousness are beloved by their fellow Muslims, which is none other than a manifestation of God's love for them. Al-Rāzī favors this interpretation, though he notes that some interpret the verse to mean that God will grant the righteous believers what they love rather than the state of being loved by others — an interpretation he considers weaker, since it is well known that believers are often despised rather than loved by the disbelievers in this world. The preferred interpretation, that God ordains love for the believers in the hearts of others, is supported by a hadith that says that when God loves a servant, He calls out to the Archangel Gabriel, "Verily I love so-and-so, so love him!" Gabriel then calls upon all those in Heaven to love this person, and the inclination to love this person is made to descend upon those on earth (see also IK, Q). The love that the believer receives from others is thus traced back to its ultimate source: the love of God, which sets in motion the love of the angels and the love of the hearts of the righteous on earth.

**The Love That Comes from Righteousness**

The verse connects love to its proper cause: faith and righteous deeds. The promise is not that love will be ordained for the merely popular, the wealthy, or the powerful, but for "those who believe and perform righteous deeds." The love that God ordains is the fruit of genuine faith and genuine goodness — the believer whose life is marked by trust in God and by righteous action is the one whose heart God fills with the love of others. This is a profound inversion of the world's economy of love: in the world, love is often sought through wealth, status, and self-display; the verse teaches that it is ordained through faith and righteousness, which draw the love of the righteous regardless of worldly standing. Some report that the verse was revealed as a consolation to ʿAbd al-Raḥmān ibn ʿAwf, who was concerned about leaving his companions in Makkah (Ṭ) — the assurance that his faith and righteousness would win him love wherever he went.

For the Shiite commentators, the love that God ordains for the righteous believers is the love of and spiritual attachment to ʿAlī ibn Abī Ṭālib (Qm, Ṭb, Ṭs), since according to the Shiite tradition there are no true Muslim believers save those in whose hearts is the love of ʿAlī; a Shiite report states that the Prophet said to ʿAlī, "Say, 'O God, ordain for me a pact with Thee, and ordain for me love in the hearts of the believers,'" after which this verse was revealed (Ṭs). For some Sufis, the love that God ordains here is none other than the unveiling of divine secrets, connected to the well-known hadith qudsī: "Verily My servant does not cease to draw nigh unto Me through supererogatory acts until I love him, and when I love him, I am the hearing with which he hears, the seeing with which he sees, the hand with which he grasps, and the foot with which he walks." Al-Qushayrī cites a similar hadith: "My servant does not cease to draw nigh unto Me until he loves Me and I love him" (Qu). In this deepest reading, the love that God ordains is not merely the affection of others but the very nearness of the Beloved — the love of God Himself, which is the goal of the spiritual path and the reward of those who believe and work righteousness.

**The Heart of the Sūrah's Message**

The verse gathers the threads of the whole sūrah into a single promise. Throughout the chapter, the sūrah has spoken of the Compassionate (al-Raḥmān) who grants sons to the barren, vindicates the pure, and promises the Garden to the repentant. Now it declares that for those who believe and perform righteous deeds, the Compassionate will ordain love. The mercy with which the sūrah opened, the servanthood it has celebrated in the prophets, the faith and righteousness it has summoned the believers to practice — all converge on this promise of love. The believer who believes and does good is not merely saved or rewarded; he is loved. And that love — ordained by the Compassionate, manifested in the hearts of the righteous, and ultimately the love of God Himself — is the deepest gift that the sūrah holds out to those who believe and work righteousness. It is the fitting promise from the chapter of the Compassionate, who ordains love for those who love Him and serve Him.

---
## Sūrah Maryam 19:97

> **We have only made this easy upon thy tongue that thou mayest give glad tidings unto the reverent thereby, and that thereby thou mayest warn a contentious people.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**Made Easy upon the Tongue**

The verse explains why the Qur'an was revealed in the manner it was: *fa-innamā yassarnāhu bi-lisānika* — "For We have only made this easy upon thy tongue." The "this" refers to the Qur'an, and the verse states that God made it easy "upon thy tongue" — that is, on the tongue of the Prophet Muhammad ﷺ (cf. 44:58). The commentators explain that God made the Qur'an easy by revealing it in Arabic (IK, R, Z), the native language of the Prophet and his people. The revelation came in a language the Prophet could recite with ease and his community could understand — not in a foreign tongue that would have been alien to them, but in the very language of their daily speech, so that the recitation flowed readily from the Prophet's tongue and entered readily into the ears and hearts of his hearers. The verse may also be read, as al-Zamakhsharī notes, to mean "We made this easy in thy tongue," referring to the revelation of the Qur'an in Arabic (Z).

The ease of the Qur'an upon the tongue connects to a wider theme in the Qur'an, where it is said to have been "made easy to remember" (54:17, 22, 32, 40). The Qur'an was not sent as an inaccessible or obscure text but as a scripture that could be recited, memorized, and preserved with relative ease — a book "easy" upon the tongue of the messenger and, by extension, upon the tongues of those who recite it. This ease is itself a mercy and a sign: the Book that guides humanity was made accessible to them, spoken in their own language, rhythmically structured for memorization, and entrusted to a messenger who could recite it with fluency. The accessibility of the Qur'an is not an accident but a deliberate divine accommodation to the capacities of its audience.

**"That Thou Mayest Give Glad Tidings unto the Reverent"**

The verse then states the purpose of this ease: *li-tubashshira bihi al-muttaqīna* — "that thou mayest give glad tidings (tubashshira) thereby unto the reverent (al-muttaqīn)." The Qur'an, made easy upon the Prophet's tongue, was to be the means of giving glad tidings to the reverent. The verb *tubashshira* (to give glad tidings) is the same root as the glad tidings (*bushrā*) that were announced to Zachariah and Mary in the accounts of John and Jesus. The Qur'an is a bearer of glad tidings — of the mercy of God, the promise of the Garden, the forgiveness of sins, and the nearness of the Lord — and these glad tidings are addressed especially to the reverent (*al-muttaqīn*), those who possess the *taqwā* that the sūrah has celebrated throughout. The Book that was made easy upon the tongue is a Book of good news for those who are reverent — a Book that assures them of the reward that their reverence has earned.

**"And That Thereby Thou Mayest Warn a Contentious People"**

The verse completes the double purpose of the Qur'an's revelation: *wa-tundhira bihi qawman luddan* — "and that thereby thou mayest warn a contentious (ludd) people." The word *ludd* (plural of *aladd*) denotes those who are intensely but vainly argumentative — people who argue on the basis of falsehood (Ṭ). The term also connotes an attitude of insolence and a certain "deafness" to hearing the truth (Ṭ). The contentious people are the opponents of the Prophet, who argued against the message, disputed the signs, and refused to hear the truth even when it was made easy upon the tongue and recited clearly to them. Against such people, the Qur'an serves a different function: not glad tidings but warning. The same Book that gives glad tidings to the reverent warns the contentious — proclaiming the consequences of rejection and calling them to account.

**The Two Audiences of the One Book**

The verse presents the Qur'an as a Book with two faces turned toward two audiences. To the reverent, it is glad tidings — the assurance of mercy, reward, and the nearness of God. To the contentious, it is a warning — the proclamation of judgment and the call to account. The one Book serves both purposes, and it was made easy upon the tongue precisely so that both functions could be fulfilled: so that the glad tidings could reach the reverent clearly, and so that the warning could reach the contentious unambiguously. The ease of the Qur'an upon the tongue is thus not an end in itself but the means to this double end. The believer who recites the Qur'an is invited to receive it as glad tidings — to hear in its verses the mercy and promise of God for the reverent — and also to heed it as a warning, lest he be numbered among the contentious who argue against the truth and are deaf to its call. The verse thus holds up the two responses to the Book and summons the reader to the first: to be among the reverent who receive its glad tidings, and not among the contentious who meet it with argument and heedlessness.

---
## Sūrah Maryam 19:98

> **How many a generation before them have We destroyed? Dost thou perceive even one of them, or hear from them a murmur?**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Closing Question Over the Destroyed Generations**

The sūrah closes with the rhetorical question that has sounded throughout its later verses: *wa-kam ahlaknā qablahum min qarnin* — "And how many a generation before them have We destroyed?" The question repeats the exclamation of verse 74 ("How many a generation We destroyed before them...") and gathers the sūrah's many warnings into a single closing reflection. The "them" refers to the current audience — the Quraysh who opposed the Prophet and denied the resurrection. The verse asks how many generations before them God has destroyed, and the question implies an answer of overwhelming magnitude: countless generations, each of which rejected its messengers and was destroyed for its wrongdoing. The history of humanity is, in part, a record of such destructions — a record that the sūrah holds up to its hearers as both warning and evidence.

The question then turns to the present condition of those destroyed generations: *hal tuḥissu minhum min aḥadin aw tasmaʿu lahum rikzan* — "Dost thou perceive even one of them, or hear from them a murmur?" The verse asks the Prophet — and through him, his audience — whether anyone can perceive any of the destroyed generations still existing, or hear from them even the slightest sound. The word *rikz* denotes the faintest sound or murmur (R, Ṭ) — a whisper, a tremor of movement. The answer is that nothing remains: no trace of the destroyed generations is perceptible, and no sound is heard from them. The peoples who once populated the earth — with their cities, their wealth, their power, and their pride — have vanished utterly, leaving not even a murmur behind.

**The Silence of the Destroyed**

The verse's closing image is one of profound and sobering silence. The generations that God destroyed — the peoples who rejected their messengers and trusted in their worldly power — are gone, and from them one hears not even a murmur. The verse is similar in tone to the many verses that bid the Arabs to observe the ruins of earlier peoples, whose empty dwellings were well known to them from their travels through the desert (cf. 3:137; 6:11; 12:109; 27:51–52). The Arabs of the Prophet's time passed the ruined habitations of the destroyed peoples — the empty wells of Thamud, the crumbled towers of vanished cities — and the verse points to these ruins as evidence: here were peoples fairer in furnishings and appearance than you, and they are gone, leaving no trace and no sound. The silence of the destroyed is the loudest warning of all.

**The Urgency of the Present**

The verse brings the sūrah to a close on a note of urgency that gathers all its teaching. The sūrah opened with the mystery of the letters and the mercy of God shown to His servants — Zachariah, John, Mary, Jesus, Abraham, Moses, Ishmael, and Idrīs — and it has traced the pattern of divine mercy and divine judgment through their accounts. Now it closes by pointing to the fate of those who rejected the message: generation after generation destroyed, leaving not a murmur. The closing question is an invitation to the present hearers — and to every generation — to learn from the silence of the past. The peoples who were destroyed were not more wicked than any others by nature; they were peoples who had the truth presented to them and rejected it, and they were destroyed. The hearers of the Qur'an stand in the same position: the truth is before them, the signs are recited, the warning is given — and the choice is theirs whether to be among the reverent who receive glad tidings or among the contentious who meet the Book with argument until it is too late. The sūrah that opened by reminding us of God's Mercy closes by reminding us of the silence that awaits those who spurn it — and the silence is the final summons to hear, before one becomes, like the destroyed generations, a people from whom no murmur is heard.

**The End of the Sūrah and Its Abiding Call**

Sūrah Maryam thus ends as it began — with the eternal realities of divine mercy and divine power held before the reader. The chapter that bears the name of the purest of women has traced the arc of God's dealings with His servants: the Mercy that grants sons to the barren and the virgin, the Power that creates by a word and raises the dead, the Compassion that promises the Garden to the repentant, and the Justice that warns of the silence awaiting the guilty. It has defended the Oneness of God against every claim of divine sonship, affirmed the universal servanthood of creation, and summoned every soul to the faith and righteousness that draw the ordained love of the Compassionate. And it closes with the question that gives all its teaching its urgency: how many generations have been destroyed, and from them no murmur remains? The question is addressed to every reader of the sūrah — the choice of which company to join, the reverent or the destroyed, is set before each soul; and the sūrah that has so fully proclaimed the Mercy of the Compassionate leaves that choice, as God always leaves it, in the hands of the servant who has been warned and gladdened by the Book made easy upon the tongue.

---
**[End of the commentary on Sūrah Maryam]**
